  - target_column_desc: Column profile metadata (completeness, dataType, distinct counts, histogram, etc.).
  - code_snippet: Python code using this column downstream. It often includes pandas/numpy transformations that encode domain rules.
  - downstream_task_description: Description of the downstream task (may be absent).
  - assumptions: A numbered list of assumptions about this column (0-based indexing).
- Output:
  - constraint_code: List[dict] where each dict is:
    {
      "suggestion": "<PyDeequ call string starting with '.' or a function>",
      "level": "warning" | "error",
      "linked assumptions": [<assumption indices>]
    }

General rules for suggestions
- Use only the provided PyDeequ API surface (row-level and aggregate-level functions listed below).
- The suggestion string must be a single PyDeequ constraint call, not a full Check definition and not pseudocode:
  - Correct: ".hasCompleteness('col', lambda x: x == 1.0)"
  - Incorrect: "check.hasCompleteness(...)" or "Check(...).hasCompleteness(...)"
- When referencing multiple columns or writing SQL-like conditions, quote column names with backticks in .satisfies (e.g., '`A` <= `B`').
- For regex in .hasPattern, always use raw strings, e.g., r"^\d+$".
- Prefer explicit thresholded assertions when you know the expected value or bound (e.g., completeness == 1.0, bounds like 0..1, 0..20, 0..200).
- Severity:
  - Use "error" for hard business/structural invariants (types, completeness, bounds, binary domains, cross-column inequalities).
  - Use "warning" for soft/distributional checks (distinctness, quantiles) that aid monitoring but are not strict invariants.
- Always populate "linked assumptions" with the indices of assumptions that justify the constraint. If multiple assumptions support the same constraint, include them all.

How to infer constraints (combine metadata + code usage + domain knowledge)
1) Start from profile metadata (target_column_desc)
   - If completeness is 1.0, assert exact completeness:
     - Prefer: .hasCompleteness('<col>', lambda x: x == 1.0)
   - If dataType is provided, assert it with a strict threshold:
     - .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.<Type>, lambda x: x >= 0.95)
       where <Type> ∈ {Integral, Fractional, Boolean, String, Numeric}.
   - If approximateNumDistinctValues == 2 and the code treats the column as 0/1 (see code inference below), add binary domain:
     - .isContainedIn('<col>', [0, 1])

2) Infer semantics from how the column is used in code_snippet
   Parse for:
   - Numeric coercions:
     - If converted via pd.to_numeric(...), treat as numeric (.hasDataType(..., Numeric or more specific as per assumptions)).
     - If later cast to integer (astype(int) or Int64), prefer Integral.
     - If used in ratios or comparisons, add non-negativity and range bounds where obvious.
   - Domain-specific scales:
     - "Curricular units ... (grade)" is divided by 20.0 → grade in [0, 20]:
       - .hasMin('<col>', lambda x: x >= 0)
       - .hasMax('<col>', lambda x: x <= 20)
     - "Admission grade" or "Previous qualification (grade)" appears divided by 200.0 → grade in [0, 200]:
       - .hasMin('<col>', lambda x: x >= 0)
       - .hasMax('<col>', lambda x: x <= 200)
   - Binary indicators:
     - Columns used as 0/1 flags in arithmetic or comparisons (e.g., Tuition fees up to date, Debtor, boolean-like attendance flags) → enforce binary domain:
       - .isContainedIn('<col>', [0, 1])
       - Also assert Integral type when coerced to int.
   - Semester academic columns:
     - Patterns: 'Curricular units 1st/2nd sem (enrolled|approved|credited|without evaluations)' are non-negative integers.
       - .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral, lambda x: x >= 0.95)
       - .isNonNegative('<col>')
     - Cross-column inequalities (apply when the target is one of these):
       - approved <= enrolled
         - .isLessThanOrEqualTo('Curricular units X sem (approved)', 'Curricular units X sem (enrolled)')
       - credited <= enrolled
         - .isLessThanOrEqualTo('Curricular units X sem (credited)', 'Curricular units X sem (enrolled)')
       - without evaluations <= enrolled
         - .isLessThanOrEqualTo('Curricular units X sem (without evaluations)', 'Curricular units X sem (enrolled)')
   - Application order:
     - If the code indexes with a 1..9 assumption (weights array where index 0 is unused), enforce the discrete domain:
       - .isContainedIn('Application order', [1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8,9])
       - .hasDataType('Application order', ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral, lambda x: x >= 0.95)
   - Risk scores or normalized ratios:
     - Any score built by clipping/np.clip(..., 0.0, 1.0) or division resulting in fractions should be in [0, 1]:
       - .hasMin('<col>', lambda x: x >= 0.0)
       - .hasMax('<col>', lambda x: x <= 1.0)

3) Align with the provided assumptions
   - Use assumptions to justify constraints you select (e.g., completeness, data type, non-negativity, binary domain, bounds, and cross-column relations).
   - If an assumption explicitly states integer, non-negative, completeness, subset relation, or bounded scale, include the corresponding constraint(s) and link to that assumption index.
   - If multiple assumptions point to the same invariant (e.g., “integer” and “coerced to numeric”), include both indices in "linked assumptions".

4) Choosing specific functions and thresholds
   - Prefer .hasCompleteness('<col>', lambda x: x == 1.0) for exactly complete columns.
   - Prefer .hasDataType with a strong threshold (>= 0.95). If the assumption says 100% conformity, you may tighten to lambda x: x == 1.0.
   - For binary domains, always use .isContainedIn('<col>', [0, 1]) in addition to type checks.
   - For non-negativity, use .isNonNegative('<col>') and, when the overall range is known (e.g., 0..20 or 0..200), also add .hasMin and .hasMax.
   - For cross-column subset/ordering logic, use .isLessThanOrEqualTo. If you need a custom composite condition, use .satisfies with a descriptive constraintName, e.g.:
     - .satisfies('`A` <= `B`', 'A_le_B')
   - Distributional/monitoring checks derived from assumptions about variety or distinctness should be "warning", e.g.:
     - .hasDistinctness(['<col>'], lambda x: x >= 0.5)

5) Formatting and quoting
   - Always wrap column names with spaces or special characters in single quotes in API calls: '.hasMin('My col', ...)' is fine.
   - Within .satisfies SQL, must use backticks around column names: '`My col`'.
   - Regex patterns must be raw strings (r"...").

6) Quality bar
   - Include all hard invariants that are clearly implied by assumptions and usage in code (to maximize recall).
   - Avoid redundant duplicates unless each adds value (e.g., type + domain + range are acceptable together).
   - Ensure every constraint has at least one linked assumption.

Examples of domain mappings seen in this project (use them when applicable to the target column)
- 'Tuition fees up to date', 'Debtor': binary 0/1, integral, complete.
- 'Application order': integral in [1..9].
- 'Curricular units {1st|2nd} sem (enrolled|approved|credited|without evaluations)': integral, non-negative; each of (approved|credited|without evaluations) <= enrolled for the same semester.
- 'Curricular units {1st|2nd} sem (grade)': numeric in [0, 20].
- 'Admission grade', 'Previous qualification (grade)': numeric in [0, 200].
- Ratios/scores clipped to [0, 1]: enforce 0.0 <= col <= 1.0.

Return format
- Return only the JSON-like Python list of dicts in constraint_code, nothing else. Each dict:
  - "suggestion": string starting with "." or a top-level function name exactly as PyDeequ expects.
  - "level": "error" or "warning".
  - "linked assumptions": list of integer indices.

Available functions (reference)
Row-level:
- .isComplete(column)
- .hasCompleteness(column, assertion)
- .areComplete(columns)
- .haveCompleteness(columns, assertion)
- .areAnyComplete(columns)
- .satisfies(columnCondition, constraintName, [assertion])
- .hasPattern(column, pattern, [assertion], [name])  [use raw strings]
- .containsCreditCardNumber(column, [assertion])
- .containsEmail(column, [assertion])
- .containsURL(column, [assertion])
- .containsSocialSecurityNumber(column, [assertion])
- .hasDataType(column, ConstrainableDataTypes.<...>, [assertion])
- .isNonNegative(column, [assertion])
- .isPositive(column, [assertion])
- .isLessThan(columnA, columnB, [assertion])
- .isLessThanOrEqualTo(columnA, columnB, [assertion])
- .isGreaterThan(columnA, columnB, [assertion])
- .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo(columnA, columnB, [assertion])
- .isContainedIn(column, allowed_values, [assertion])

Aggregate-level:
- .hasSize(assertion)
- .haveAnyCompleteness(columns, assertion)
- .isUnique(column)
- .hasUniqueness(columns, assertion)
- .hasDistinctness(columns, assertion)
- .hasUniqueValueRatio(columns, assertion)
- .hasNumberOfDistinctValues(column, assertion, binningUdf=None, maxBins=1000)
- .hasMutualInformation(columnA, columnB, assertion)
- .hasApproxQuantile(column, quantile, assertion)
- .hasMin(column, assertion)
- .hasMax(column, assertion)
- .hasMean(column, assertion)
- .hasStandardDeviation(column, assertion)
- .hasCorrelation(columnA, columnB, assertion)

2026/01/14 11:24:17 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 4: Proposed new text for dataflow_inspector: Task
Identify all 1-based line ranges in the given Python code where a specified target column is used or influences code execution, optionally restricted to a given sink variable.

Inputs
- code_script: The full Python snippet to analyze.
- target_column: The exact column name (string) to track. The name may include spaces or special characters (e.g., 'Daytime/evening attendance\t'); match it exactly.
- sink_variable: Optional. If empty, consider the entire snippet. If provided, include only lines that lie on a dependency path from the target column to this sink.

Output
Return only valid JSON with this exact shape:
{ "sources": [ { "start_line": int, "end_line": int }, ... ] }
- Use exact 1-based line numbers from the snippet.
- Merge consecutive line numbers into a single range; non-consecutive lines must be separate ranges.
- Sort ranges in ascending order. No extra keys or text.

What counts as “use” of the target column
Include a line if it meets any of these conditions:
1) Direct column access:
   - df['<target_column>'] or df[alias_var] where alias_var resolves to the target column string.
   - df.loc[..., '<target_column>'] or df.loc[..., alias_var].
   - Any pandas operation that indexes, selects, assigns, or computes using the column (e.g., .astype, .to_numeric, arithmetic, .clip, .fillna, .map, .isin, .value_counts, .quantile, groupby/agg, merges/joins using the column).
2) Alias definition and propagation:
   - var = '<target_column>' (define alias).
   - var2 = var (alias copy). Track chained aliases.
   - If an alias for the target column appears in a list/dict/set (e.g., num_cols = [..., alias, ...]), record that container for later use.
3) Loops and bulk transforms that act on the target via an iterator/placeholder:
   - for col in num_cols: ... df[col] ... If num_cols contains the alias/target, include:
     - The loop header line, and
     - The loop body lines that reference the iterator (e.g., df[col], type coercions, renames) because one iteration will process the target column.
4) Downstream dataflow (transitive dependencies):
   - If a line defines a new variable from a variable that already depends on the target (e.g., age = df['Age at enrollment'] -> age_risk = f(age) -> raw_score uses age_risk -> risk_score uses raw_score), include each assignment/use line along this chain.
   - Include lines that write or export target-derived variables (e.g., assigning to DataFrame columns, selecting them into out DataFrames, to_csv, json dump fields) when those outputs include or are computed from target-derived variables.
5) Column selection/output lists:
   - Selecting columns via a list that contains the target or its alias (e.g., out = df[[..., alias, ...]]).
   - Writing those selections to disk (e.g., to_csv) when they include the target or target-derived variables.

What NOT to include
- Lines that do not reference the target column (by literal or alias) and do not reference variables already known to depend on the target.
- Pure constant/configuration definitions that do not reference the target or any target-derived variable (e.g., allowed value lists like allowed_infl) unless the target/alias appears on the same line.
- Unrelated control-flow lines (if/with/for) that do not themselves reference the alias/iterator or do not enclose body lines that operate on the target.

Sink-restricted mode
- If sink_variable is provided (non-empty), include only lines that are on a dependency path from the target column to that sink. Treat the sink as a variable/column name:
  - If sink is a Python variable (e.g., risk_score), include lines that define it or compute inputs that flow into it from the target.
  - If sink is a DataFrame column name (e.g., 'risk_score'), include lines that assign/compute that column or write it out, plus upstream computations from the target that feed into it.
- Exclude branches that do not contribute to the sink.

Procedure (recommended)
1) Parse code_script into numbered lines (1-based), including blanks and comments (they count toward line numbers).
2) Build alias set for the target_column:
   - Add any variable assigned exactly to the target string.
   - Propagate through simple alias copies (var2 = var).
3) Identify direct-use lines:
   - Lines containing df['<target_column>'] or df[alias], or other explicit pandas operations on that column/alias.
   - Lines where the alias/target is included in column selectors (lists/dicts) subsequently used for selection or writing.
4) Identify loop-driven uses:
   - If a loop iterates over a container that contains the alias/target, include the loop header and any body lines that operate on the iterator variable for DataFrame column access.
5) Build a dependency map:
   - For each assignment target = expr, record which variables appear in expr.
   - Mark a line if expr depends (directly or transitively) on the target/alias or on a previously marked variable.
   - Propagate forward so all variables computed from target-dependent variables are included.
6) If sink_variable is provided, back-filter:
   - Keep only lines on paths that lead from the target to the sink variable/column; drop marked lines not on these paths.
7) Collect all marked line numbers, merge consecutive numbers into [start_line, end_line] ranges, sort ascending.
8) Output exactly:
   { "sources": [ { "start_line": int, "end_line": int }, ... ] }

Domain-specific patterns to watch for (from typical inputs)
- Column aliases for academic metrics: c1_apr = 'Curricular units 1st sem (approved)', c1_enr, c1_noe, c1_grd, etc., and their 2nd-sem counterparts. These aliases are used in divisions, ratios, and grade-based computations.
- Type coercion loops over a list of columns (num_cols) applying pd.to_numeric: include the loop if the target alias is in the list.
- Macro variables and mapping ranks (e.g., infl_col = 'Inflation rate'; infl_idx = df[infl_col].map(infl_rank).astype(int)): include lines that index df with infl_col and all downstream computations that use infl_idx; do not include the constant lists unless the target/alias appears on that line.
- Attendance/age features: age = df['Age at enrollment'].astype(float); age_risk = f(age); raw_score -> risk_score -> priority -> output. Include all steps along this chain and any write-out that includes these derived variables.
- Output steps: selecting columns into an output DataFrame and writing to CSV/JSON when those selections or computed fields depend on the target.

Validation
- Match the target column string exactly (including trailing tabs/spaces).
- Use 1-based line numbers.
- Output only valid JSON with a top-level "sources" key and an array of { "start_line", "end_line" } objects.

Your goal is to produce a JSON object of the form:
{ "assumptions": [{ "text": str, "sources": [{ "start_line": int, "end_line": int }] }, ...] }
- JSON numbers must not have leading zeros.
- sources is a list of inclusive line ranges from the provided focused_code that support each assumption. If an assumption comes only from target_column_desc (metadata) and not from code, set sources to an empty list.

Follow these steps and rules:

1) Parse the inputs
- target_column: exact column name string; preserve it verbatim in your text.
- target_column_desc: use it to extract and assert metadata-based constraints (e.g., completeness, data type, approximate number of distinct values).
- focused_code: inspect how the target column is referenced, cast, and used.
- downstream_task_description: may be empty; do not infer usage from it if unspecified.

2) Always include these column invariants (when supported by target_column_desc):
- Data type: If dataType is Integral, add an assumption that the column has Integral (integer) values (counts). If code coerces this column with pd.to_numeric(..., errors='coerce') or astype(int/float), also add a parseability assumption and cite those lines.
- Non-negativity: Because these curricular unit fields are counts, add an explicit assumption that values are >= 0. If the code uses the column as a denominator and replaces 0 with NaN (e.g., .replace(0, np.nan)), explicitly state that zero is allowed but is treated as NaN during ratio computations; cite those lines.
- Completeness: If completeness in target_column_desc is 1.0, add an assumption that there are no missing values for this column (note: this comes from metadata, so sources can be empty). If the code performs numeric coercion that could introduce NaNs (e.g., pd.to_numeric with errors='coerce'), add an assumption that inputs must be parseable to avoid unintended NaNs; cite the coercion lines.

3) Determine actual usage from the code and be precise
- Identify where the exact target_column (or its variable alias, e.g., c2_enr, c2_cred, etc.) is:
  - Declared/bound to a string constant (cite those lines).
  - Cast to numeric (astype(...) or pd.to_numeric): cite those lines.
  - Used to construct denominators: look for patterns like den = df[col].replace(0, np.nan); cite those lines.
  - Used in ratios: pass_rate, fail_rate, no_eval_ratio, credit_ratio, grade computations; cite calculation lines.
  - Propagated into composite metrics (e.g., academic_risk, academic_gap, credit_gap_avg), then into risk_score; cite those lines.
- Only claim usage that is explicitly present. Many scripts load columns but do not actually use them in the final metrics. If the target column is only defined/cast but not used in any ratio or in the final risk/priority logic, add an assumption that it is present but not directly used in downstream risk computations; cite the definition/cast lines and avoid claiming effects on risk.

4) Domain-specific guidance for common columns
- Curricular units 2nd sem (enrolled): typically used as a denominator for second-semester rates (den2 = df[c2_enr].replace(0, np.nan)). Include the zero-handling and denominator role assumptions with citations.
- Curricular units 1st/2nd sem (approved/credited/without evaluations): may be used as numerators in rate computations (e.g., credit_ratio, no_eval_ratio) in some scripts; verify usage before asserting. In some scripts, credited fields are loaded but not used in the final academic gap/academic risk; do not claim usage unless you find it in formulas.
- Grade columns are often normalized by 20; if your target column is a grade, assert expected range normalization accordingly only if present in code.

5) Map assumptions to validation-friendly constraints
To improve alignment with validation, include explicit assumptions that correspond to:
- hasDataType(target_column, Integral) when dataType is Integral in target_column_desc.
- isComplete(target_column) when completeness is 1.0 in target_column_desc.
- hasMin(target_column, x >= 0) for count-like columns.
These can be metadata-based; use empty sources when not present in code.

6) Sourcing
- Use the exact line numbers shown in focused_code for start_line and end_line (inclusive). Prefer the smallest range that precisely supports the claim.
- Typical places to cite:
  - The constant assignment of the column alias (e.g., c2_enr = 'Curricular units 2nd sem (enrolled)').
  - Numeric coercions (pd.to_numeric(...), astype(int/float)).
  - Zero-to-NaN replacement for denominators.
  - The exact ratio computations and any aggregation into academic risk/score.
  - Final risk_score or priority usage only if the target column’s derived features flow into those computations.

- Do not state that the column “contributes to academic risk” unless you can trace it through computed features that enter academic_risk/academic_gap/credit_gap_avg/etc. and into risk_score.
- If the column is read but unused downstream, say so clearly.

8) Keep the output concise, factual, and in the required JSON format
- No extra keys beyond "assumptions".
- Each assumption should be a single, clear sentence.
- Use multiple assumptions when needed to cover type, completeness, non-negativity, zero-handling, and actual usage.

Example assumption patterns to include when applicable:
- "The 'X' column contains non-negative integer counts (Integral data type) representing ..."; sources: [] and/or numeric coercion lines.
- "The 'X' column is complete (no missing values) per metadata (completeness: 1.0)."; sources: [].

- "'X' is loaded but not used in the academic risk or risk score computations in this script."; sources: definition/cast lines.

Use the provided metadata, code snippet, downstream task description, and assumptions to infer concrete, actionable data-quality constraints. Your output will be parsed by code, so follow the formatting rules exactly.

Task
- Generate PyDeequ constraints for the target_column that ensure downstream code can run without errors and the data semantics assumed by the script hold.
- Output: a JSON list of objects with the shape:
  [
    {"suggestion": ".<PyDeequ call>", "level": "warning"|"error", "linked assumptions": [indices]},

  ]

- target_column: Name of the column to validate.

- .hasPattern(column, pattern, [assertion], [name])  // pattern must be a raw string r"..."
- .containsCreditCardNumber(column, [assertion])
- .containsEmail(column, [assertion])
- .containsURL(column, [assertion])
- .containsSocialSecurityNumber(column, [assertion])
- .hasDataType(column, ConstrainableDataTypes.<...>, [assertion])  // String, Integral, Fractional, Boolean, Numeric, Null
- .isNonNegative(column, [assertion])
- .isPositive(column, [assertion])
- .isLessThan(columnA, columnB, [assertion])
- .isLessThanOrEqualTo(columnA, columnB, [assertion])
- .isGreaterThan(columnA, columnB, [assertion])
- .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo(columnA, columnB, [assertion])
- .isContainedIn(column, allowed_values, [assertion])

Aggregate-level:
- .hasSize(assertion)
- .haveAnyCompleteness(columns, assertion)
- .isUnique(column)
- .hasUniqueness(columns, assertion)
- .hasDistinctness(columns, assertion)
- .hasUniqueValueRatio(columns, assertion)
- .hasNumberOfDistinctValues(column, assertion)  // requires binningUdf/maxBins at runtime; you may omit those here
- .hasMutualInformation(columnA, columnB, assertion)
- .hasApproxQuantile(column, quantile, assertion)
- .hasMin(column, assertion)
- .hasMax(column, assertion)
- .hasMean(column, assertion)
- .hasStandardDeviation(column, assertion)
- .hasCorrelation(columnA, columnB, assertion)

General rules
1) Prioritize constraints that prevent runtime errors and enforce core semantics used in the code:
   - Completeness when completeness is 1.0 in metadata or the code assumes no nulls.
   - Data type constraints based on target_column_desc.dataType or explicit casts in code.
   - Bounds or domain restrictions when the column drives indexing, division, or ratios.
   - Use dedicated comparators (e.g., .isLessThanOrEqualTo) for cross-column relationships when validating the target column against another column used in code.

2) Use the code_snippet to infer how the target column is used:
   - If the column is converted with pd.to_numeric(..., errors='coerce') and then standardized or used in arithmetic, enforce Fractional or Numeric data type and completeness.
   - If the column is cast to int/Int64, enforce Integral data type and, if counts, non-negativity.
   - If the column is used as a binary flag (0/1), enforce allowed values [0, 1].
   - If the column indexes arrays (e.g., commit_weights[app_order]), enforce integer domain and valid range (see domain specifics below).

3) Use target_column_desc to set reasonable constraints:
   - If completeness is 1.0, add .isComplete(target_column) with level="error".
   - If histogram lists explicit discrete values and approximateNumDistinctValues is small (e.g., <= 24), consider .isContainedIn with those values (error if strictly required by code, else warning).
   - Use histogram min/max or obvious business logic to set conservative .hasMin/.hasMax bounds (e.g., rates >= 0, grades within 0..20, age within plausible human range).
   - For Numeric columns where negatives are meaningful (e.g., GDP growth), do not enforce non-negativity.

4) Levels:
   - Use level="error" when violations would break the script (e.g., nulls before arithmetic, invalid indices 1..9 for Application order, non-numeric where numeric math is done, impossible relations like approved > enrolled).
   - Use level="warning" for distributional checks (.hasApproxQuantile, .hasNumberOfDistinctValues, .hasMean, .hasStandardDeviation) or tight domain enumerations that might evolve.

5) Cross-column rules:
   - Only add cross-column constraints if they directly involve the target_column and the paired columns are clearly present in the snippet (e.g., approved <= enrolled for the same semester).
   - Prefer the dedicated comparator functions over .satisfies when possible:
     - .isLessThanOrEqualTo('<target>', '<other>')
     - .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<target>', '<other>')
   - For count columns in academic data:
     - approved <= enrolled
     - credited <= enrolled
     - without evaluations <= enrolled
     - Non-negativity for all count-like fields

6) Output format:
   - Output only the JSON list (no prose).
   - Each entry: "suggestion" (PyDeequ call starting with '.'), "level" ("error"|"warning"), "linked assumptions" (list of indices).
   - Use backticks (`) for any column containing spaces inside SQL in .satisfies.
   - Use raw strings r"..." for regex patterns in .hasPattern.

Domain-specific knowledge for this dataset
- Macroeconomic columns used in scripts:
  - GDP: Fractional numeric; allowed discrete set often observed:
    [-4.06, -3.12, -1.7, -0.92, 0.32, 0.79, 1.74, 1.79, 2.02, 3.51]
    Negative values are valid. Used in standardization and macro stress; enforce completeness and Fractional type. Consider isContainedIn with the above set if metadata shows exactly these values.
  - Unemployment rate: Fractional numeric; typical values:
    [7.6, 8.9, 9.4, 10.8, 11.1, 12.4, 12.7, 13.9, 15.5, 16.2]
    Represents percent-like numbers; enforce completeness, Fractional type, hasMin >= 0.0, hasMax <= 100.0. Consider isContainedIn with the list above when applicable.
  - Inflation rate: Fractional numeric; typical values:
    [-0.8, -0.3, 0.3, 0.5, 0.6, 1.4, 2.6, 2.8, 3.7]
    May be negative; enforce completeness and Fractional type. Consider isContainedIn when applicable.

- Application order:
  - Used as array index (commit_weights[app_order]) with expected values 1..9 inclusive.
  - Enforce Integral type, completeness, and domain 1..9 (use .isContainedIn([...]) or .satisfies('`Application order` BETWEEN 1 AND 9', '...')).

- Binary flags:
  - Debtor, Tuition fees up to date, Scholarship holder: cast to int; enforce Integral type, completeness, and allowed values [0, 1].

- Academic counts and grades:
  - Curricular units 1st/2nd sem (enrolled|approved|credited|without evaluations): Integral, non-negative.
  - Relations per semester:
    - approved <= enrolled
    - credited <= enrolled
    - without evaluations <= enrolled
    - Optionally, (approved + credited + without evaluations) <= enrolled (use as warning if unsure).
  - Curricular units ... (grade): Fractional within [0.0, 20.0].

- Age at enrollment: Numeric; reasonable bounds [15, 100] (use warning if bounds are not explicitly stated).

Best practices
- Prefer comparator constraints (.isLessThanOrEqualTo, .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo) to encode relationships instead of .satisfies when available.
- Avoid dataset-wide or non-column-specific constraints like .hasSize unless directly justified by the target column assumptions.
- Keep constraints conservative to avoid false positives; where exact allowed sets are present in metadata or code, you may use isContainedIn (error if strictly required by downstream logic).
- Link each constraint to the most relevant assumption indices.

Examples of good mappings
- If target_column is 'Curricular units 1st sem (approved)':
  - .isComplete('Curricular units 1st sem (approved)')  // error
  - .hasDataType('Curricular units 1st sem (approved)', ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral)  // error
  - .isNonNegative('Curricular units 1st sem (approved)')  // error
  - .isLessThanOrEqualTo('Curricular units 1st sem (approved)', 'Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)')  // error
  - Optionally .hasNumberOfDistinctValues('Curricular units 1st sem (approved)', lambda x: x >= 20 and x <= 30)  // warning

- If target_column is 'Unemployment rate':
  - .isComplete('Unemployment rate')  // error
  - .hasDataType('Unemployment rate', ConstrainableDataTypes.Fractional)  // error
  - .hasMin('Unemployment rate', lambda x: x >= 0.0)  // error
  - .hasMax('Unemployment rate', lambda x: x <= 100.0)  // error
  - Optionally .hasApproxQuantile('Unemployment rate', 0.5, lambda x: x >= 0.0 and x <= 100.0)  // warning
  - Optionally .isContainedIn('Unemployment rate', [7.6, 8.9, 9.4, 10.8, 11.1, 12.4, 12.7, 13.9, 15.5, 16.2])  // warning or error based on assumptions

Remember: produce only the JSON list of constraint objects with valid PyDeequ calls, appropriate levels, and correct assumption indices.

2026/01/14 11:29:37 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 7: Proposed new text for dataflow_inspector: You are given:
- code_script: a Python code snippet.
- target_column: the exact column name to track (string).
- sink_variable: optional variable name; if non-empty, only include lines that ultimately affect this sink.

Your task:
Return only valid JSON of the form:
{ "sources": [{ "start_line": int, "end_line": int }, ...] }
with 1-based line numbers from code_script, representing contiguous ranges of lines that read, write, filter, transform, merge, or otherwise pass through the target column. Merge adjacent lines into one range; non-adjacent lines must be separate ranges. No extra text or formatting.

Key domain context and common patterns:
- Datasets often include columns such as:
  - "Curricular units 1st/2nd sem (enrolled/approved/credited/without evaluations/grade)"
  - "Application order", "Daytime/evening attendance\t", "Debtor", "Tuition fees up to date", "Scholarship holder"
  - "GDP", "Unemployment rate", "Inflation rate"
- Column aliases are frequently defined as variables first, then used:
  - Example: c2_apr = 'Curricular units 2nd sem (approved)'; later a2 = df[c2_apr].astype(float)
  - Example: col_ap2 = 'Curricular units 2nd sem (approved)'; later ap2 = pd.to_numeric(df[col_ap2], errors='coerce').astype(int)
- Derived variables are computed from the column and then used in further computations and risk aggregation:
  - pass rates: pass_rate2 = np.where(en2 > 0, ap2 / en2.replace(0, np.nan), 1.0)
  - statistics: ap2.std(), ap2.mean(), ap2.corr(en2), expected_ap2, success_gap2
  - aggregates: total_approved = ap1 + ap2; progress = total_approved / total_enrolled
  - downstream components: progress_component, risk_raw, risk_score, priority tiers, flags
- Multi-line expressions are common (e.g., risk composition blocks). Include all lines in such an expression if any part depends (transitively) on the target column.
- Output selections may “pass through” the column by including it in DataFrame column lists.

What to include (taint/dataflow rules):
1) Line numbering:
   - Split code_script by newline. Use 1-based line numbers. Count comments and blank lines.

2) Identify alias variables for the target column string:
   - Include a variable as an alias if you find an assignment of the form:
     var = 'exact target_column'
   - Record the alias var name(s).
   - Include the alias definition line only if the alias is used downstream in a way that reads/passes the column (see steps 3–6). If never used, do not include the alias line by itself.

3) Identify direct column reads and writes:
   - Any line that indexes a DataFrame with the target column string or its alias, e.g.:
     df[target_column], df[alias], df.loc[:, alias], df.filter([...]) including alias/target_column
     pd.to_numeric(df[alias], ...), df[alias].astype(...), df[alias].map(...), df[alias].replace(...)
   - Mark the left-hand-side variable (if any) as “dependent”.
   - Include these lines.

4) Propagate dependencies (forward):
   - If a line uses any dependent variable, include that line and mark any new variables assigned on that line as dependent.
   - Consider all operations as propagation: arithmetic, boolean ops, comparisons, indexing, numpy/pandas ops (mean, std, corr, clip, where, fillna, astype, map, replace, cut, pd.concat, merges/joins, etc.).
   - Propagate through multi-line expressions: include all contiguous lines that form a single expression if any dependent variable is referenced in that expression.

5) Selection and export that passes the column or its data:
   - Include lines that pass the target column through outputs/selections:
     - out = df[[..., alias_or_target_column, ...]].copy()
     - queue_cols = [..., alias_or_target_column, ...]
     - out.to_csv(...), queue[queue_cols].to_csv(...), if the selected columns include the target column alias/string.
   - If only the alias string variable appears in a list (and that list is used to select columns), it counts as “pass through”.

6) Statistics and higher-level features:
   - Include lines computing statistics from the dependent variable(s): .mean(), .std(), .corr(), expected values, gaps, normalized grades, ratios, pass rates, totals/aggregates that include the dependent variable, and any downstream risk components and final scores that use those features.
   - Example patterns to include for 'Curricular units 2nd sem (approved)':
     - col_ap2 = '...approved...'
     - ap2 = pd.to_numeric(df[col_ap2], ...)
     - pass_rate2, slope2/intercept2, expected_ap2, success_gap2
     - total_approved = ap1 + ap2; progress
     - progress_component; risk_raw; risk_score; priority; any flags using these.

7) If sink_variable is provided (non-empty):
   - Restrict to lines on any dataflow path from the target column to sink_variable.
   - Build a backward dependency set starting from sink_variable and all its contributing variables (recursively). Intersect with the forward dependency set from steps 3–4 to keep only lines that truly affect the sink.
   - Exclude lines not on a path to the sink, even if they read the target column.

8) Merging and ordering:
   - After collecting included line numbers, sort them and merge contiguous sequences into ranges {start_line, end_line}.
   - Return only the JSON object with "sources": [ranges], ordered by start_line.

What to exclude:
- Lines that merely define the alias variable but are never used in a way that reads/passes the column.
- Unrelated code that doesn’t read/transform/pass the target column or its derived variables, or doesn’t contribute to the sink when sink_variable is set.
- Comments only lines, unless part of a merged multi-line expression with a dependent code line (normally comments are separate and should be excluded).

Implementation hints:
- Token matching:
  - For alias detection, match exact string equality to target_column on the right-hand side.
  - For usage detection, look for df[alias] or df['exact target_column'] and treat the assigned LHS as dependent.
  - For propagation, treat any usage of a dependent variable name as propagation; avoid matching inside string literals or comments.
- Be careful with chained assignments and intermediate variables (e.g., e2_safe from e2, then used to compute ratios).
- Consider that computations may use short names (a2, ap2) derived from df[alias]; you must propagate through these.
- Handle multi-line parentheses: each physical line in the expression is included if any part references dependent variables.

Output format strictly:
- Return only: { "sources": [{ "start_line": int, "end_line": int }, ... ] }
- No markdown, no extra keys, no commentary.

What to include (prioritize these, as they map to common validators used in scoring):
1) From target_column_desc (schema/quality):

7) Output format:
   - Return only a single top-level JSON object: { "assumptions": [ ... ] }.

- Start by mapping the target_column to the exact code alias/string (watch for subtle differences like trailing tabs).

2026/01/14 11:34:12 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 9: Proposed new text for ir_generation: You are given a single column (target_column) and context (profiling stats, a code snippet showing how it’s used, and free-text assumptions). Generate a list of PyDeequ constraint suggestions for that column only.

Goal:
- Produce constraint code that will prevent runtime errors in the downstream code and preserve intended semantics. Favor constraints that are strictly required by how the column is used. Add additional domain-consistent constraints when strongly implied by assumptions and stats.

Inputs:
- target_column: exact column name (preserve it literally, including any tabs or trailing spaces).
- target_column_desc: profiling summary (completeness, approximateNumDistinctValues, dataType, histogram).
- code_snippet: Python code that uses this column (often pandas). Infer how the column is used (type, value range, set membership, relationships to other columns).
- downstream_task_description: optional.
- assumptions: a numbered list of assumptions for this column derived from the snippet/desc. Your constraints must reference these indices.

Output:
- constraint_code: a JSON-like Python list of dicts:
  [
    {'suggestion': '<PyDeequ method call starting with "." or a function>', 'level': 'error'|'warning', 'linked assumptions': [int, ...]},
    ...
  ]
- Do not include any extra commentary. Do not wrap in Markdown. Only return the list structure.

Critical formatting rules:
- Use the target_column string exactly as provided. Do not escape or normalize characters. If the name contains tabs or trailing spaces, include them literally in the quotes.
- Start each suggestion with a dot (.) so it can be chained in a Check builder, e.g., .isComplete('...').
- When using .satisfies, write SQL-like conditions and wrap column names with backticks if they contain spaces or special characters. Use the exact literal column name inside the backticks.
- For regex patterns in .hasPattern, use raw strings, e.g., r'^\d+$'.
- Only use the functions listed below. For hasNumberOfDistinctValues, include binningUdf=None and maxBins=1000 as shown.

Available Row-Level Functions:
- .isComplete(column)
- .hasCompleteness(column, assertion)
- .areComplete(columns)
- .haveCompleteness(columns, assertion)
- .areAnyComplete(columns)
- .satisfies(columnCondition, constraintName[, assertion])
- .hasPattern(column, pattern[, assertion[, name]])  // use raw string patterns
- .containsCreditCardNumber(column[, assertion])
- .containsEmail(column[, assertion])
- .containsURL(column[, assertion])
- .containsSocialSecurityNumber(column[, assertion])
- .hasDataType(column, datatype[, assertion])  // ConstrainableDataTypes.{Null,Fractional,Integral,Boolean,String,Numeric}
- .isNonNegative(column[, assertion])
- .isPositive(column[, assertion])
- .isLessThan(columnA, columnB[, assertion])
- .isLessThanOrEqualTo(columnA, columnB[, assertion])
- .isGreaterThan(columnA, columnB[, assertion])
- .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo(columnA, columnB[, assertion])
- .isContainedIn(column, allowed_values[, assertion])

Available Aggregate-Level Functions:
- .hasSize(assertion)
- .haveAnyCompleteness(columns, assertion)
- .isUnique(column)
- .hasUniqueness(columns, assertion)
- .hasDistinctness(columns, assertion)
- .hasUniqueValueRatio(columns, assertion)
- .hasNumberOfDistinctValues(column, assertion, binningUdf=None, maxBins=1000)
- .hasMutualInformation(columnA, columnB, assertion)
- .hasApproxQuantile(column, quantile, assertion)
- .hasMinLength(column, assertion)
- .hasMaxLength(column, assertion)
- .hasMin(column, assertion)
- .hasMax(column, assertion)
- .hasMean(column, assertion)
- .hasStandardDeviation(column, assertion)
- .hasCorrelation(columnA, columnB, assertion)

How to derive constraints:
1) Type and completeness:
   - If code casts to int/float or uses arithmetic, comparisons, or array indexing with this column, set:
     - .isComplete(target_column) as 'error' when desc.completeness == 1.0 or code assumes no nulls (e.g., astype(int) with no null handling).
     - Otherwise .hasCompleteness(target_column, lambda x: x >= threshold) with threshold inferred from assumptions (default 0.95) and level 'warning' unless code would break.
     - .hasDataType with the strictest needed type:
       - Integral for counts and 0/1 encoded fields.
       - Fractional/Numeric for ratios, grades, continuous measures.
   - Link these to assumptions stating completeness/type.

2) Value sets and ranges from code usage:
   - If used as a binary indicator (e.g., 1 - col, mean over 0/1, or histogram shows only 0 and 1):
     - .isContainedIn(target_column, [0, 1]) [error]
     - .hasMin(target_column, lambda x: x >= 0) [error]
     - .hasMax(target_column, lambda x: x <= 1) [error]
     - .hasNumberOfDistinctValues(target_column, lambda x: x == 2, binningUdf=None, maxBins=1000) [warning or error depending on assumptions]
   - If code uses isin([...]) to gate logic or mapping to a fixed set (e.g., Application order in [1..6] or commit_weights index 1..9):
     - .isContainedIn(target_column, allowed_list) [error]
     - Optionally .hasMin/.hasMax to bound range [error].
   - If code maps values to indices using an explicit allowed list (e.g., GDP/unemployment/inflation lists):
     - .isContainedIn(target_column, that_explicit_list) [error].
   - If counts are used (enrolled/approved/credited/without evaluations):
     - .isNonNegative(target_column) [error]
     - .hasMin(target_column, lambda x: x >= 0) [error]
     - If approximateNumDistinctValues suggests 21 for semester unit counts, assume 0..20 is the valid range unless contradicted by assumptions:
       - .hasMax(target_column, lambda x: x <= 20) [warning or error if explicitly stated].
   - If grades are on a 0–20 scale (as common in the provided scripts):
     - .hasDataType(target_column, ConstrainableDataTypes.Fractional or Numeric) [error]
     - .hasMin(target_column, lambda x: x >= 0) [error]
     - .hasMax(target_column, lambda x: x <= 20) [error]

3) Cross-column relational constraints when strongly implied by assumptions/domain and the code uses those columns together:
   - For academic unit columns in the same semester:
     - credited <= approved <= enrolled:
       - .isLessThanOrEqualTo('Curricular units X sem (credited)', 'Curricular units X sem (approved)') [error]
       - .isLessThanOrEqualTo('Curricular units X sem (approved)', 'Curricular units X sem (enrolled)') [error]
     - If the target_column is one of these, express the relationship with target_column on one side as appropriate.
   - For the target used as a denominator guard (e.g., replaced 0 with NaN to avoid division by zero), do NOT force strictly positive; allow zero but enforce non-negative integer.

4) Levels:
   - Use 'error' when violating the constraint would break the code, cause exceptions, or invalidate the modeled logic (type, completeness needed by astype/int operations, set membership needed for indexing/mapping, non-negativity for counts, hard ranges like 0/1).
   - Use 'warning' for softer expectations from profiling (e.g., exact number of distinct values, typical maxima inferred from metadata rather than code necessity).

5) Link to assumptions:
   - For each constraint, include indices of all assumptions that support it.
   - If multiple assumptions reinforce a constraint, include all their indices.
   - If a constraint is purely from code usage and not explicitly in assumptions, link to the closest relevant assumption(s) (e.g., type/completeness or usage description). Avoid empty lists.

6) Do:
   - Quote column names with single quotes in PyDeequ method calls and preserve them exactly (including any literal tabs or trailing spaces).
   - In .satisfies conditions, wrap the exact column name (including special characters) with backticks.
   - Prefer explicit .isContainedIn over .satisfies IN(...) unless you need a composite condition.
   - When using .hasNumberOfDistinctValues, include binningUdf=None, maxBins=1000.

7) Don’t:
   - Don’t change or escape the target column name; never replace literal control characters (e.g., a tab) with escape sequences.
   - Don’t add constraints for unrelated columns unless they are part of a cross-column relation that directly constrains the target_column.
   - Don’t invent thresholds not supported by assumptions or code usage; be conservative.

Domain-specific hints from the provided scripts:
- 'Daytime/evening attendance\t' is a 0/1 integral indicator (1=day, 0=evening).
- 'Application order' typically uses allowed sets either [1..6] or [1..9], depending on the script; use the set explicitly present in the code.
- Semester unit columns (enrolled/approved/credited/without evaluations) are integral, non-negative, typically within 0..20, and obey credited <= approved <= enrolled.
- Grade columns are on a 0–20 scale and numeric (fractional).
- Macro columns (GDP, Unemployment rate, Inflation rate) are constrained to an explicit allowed list when mapped.

Return only the constraint_code list, no explanations.

Task
- Identify all lines in the snippet where the target column is read, written, filtered on, transformed, merged, passed through, or otherwise influences downstream computations or outputs.
- If sink_variable is provided, only include lines that are on a dataflow path that ultimately affects that sink. If sink_variable is empty, consider all usages throughout the snippet.

Input
- code_script: the full code snippet as provided (line numbers are 1-based, and include blank lines and comments).
- target_column: the exact column name (string).
- sink_variable: optional. If empty, consider the entire snippet. If provided, include only lines that influence that sink.

Output
- Return only valid JSON with this schema:
  { "sources": [ { "start_line": int, "end_line": int }, ... ] }
- Merge adjacent used lines into a single range. Non-adjacent lines must be separate ranges.
- Sort ranges by start_line. Use integers for line numbers.
- If there are no matches, return {"sources": []}.

What counts as a “use” of the target column
1) Direct DataFrame access by column:
   - df['<target_column>'], df[[..., '<target_column>', ...]]
   - df[col_var] where col_var holds '<target_column>'
   - df.loc[:, '<target_column>'] or df.loc[mask, '<target_column>']
   - Any pandas/numpy operation that reads the column via these patterns (e.g., pd.to_numeric(df[col], ...), astype, arithmetic, comparisons, boolean masks, groupby keys, merges/joins on that column, etc.)

2) Column alias definitions:
   - If a variable is assigned the literal target column name (e.g., col_x = '<target_column>'), include that alias definition line.
   - Track every subsequent use of that alias variable as a use of the target column.

3) Derived variables and propagation (dataflow/taint):
   - If a variable is computed from the target column (directly or via an alias), mark it tainted. Any variable that depends on a tainted variable is also tainted.
   - Include lines that compute tainted variables via:
     - arithmetic, logical operations, numpy functions (np.where, np.clip, np.maximum, correlations, means, std, etc.)
     - pandas transformations (astype, to_numeric, division, addition, ratios, cuts/bins, etc.)
     - boolean filtering or masks derived from the target column
     - aggregations, combinations, concatenations where the tainted signal is carried forward
   - Continue propagating taint through all downstream computations until the signal is no longer used.

4) Passing through in selections and outputs:
   - If the target column (or its alias) is included in a list of columns used to subset or export a DataFrame (e.g., queue_cols = [..., col_alias, ...]; df[queue_cols].to_csv(...)), include:
     - the line defining the list that includes the target column (or its alias)
     - the line(s) where that list is used for selection, export, or further processing
   - If tainted variables contribute to outputs (e.g., written to CSV, assigned to DataFrame columns, used to sort/select), include those lines because the target column’s value “passes through” them.

5) Sinks (when sink_variable is provided):
   - Treat sink_variable as the name of the final variable or output you are focusing on. Include only lines that influence this sink via the taint rules above.
   - Match sink_variable if it appears as:
     - a variable identifier (e.g., risk_score)
     - a DataFrame column assignment or access like df['risk_score'] (match the string 'risk_score')
     - a named intermediate that is written out (e.g., used in to_csv, sorting, or downstream selections)
   - Build the taint path forward from the target column and only keep the subset of tainted lines that lead to the sink. If multiple paths exist, include all lines on any path to the sink.

Important patterns from typical scripts (domain-specific cues)
- Columns may first be assigned to alias variables:
  - col_app_order = 'Application order'
  - col_wo1 = 'Curricular units 1st sem (without evaluations)'
  - col_gr2 = 'Curricular units 2nd sem (grade)'
  Include the alias line if it’s the target column.
- Reading columns through aliases and converting types:
  - x = pd.to_numeric(df[col_alias], errors='coerce').astype(int or 'Int64')
- Propagation through derived features:
  - Ratios and normalized values (e.g., noeval_ratio1 = np.where(en1 > 0, wo1 / en1.replace(0, np.nan), 0.0))
  - Aggregations of semester metrics (e.g., mean_noeval_ratio = (noeval_ratio1 + noeval_ratio2) / 2.0)
  - Risk components and composition:
    - payment_component, progress_component, grade_component, gap_component, noeval_component, commitment_component, macro_component, prep_component
    - risk_raw = sum of components
    - risk_score = np.clip(risk_raw, 0.0, 1.0)
  If the target column contributes to any component (directly or via derived features), include the lines computing that component, risk_raw, risk_score, and any downstream usage.
- Output and queue generation:
  - output = df.copy()
  - output['dropout_risk_score'] = risk_score
  - output['priority_tier'] = pd.cut(output['dropout_risk_score'], bins=..., labels=...).astype(str)
  - queue_cols = [..., col_app_order, ...]
  - queue = output.sort_values('dropout_risk_score', ascending=False).head(200)
  - output[...] or queue[...].to_csv(...)
  If the target column flows into risk_score or other outputs, include these lines. If the target column (or its alias) appears in queue_cols, include the queue_cols definition and the selection/export lines.

Adjacency and ranges
- Use exact 1-based line numbers from the snippet.
- Merge only truly adjacent lines (e.g., 10 and 11 merge; a gap at 12 breaks the range). Comments and blank lines count in numbering; if they are not “used,” they break adjacency.
- Do not include unrelated lines (imports, unrelated constants) unless they read/transform/pass the target column or are on the tainted path to the sink.

Precision vs. recall
- When uncertain, prefer including a line if it clearly depends on a tainted variable or includes the target column (directly or via alias). Do not include broad unrelated blocks (e.g., entire alias definition sections) unless the specific line defines the target column alias.

JSON-only output
- Example format: {"sources":[{"start_line":12,"end_line":15},{"start_line":27,"end_line":27}]}
- Use double quotes for keys/strings. No trailing commas. No extra text.

Edge cases
- If the target column never appears (directly or via alias) and does not influence any computation or sink, return {"sources": []}.
- If sink_variable is provided but never appears, return {"sources": []} unless you can trace a clear path where a tainted variable feeds that sink by name or as a DataFrame column.

2026/01/14 11:40:52 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 12: Proposed new text for ir_generation: Task: Generate PyDeequ validation constraints for a single target column using:
- target_column (exact column name)
- target_column_desc (profile: completeness, approx distinct values, dataType)
- code_snippet (how the column is used downstream)
- downstream_task_description (optional context)
- assumptions (indexed list; cite indices in “linked assumptions”)

Output format:
- Return a Python list of dicts. Each dict must have:
  - "suggestion": a PyDeequ Check-builder call starting with "." (chainable method) or function (if applicable). Use the function names exactly as provided in the Available Functions lists.
  - "level": "error" or "warning"
  - "linked assumptions": [list of integer indices from the assumptions that justify this constraint]

General strategy:
1) Parse target_column_desc first to set base constraints:
   - Completeness:
     - If completeness == 1.0, add .isComplete('<col>') with level "error".
     - If completeness < 1.0 or not guaranteed, add .hasCompleteness('<col>', lambda x: x >= threshold) with level "warning". Choose threshold from the description or a conservative default (e.g., 0.95) if not stated.
   - Data type:
     - Map profile dataType to ConstrainableDataTypes: Fractional, Integral, Boolean, String, Numeric.
     - Add .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.<type>) with level "error".
   - Approx distinctness:
     - If approximateNumDistinctValues is present, add a banded distinct-count check as "warning":
       .hasNumberOfDistinctValues('<col>', lambda x: x >= lower and x <= upper)
       where lower and upper are a reasonable band around the provided value (e.g., ±20% or ±5 counts, whichever is larger). Avoid equality checks on distinct counts.

2) Analyze code_snippet for how the target column is used. Derive constraints from:
   - Casting/conversions:
     - If the column is cast with .astype(int) or similar in the code, keep hasDataType(..., Integral) with level "error".
     - If .astype(float) or fractional arithmetic is used, use Fractional.
   - Arithmetic and bounds:
     - If code forms ratios or clips to [0, 1] using the target column, enforce expected ranges with min/max or satisfies:
       - For numeric [0, 1] normalization, prefer:
         .hasMin('<col>', lambda x: x >= 0.0) and .hasMax('<col>', lambda x: x <= 1.0) (level "warning" unless stated as invariant).
       - For known domain bounds (e.g., grades 0..20), add:
         .hasMin('<col>', lambda x: x >= 0.0) and .hasMax('<col>', lambda x: x <= 20.0) (typically "error" for hard domain rules).
   - Division-denominator usage:
     - If the code replaces zeros with NaN to avoid division by zero (e.g., replace(0, np.nan)), do NOT enforce positivity; zero is allowed. Add:
       .isNonNegative('<col>') (level "error") for counts, and avoid .isPositive unless the code or assumptions require strictly > 0.
   - Logical relationships and cross-column constraints:
     - If the target is a count of approvals/credits/without-evaluations and the code relates it to enrolled counts, add:
       .isLessThanOrEqualTo('<target>', '<enrolled_col>') (level "error").
     - If the target is enrolled counts and the code uses approved/credited/no-eval alongside it, add constraints to ensure logical consistency:
       .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<enrolled_col>', '<approved_col>')
       .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<enrolled_col>', '<credited_col>')
       .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<enrolled_col>', '<without_eval_col>')
       These can be "error" when stated or implied invariants.
   - Correlation/relationships used in modeling:
     - If the code computes regression-like expectations or refers to “strong positive correlation” (e.g., approvals vs enrolled), add:
       .hasCorrelation('<colA>', '<colB>', lambda x: x >= 0.6) (level "warning" unless the assumptions mark it as a strict precondition).
   - Enumerations/allowed sets and isin checks:
     - If the code uses .isin([...]) or maps values by membership, add:
       .isContainedIn('<col>', [allowed_values]) (level "error" if mandatory; "warning" otherwise).
   - Multi-column completeness when used together:
     - If the code uses a group of columns together (e.g., to compute ratios), add:
       .areComplete([list_of_cols]) (level "error") when completeness is expected to be perfect, else:
       .haveCompleteness([list_of_cols], lambda x: x >= threshold) (level "warning").

3) Domain defaults for the student dataset (apply only when consistent with assumptions and code):
   - Semester counts:
     - Columns like “Curricular units [1st|2nd] sem (enrolled|approved|credited|without evaluations)” are non-negative integers.
     - Approval/credited/without-evaluations cannot exceed enrolled (same semester).
     - Suggested constraints:
       - .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral) (error)
       - .isNonNegative('<col>') (error)
       - If target is approved/credited/without-evaluations: .isLessThanOrEqualTo('<target>', '<enrolled>') (error)
       - If target is enrolled: .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<enrolled>', '<approved>'), etc. (error)
   - Grades:
     - “Curricular units [1st|2nd] sem (grade)” are numeric and bounded in [0, 20].
     - Suggested constraints:
       - .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.Fractional) (error)
       - .hasMin('<col>', lambda x: x >= 0.0) (error)
       - .hasMax('<col>', lambda x: x <= 20.0) (error)
   - Binary indicator columns (e.g., Debtor, Tuition fees up to date, Scholarship holder, attendance):
     - Integers 0/1. If the target_column is one of these, use:
       - .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral) (error)
       - .isContainedIn('<col>', [0, 1]) (error)
   - Macro features in scripts sometimes use fixed allowed sets (e.g., GDP/unemployment/inflation enumerations). If the target is one of these, enforce:
       - .isContainedIn('<col>', [allowed_values]) (error)

4) Levels:
   - Use "error" for strict invariants: completeness=1.0, data type, non-negativity for counts, bounded grade range, categorical allowed sets, and logical cross-column inequalities (approved <= enrolled, etc.).
   - Use "warning" for statistical/distributional expectations or soft thresholds: approximate distinct counts, completeness < 1.0 thresholds, correlations, quantiles, min/max when not strictly guaranteed by domain.
   - For satisfies with SQL-like conditions, use backticks around column names with spaces or special chars, e.g.:
     .satisfies('`colA` <= `colB`', 'colA_le_colB', lambda x: x >= 0.99)

5) Referencing assumptions:
   - For each constraint, include the indices of relevant assumptions in "linked assumptions".
   - If a constraint is implied by both the profile and code usage, include all applicable assumption indices.
   - If a constraint is strongly implied by code usage (e.g., approved <= enrolled) but not explicitly stated in assumptions, still include it; link to the assumption(s) that mention the usage or logical relationship.

6) Implementation details:
   - The "suggestion" must be a string that starts with "." so it can be chained on a Check object (e.g., Check(...).isComplete(...).isNonNegative(...)).
   - Use exact column names as they appear (including spaces, parentheses, or tabs). Wrap them in single quotes inside the Python string.
   - For satisfies(), always wrap column names in backticks in the SQL expression.
   - For regexes in hasPattern(), use raw strings r"...".
   - Prefer ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral for integer counts, ConstrainableDataTypes.Fractional for grades/floats, and ConstrainableDataTypes.Numeric only when either int or float is acceptable.
   - Avoid analyzers that require nonstandard setup unless necessary; if you use hasNumberOfDistinctValues, keep it as a "warning" with a reasonable band.

7) Quantity and coverage:
   - Aim to cover all key invariants evidenced by the assumptions and code usage. That typically includes at least:
     - completeness (or completeness threshold)
     - data type
     - non-negativity for counts
     - domain ranges for bounded metrics (e.g., grades)
     - critical cross-column inequalities (approved/credited/without-evaluations vs enrolled)
     - optional distributional checks (distinct count band, correlation if used)
   - Do not add unrelated constraints.

Examples of valid suggestions formatting:
- ".isComplete('Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)')"
- ".hasDataType('Curricular units 2nd sem (grade)', ConstrainableDataTypes.Fractional)"
- ".isNonNegative('Curricular units 1st sem (approved)')"
- ".isLessThanOrEqualTo('Curricular units 1st sem (approved)', 'Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)')"
- ".hasCorrelation('Curricular units 1st sem (approved)', 'Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)', lambda x: x >= 0.6)"
- ".hasNumberOfDistinctValues('Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)', lambda x: x >= 20 and x <= 30)"
- ".hasMin('Curricular units 2nd sem (grade)', lambda x: x >= 0.0)"
- ".hasMax('Curricular units 2nd sem (grade)', lambda x: x <= 20.0)"
- ".satisfies('`Curricular units 1st sem (approved)` <= `Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)`', 'ap1_le_en1', lambda x: x >= 0.99)"

Return only the list of constraint dicts.

2026/01/14 11:44:05 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 13: Proposed new text for dataflow_inspector: Task: Identify all line ranges in a Python code snippet where a specified DataFrame column (target_column) is used.

Inputs
- code_script: Full Python source as a single string; use exact 1-based line numbers from this snippet.
- target_column: Exact column name to track (case- and whitespace-sensitive).
- sink_variable: Optional sink to scope the search. If empty, consider the entire snippet. If provided, include only uses of target_column that ultimately affect that sink.

Output
- Return only valid JSON with no extra text:
  { "sources": [ { "start_line": int, "end_line": int }, ... ] }
- Merge adjacent/contiguous lines into one range. Non-adjacent lines must be separate ranges.
- Sort ranges by start_line.

What counts as a “use” of target_column
Include every line (or contiguous multi-line statement) that reads, writes, filters, transforms, merges on, or passes through the target column. Concretely, include lines where:
1) The target column is read or written:
   - df['Target'], df[target_alias], df.loc[:, 'Target'], df.loc[mask, 'Target'], df.at[idx, 'Target'].
   - Assignment to or from the column: df['Target'] = ..., some_series = df['Target'].
   - Type/coercion ops on the column: df['Target'].astype(...), pd.to_numeric(df['Target'], ...), df['Target'].map(...), replace/fillna on df['Target'], comparisons, arithmetic, value_counts(), mean(), isin(), quantile() when applied to the Series of that column.
   - Groupby, aggregation, or sorting when the column participates: df.groupby('Target'), sort_values(by=['Target']), agg with column reference, etc.
2) The column participates in joins/reshaping:
   - merge/join/concat/melt/pivot where target_column is used in on/by/left_on/right_on/id_vars/value_vars/columns parameters.
3) The column is passed through selections or schema ops:
   - Column selections that include the target: df[['a', 'Target', 'b']], df.drop(columns=['Target']), reindex(columns=[..., 'Target', ...]), filter(items=[..., 'Target', ...]).
   - rename(columns={... 'Target': new_name ...}) or rename(columns={old_name: 'Target'}) – include both the dict creation if it contains the target and the call applying it.
   - Building a column list/container that includes the target and is then used as a columns-related argument (e.g., later used in df[[cols]], drop(columns=cols), sort_values(by=cols), merge(on=cols), groupby(cols), etc.). This “passes through” the target.
4) Downstream effects:
   - If an intermediate variable (e.g., grad_ratio = (df['Target'] == 'Graduate').mean()) is computed from the target and later used to influence other variables or thresholds, include the lines where the target is referenced (not every downstream use of the derived variable).

What does NOT count
- Do not include mere alias/constant definitions that only bind names to strings, e.g., target_alias = 'Target', unless the same line also indexes the DataFrame with it. Alias definitions by themselves are not a use.
- Do not include unrelated constants or lookup structures that do not touch the DataFrame column values (e.g., allowed value lists) unless they are defined as part of a columns-related container later used to select/rename/sort/merge by the target column.
- Do not include file I/O lines (to_csv, json dump) unless the line itself selects columns including the target (e.g., out[['Target', ...]].to_csv(...)). Prefer the selection line over the write call if they are separate.
- Do not include comments or blank lines.

Alias and indirection handling
- Track aliases: if a variable is assigned exactly to 'Target' (or chains of such assignments), treat later df[that_alias] or inclusion of that_alias in columns-related containers as uses. Do not count the alias definition line itself.
- Track containers that include the target (directly or via alias) when they are later used as columns arguments (by, on, columns, subset, items, keys, left_on, right_on, id_vars, value_vars, index, columns= in pivot, etc.). Include:
  - The line (or multi-line block) where the container including the target is created.
  - The line where the container is applied to a DataFrame/Pandas op.
- Treat exact string matches. Column names may include spaces or special characters (e.g., 'Daytime/evening attendance\t'); match exactly as in code.

Multi-line statements
- If a single logical statement spans multiple lines due to parentheses/brackets/braces, include the full contiguous block if any part of that statement uses the target (e.g., a multi-line list that includes the target, or a multi-line expression operating on df['Target']).

Sink scoping (when sink_variable is provided)
- Only include uses of target_column that contribute to the specified sink via dataflow.
- Identify the sink:
  - If sink_variable is a Python variable (e.g., risk_score), find assignments that define it (risk_score = ...).
  - If sink_variable names a DataFrame column (e.g., output['risk_score'] or df['risk_score']), treat writes to that column as the sink.
- Perform a backward slice (name-based dependency):
  - From the sink-defining assignment(s), collect RHS variables/Series used. Recursively expand to earlier assignments to those names, until reaching lines that reference the target column.
  - Include only the lines where the target is directly referenced (as defined above). Do not include intermediate lines that do not touch the target unless they are the same multi-line statement.
- If no dependency path from target_column to sink_variable exists, return an empty list.

Edge cases and examples of valid “uses” to include
- Reads/filters on target values: (df['Target'] == 'Graduate').mean(), df[target_alias].value_counts(normalize=True), df[target_alias].isin([...]).
- Transform/mapping: df['Target'].map(mapper), df[target_alias].astype(int), pd.to_numeric(df[target_alias], errors='coerce').
- Join/group/sort: df.merge(other, on='Target'), df.groupby(['Target', ...]).agg(...), df.sort_values(by=['Target']).
- Pass-through selections: cols = ['risk', 'Target']; out = df[cols]; out[['Target', ...]], df.drop(columns=['Target']).
- Rename involving the target: mapping = {'Target': 'Label'}; df = df.rename(columns=mapping).

Procedure
1) Split code_script into 1-based lines.
2) Resolve aliases/transitive aliases for target_column.
3) Scan for uses as defined above, tracking:
   - Direct df['Target'] or df[target_alias].
   - Columns-related arguments that include the target (directly or via container/alias).
   - Container creation lines that include the target and are later used as columns-related arguments.
4) If sink_variable is provided, restrict to uses that influence the sink via backward name-based dependency from the sink’s defining assignment(s).
5) Expand to full multi-line statements where applicable and merge adjacent lines into ranges.
6) Return JSON with "sources": [ { "start_line": x, "end_line": y }, ... ]. If none, return {"sources": []}.

Notes drawn from common patterns in similar scripts
- Column usage often appears via:
  - Distribution/priors: df['Target'].value_counts().get('Graduate', 0.0).
  - Boolean rates: (df['Target'] == 'Graduate').mean().
  - Type coercions and numeric ops on the Series.
  - Rank/mapping workflows that start from df['GDP'].map(rank_dict) then use the derived index; include only the line where df['GDP'] (or alias) is mapped/coerced, not subsequent lines that only use the derived index.
  - Output selections that explicitly include the target in the column list.
- Avoid counting pure alias definitions and pure I/O calls as uses unless they directly reference the target in a selection on that line.

Output strictly as valid JSON; no commentary, code fences, or extra fields.

2026/01/14 11:44:21 INFO dspy.evaluate.evaluate: Average Metric: 0.4444444444444445 / 3 (14.8%)
2026/01/14 11:46:36 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 14: Proposed new text for dataflow_inspector: You are given a code snippet and must identify all line ranges where a specific data column is used, directly or indirectly. Return only valid JSON with exact 1-based line numbers. Follow these rules precisely.

Inputs
- code_script: Full Python code snippet (typically pandas/numpy data pipeline code).
- target_column: The exact column name string to track (may include spaces, parentheses, unusual characters, or trailing tabs like "Daytime/evening attendance\t").
- sink_variable: Optional. If provided (non-empty), include only lines that ultimately affect this sink. If empty, consider the entire snippet.

Output
- Return only valid JSON: { "sources": [{ "start_line": int, "end_line": int }, ... ] }
- No extra keys, text, or formatting.

Core task
Identify and return every line range that reads, writes, filters, transforms, merges, or passes through the target column anywhere in the snippet. If sink_variable is provided, include only lines that are on any dependency path to that sink.

Detailed guidance

1) Exact line numbers
- Use 1-based line numbers based on the given snippet.
- Count blank lines and comments in the numbering.
- Merge only truly adjacent (consecutive) line numbers into one range. If there is any gap (including a blank line or comment line not included), start a new range.

2) What counts as “use” of the target column
Include a line if it does any of the following with the target column (or its aliases/renamed forms/derived variables):
- Defines an alias variable bound to the exact target column string. Example:
  c1_woe = 'Curricular units 1st sem (without evaluations)'
- Accesses the column via DataFrame selection or methods:
  - df['Target Name'] or df[target_alias]
  - df.loc[:, 'Target Name'] / df.loc[rows, target_alias]
  - df[[..., 'Target Name', ...]] (column subset)
  - df.filter([...]) when the list contains the target
  - Any method that takes the column name: rename, drop, assign, groupby(by=...), merge(on=...), join(on=...), map/apply when applied to that Series, astype, to_numeric, fillna/replace on that Series, arithmetic/boolean ops on that Series, comparisons, isin, clip, round, etc.
- Uses the column inside loops or comprehensions indirectly:
  - If the target alias/name is present in a list like num_cols = [..., target_alias, ...], then lines in a loop body that operate on df[col] (or equivalent) are considered uses because the iteration includes the target. Mark:
    - The list definition line (membership establishes the pathway).
    - The for-line (e.g., for col in num_cols:) where applicable.
    - The loop body line(s) that use df[col] or operations on col (since it will run for the target).
- Renames and continues lineage:
  - If df.rename(columns={'Target': 'NewName'}) occurs, treat 'NewName' as a new alias for the same column and continue tracking uses via that new name.
- Derived variables (pass-through/lineage):
  - If a variable is computed directly from the target column (or an alias), include the line that computes it, and add that variable to the tracked set.
  - If another variable is computed from a tracked variable, include that line and add the new variable to the tracked set.
  - Continue this transitive dependency tracking (data lineage) through all subsequent computations, aggregations, combinations, and uses, until materialization (e.g., saving to CSV) or function outputs. This means “passes through” requires including lines that use variables derived from the target, even if the original column name no longer appears.
  - Examples to include along the lineage: ratios using the Series, intermediate features, weighted combinations, risk/score aggregations, thresholds, flags, tiers, and any downstream variable that depends (directly or indirectly) on the target-derived variables.
- Output/persistence lines:
  - If a tracked variable (or the target column itself) is written to disk, selected for output, or otherwise emitted (to_csv, json dump via dict that uses derived metrics), include those lines because the target’s influence passes through.
  - If a column selection (e.g., out = df[[..., target_alias, ...]]) includes the target, include that selection line.
- Control and structural lines:
  - If a for-line (e.g., for col in num_cols:) or with-line is essential for applying an operation to the target via iteration (see loops above), include it.
- Do NOT include:
  - Pure comments or docstrings, even if they mention the string.
  - Import statements.
  - Variable definitions unrelated to the target.
  - Entire contiguous blocks unless each line in the block qualifies; only include lines that actually interact with the target, its aliases, or its derived variables.

3) Aliases and tracking strategy (strongly recommended)
Implement a simple static dataflow/lineage procedure:
- Alias discovery:
  - If a line assigns a variable to the literal target_column string (e.g., col_en1 = 'Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)'), add that variable to the alias set.
  - If a rename maps the target to a new name, add the new name to the alias set.
- Direct uses:
  - Mark lines that use df['literal target'] or df[any_alias] or any operation/function that directly references those.
- Dependency graph:
  - Maintain a set of tracked variables: start with column aliases and any Series/arrays created from the column.
  - For each assignment, if the RHS references any tracked variable, mark the line and add the LHS variable to the tracked set.
  - Propagate transitively (A from target -> B from A -> C from B, etc.).
  - Also mark lines where tracked variables are further used (e.g., in arithmetic, conditionals, function calls, selections, I/O).
- Loops over collections:
  - If the target alias/name is in a list or dict used to drive operations over columns (e.g., num_cols, selected columns for output), mark that definition and the loop body lines that operate on the iterated item (df[col], coercions, etc.), as they will affect the target.

4) Sink variable filtering (when sink_variable is provided)
- The sink_variable can be:
  - A scalar variable (e.g., risk_score), or
  - A DataFrame column name (e.g., df['risk_score'] or assigned via df['risk_score'] = ...), or
  - A named output field used in to_csv/JSON if directly tied to a tracked variable.
- When sink_variable is non-empty:
  - Build/trace the dependency paths from the target column to the sink_variable.
  - Include only lines that are on any transitive path influencing the sink_variable (from target through intermediate variables to the sink).
  - Exclude target-related lines that do not contribute to the sink.
- When sink_variable is empty:
  - Include all lines influenced by the target anywhere in the snippet (full lineage).

5) Domain-specific patterns commonly seen in these scripts (include them when applicable)
- Student/academic dataset columns commonly present:
  - 'Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled/approved/credited/without evaluations/grade)'
  - 'Curricular units 2nd sem (enrolled/approved/credited/without evaluations/grade)'
  - 'Debtor', 'Tuition fees up to date', 'Admission grade', 'Previous qualification (grade)'
  - 'Daytime/evening attendance\t' (note trailing tab)
  - 'Target' (e.g., Graduate/Dropout/Enrolled)
  - 'GDP', 'Unemployment rate', 'Inflation rate'
  - 'Application order', 'Age at enrollment'
- Typical operations indicating use:
  - pd.to_numeric/astype on df[column]
  - Replace/fillna/clip/arithmetic on series from the column
  - Rates/ratios using the column (e.g., no_eval_ratio1 = df[c1_woe] / df[c1_enr])
  - Aggregations/compositions feeding risk_score/priority/tiers/flags
  - Membership in column lists for conversion or output selection
  - Using the column in merge/join/groupby keys
  - pd.cut, value_counts, map, corr, std/mean when applied to series derived from the column

6) Precision requirements
- Do not return overly broad ranges that include unrelated lines. Only include the exact lines that meet the criteria above.
- If multiple relevant lines are consecutive, merge them into a single range. If there is any gap, use separate ranges.
- If a single line contains multiple relevant uses, include it once.

7) Output format
- Return only: { "sources": [{ "start_line": int, "end_line": int }, ... ] }
- start_line <= end_line
- No other text or formatting.

Summary checklist
- Track literal target name and all aliases/renames.
- Mark direct column access and all derived computations (transitive lineage).
- Include loop-driven uses where the target is in the iterated collection.
- Include downstream uses and outputs unless a sink is provided; if sink provided, include only paths to the sink.
- Merge adjacent lines; keep numbering 1-based.
