^[2026/01/14 11:48:27 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 15: Proposed new text for assumption_generation: You are given:
- target_column: The exact column name to focus on.
- target_column_desc: A structured summary for that column (completeness, dataType, approximateNumDistinctValues, histogram, etc.).
- focused_code: A code snippet with line numbers, showing how columns are used downstream.
- downstream_task_description: Free-text description (often empty; do not rely on it).

Your task:
Produce a JSON object with key "assumptions" whose value is a list of objects:
- Each object: { "text": str, "sources": [{ "start_line": int, "end_line": int }] }
- sources is a list of zero or more source ranges from focused_code (inclusive).
- If an assumption is supported only by target_column_desc (metadata) and not by focused_code, provide an empty sources list [].
- Output only the JSON object. Do not include extra text. JSON numbers must not have leading zeros.

What to extract and how:
1) Always read target_column_desc and turn its facts into explicit assumptions:
   - Completeness: If completeness == 1.0, add an assumption that the column has no missing values. If not 1.0, state the observed completeness.
   - Data type: Add the declared dataType (e.g., Integral) and whether isDataTypeInferred is true/false.
   - Distinct values: Include approximateNumDistinctValues if provided.
   - Histogram: If present, summarize observed values and their proportions; for binary-like columns, infer allowed set (e.g., values in {0,1}) and the empirical distribution.
   - These assumptions come from target_column_desc; set sources: [].

2) Map the target column name to its usage in focused_code:
   - Identify where the string column name is assigned to a variable and/or referenced in df[...] (e.g., scholar_col = 'Scholarship holder'; scholar = df[scholar_col].astype(int)).
   - For each usage, extract constraints implied by the code and cite exact line ranges:
     - Type conversions (astype(int/float), to_numeric(...).astype(int), etc.) imply numeric constraints and should be included as assumptions (sources = conversion lines).
     - Arithmetic/division and clipping imply ranges. For example, if the target column is a denominator in a ratio with .clip(0.0, 1.0) or replace(0, np.nan), note the expectation of non-negative counts and that ratios are bounded [0,1] (sources = those lines).
     - Indexing into arrays/vectors using the column (e.g., weights[app_order]) implies valid index ranges. If comments or code indicate expected ranges (e.g., 1..9; “relies on 1..9”), include a range constraint assumption (sources = comment/line range around the indexing).
     - Comparisons and logical conditions imply value sets (e.g., app.isin([1,2,3,4,5,6]) suggests allowable subset; equality checks against 1 imply binary or special sentinel values). Capture these as assumptions about allowed values or common values (sources = those lines).
     - Any subsequent clipping to [0,1] of a function using the target column may imply its contribution is normalized; include only if it constrains the target column’s expected range/usage.
   - Keep sources minimal and precise: cite the tightest line range that supports the assumption.

3) Prioritize assumptions that are commonly validated downstream:
   - isComplete (completeness == 1.0).
   - hasDataType (e.g., Integral).
   - Valid value ranges or sets inferred from histogram and code usage (e.g., binary {0,1}, integer counts >= 0, Application order in 1..9).
   - Where applicable, mention that code expects integer semantics even if the dataset is integral (due to astype(int)).

4) Keep assumptions atomic and directly tied to the target column:
   - Do not include statements about other columns unless they impose a constraint on the target column through an interaction (e.g., target column used in a condition that restricts its values).
   - Avoid speculative modeling claims. Only state what is supported by target_column_desc and/or focused_code.

5) Domain specifics from the provided examples that you should apply when they match the target column:
   - Scholarship holder:
     - Typically a 0/1 integral indicator; code often casts to int and uses it in conditional adjustments (e.g., bonuses applied only when Tuition fees up to date == 1). Cite the cast and conditional lines for usage; distribution and binary nature come from target_column_desc.
   - Application order:
     - Integral with values typically in 1..9; 1 denotes first choice. Code may assume 1-based indexes (comments like “relies on 1..9”) and may check membership in [1..6] for cohort cohesion. Include the valid range (from histogram) and usage constraints (from code). Cite array indexing or isin/clip logic lines.
   - Curricular units ... (enrolled):
     - Integral non-negative counts; cast to int; commonly used as denominators to compute ratios (pass rates, no-evaluation ratios) and in linear relationships with approvals. Note non-negativity and integer nature (from code), and any ratio construction details. Cite the int cast and ratio lines.

6) Formatting and quality:
   - Provide 5–12 concise assumptions that cover metadata constraints and key code-implied constraints for the target column.
   - De-duplicate overlapping assumptions.
   - Ensure sources contain valid integer line numbers from focused_code and are present only when supported by code; otherwise, use [].
   - Return exactly: { "assumptions": [ ... ] } with no extra keys or commentary.

Example patterns to emulate (adjust to the given inputs):
- From metadata (no code source):
  { "text": "The column is complete with no missing values (completeness = 1.0).", "sources": [] }
  { "text": "Data type is Integral and is not inferred (isDataTypeInferred = false).", "sources": [] }

- From code (with precise line spans):
  { "text": "The column is cast to integer before use.", "sources": [{ "start_line": 69, "end_line": 71 }] }

- target_column: the exact name of a single column to focus on.
- target_column_desc: a short schema/summary with fields like completeness, approximateNumDistinctValues, dataType, isDataTypeInferred, and optionally a histogram of values.
- focused_code: a numbered Python code snippet (with line numbers) that may reference and use this column.
- downstream_task_description: a brief task note (may be empty).

Your task:
Produce a JSON object of the form:
{ "assumptions": [ { "text": str, "sources": [ { "start_line": int, "end_line": int } ] }, ... ] }
Rules:
- Return only valid JSON. Do not include extra text.
- JSON numbers must not have leading zeros.
- sources is a list of zero or more objects with start_line/end_line integers pointing to evidence lines in focused_code.
- If an assumption is based only on target_column_desc (metadata) and not on the code, use an empty sources list [] for that assumption.
- If an assumption is supported by the code, include the smallest precise line span(s) that show the evidence.

General approach:
1) Read target_column_desc verbatim to extract schema-level facts:
   - completeness: If completeness == 1.0, add an assumption using canonical phrasing:
     "Column '<target_column>' is complete (no missing values)."
     If completeness < 1.0, state that missing values may be present and include the given completeness value.
   - dataType: Add an assumption using canonical phrasing:
     "Column '<target_column>' has data type <dataType>."
     Use the exact dataType token from target_column_desc (e.g., Integral, Fractional, String).
   - approximateNumDistinctValues: If present, add:
     "Column '<target_column>' has approximately <approximateNumDistinctValues> distinct values."
   - histogram: If present, add concise distribution insights grounded in the histogram (e.g., dominant values, binary 0/1, majority zeros). Do not speculate beyond what the histogram shows.
   - For all metadata-derived assumptions, leave sources: [].

2) Analyze focused_code to capture usage-level assumptions specifically about target_column:
   - Identify where the code defines a variable equal to the exact column name (e.g., c2_woe = 'Curricular units 2nd sem (without evaluations)'). Use that line as a source to anchor that the code references the column.
   - If the column appears in a list used for numeric coercion (to_numeric with errors='coerce'), add:
     "Values in '<target_column>' are coerced to numeric for computations."
     Cite the lines that build the list and the loop that applies to_numeric.
   - If the column is used in computations, ratios, conditionals, or as a denominator/numerator, add assumptions describing that role:
     - Denominator handling: If used as a denominator after .replace(0, np.nan), add:
       "Zero values in '<target_column>' are treated as NaN when used as denominators to avoid division by zero."
       Cite the specific replace/denominator lines.
     - Ratio features: If used to compute a ratio (e.g., pass_rate, no_eval_ratio, credit_ratio), describe the ratio definition succinctly and cite the computation lines.
     - Scaling/transform: If values are scaled/normalized (e.g., grade/20.0), describe this and cite the lines.
     - Risk features: If it contributes (directly or via derived features) to academic_risk or risk_score, state this and cite the aggregation/weight lines where applicable.
   - If the column is never referenced in the code, do not invent code-based assumptions.

3) Domain/contextual details to include when supported by the code:
   - The script coerces selected columns to numeric via pandas.to_numeric(errors='coerce').
   - For enrolled counts used as denominators, zeros are replaced with NaN before ratio calculations.
   - Academic risk is aggregated as:
     0.40 * fail_rate_avg + 0.30 * no_eval_avg + 0.20 * grade_risk_avg + 0.10 * credit_gap_avg.
   - Overall risk_score combines:
     0.50 * academic_risk + 0.30 * finance_risk + 0.10 * prep_risk + 0.05 * attendance_risk + 0.05 * risk_age.
   - Thresholding and tiers are derived from the risk_score distribution and graduate ratio.
   Include these only if the target_column participates in the relevant intermediate features or if citing these lines clarifies how the target_column’s derived metrics affect risk.

4) Quality and phrasing requirements:
   - Use the exact target_column name in each assumption.
   - Keep assumptions atomic and specific; avoid bundling unrelated facts.
   - Prefer canonical phrasing for common constraints to aid validation:
     - "Column '<target_column>' is complete (no missing values)." when completeness == 1.0.
     - "Column '<target_column>' has data type <dataType>."
   - Do not speculate beyond metadata or code. Do not infer ranges not shown in histogram or code.
   - De-duplicate overlapping assumptions.

What to prioritize:
- Always include the completeness and data type assumptions from target_column_desc using the canonical phrasing above.
- If histogram shows binary 0/1 or dominant zeros, add a concise distribution assumption.
- If the code uses the column in numeric coercion or computations, include those usage assumptions with precise line citations.

Output format example (structure only; content will differ):
{
  "assumptions": [
    { "text": "Column '<target_column>' is complete (no missing values).", "sources": [] },
    { "text": "Column '<target_column>' has data type Integral.", "sources": [] },

    { "text": "Zero values in '<target_column>' are treated as NaN when used as denominators to avoid division by zero.", "sources": [ { "start_line": 52, "end_line": 53 } ] }
  ]
}

2026/01/14 11:52:28 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 17: Proposed new text for ir_generation: Task: Given a single target column and context, generate PyDeequ validation constraints that are specific, chainable, and justified by the provided assumptions and code usage.

Inputs you will receive:
- target_column (exact column name)
- target_column_desc (profile with: completeness, approximateNumDistinctValues, dataType)
- code_snippet (how the column is used downstream)
- downstream_task_description (optional)
- assumptions (indexed list; you must cite indices in “linked assumptions”)

Required output:
- Return only a Python list of dicts. Each dict must contain:
  - "suggestion": a string starting with "." that is a valid PyDeequ Check-builder call (chainable), or a Check function when applicable. Use exact function names.
  - "level": "error" or "warning"
  - "linked assumptions": [list of integer indices] that justify the constraint
- Do not return any prose/explanation outside of the list.

Available/commonly used PyDeequ functions and constants:
- .isComplete('<col>')
- .hasCompleteness('<col>', lambda x: x >= <threshold>)
- .areComplete(['<colA>', '<colB>', ...])
- .haveCompleteness(['<colA>', '<colB>', ...], lambda x: x >= <threshold>)
- .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.<Integral|Fractional|Boolean|String|Numeric>)
- .isNonNegative('<col>')
- .isPositive('<col>')  [use sparingly; see guidance]
- .hasMin('<col>', lambda x: x >= <value>)
- .hasMax('<col>', lambda x: x <= <value>)
- .isLessThanOrEqualTo('<colA>', '<colB>')
- .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<colA>', '<colB>')
- .hasCorrelation('<colA>', '<colB>', lambda x: x >= <threshold>)
- .hasNumberOfDistinctValues('<col>', lambda x: x >= <lower> and x <= <upper>)
- .isContainedIn('<col>', [allowed_values])
- .satisfies('<sql_condition>', '<rule_name>', lambda x: x >= <threshold>)
  - For satisfies(), wrap column names with spaces/special chars in backticks.

General strategy:
1) Start from the target_column_desc (profile) to set base constraints:
   - Completeness:
     - If completeness == 1.0, add .isComplete('<col>') with level "error".
     - If completeness < 1.0 or not guaranteed, add .hasCompleteness('<col>', lambda x: x >= threshold) with level "warning". Use threshold from the description if given, else 0.95 by default.
   - Data type:
     - Map profile dataType to ConstrainableDataTypes: Integral, Fractional, Boolean, String, or Numeric.
     - Add .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.<type>) with level "error".
     - If the code forces numeric (e.g., pd.to_numeric) and the column is used for arithmetic, prefer Integral for counts and Fractional for grades; use Numeric only if both int and float are acceptable.
   - Approximate distinctness:
     - When approximateNumDistinctValues is present, add a banded distinct-count check as "warning":
       - Compute delta = max(int(round(0.2 * approx)), 5); lower = max(1, approx - delta); upper = approx + delta.
       - .hasNumberOfDistinctValues('<col>', lambda x: x >= lower and x <= upper)
       - Avoid equality checks on distinct counts.

2) Analyze code_snippet to derive additional constraints:
   - Casting/conversions:
     - If the column is cast or coerced to numeric and used in integer-like operations, keep Integral as data type.
     - If used in fractional arithmetic (e.g., division by 20.0), use Fractional.
   - Arithmetic and bounds from usage:
     - If the code normalizes or assumes bounded domains, enforce those ranges:
       - For numeric ratios expected in [0, 1], use:
         .hasMin('<col>', lambda x: x >= 0.0) and .hasMax('<col>', lambda x: x <= 1.0) as "warning" unless it’s a strict invariant.
       - For known domain bounds (e.g., grades 0..20), add:
         .hasMin('<col>', lambda x: x >= 0.0) and .hasMax('<col>', lambda x: x <= 20.0) as "error".
   - Non-negativity and positivity:
     - For count-like columns (enrolled/approved/credited/without evaluations), add:
       .isNonNegative('<col>') with level "error".
     - If the code replaces zeros in a denominator with NaN to avoid division-by-zero, treat zero as allowable; do NOT force strictly positive.
   - Cross-column logical constraints:
     - If the target is “approved”, “credited”, or “without evaluations” for a given semester, enforce it cannot exceed “enrolled” for the same semester:
       .isLessThanOrEqualTo('<target>', '<enrolled>') (error).
     - If the target is “enrolled”, enforce it is >= each of “approved”, “credited”, and “without evaluations” for the same semester:
       .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<enrolled>', '<approved>'), etc. (error).
     - If you need a soft, near-always invariant across columns, you may use satisfies with a high coverage (e.g., 0.99) and name the rule; still prefer dedicated comparators when the relationship is strict.
   - Enumerations/allowed sets:
     - If code uses .isin([...]) or equality to constants (e.g., binary indicators), enforce:
       .isContainedIn('<col>', [allowed_values]) (error).
   - Columns used together:
     - When the target is used jointly with other columns (e.g., dividing “approved/credited/without evaluations” by “enrolled”), add a multi-column completeness check:
       - If all involved columns have completeness 1.0 in their profiles, use .areComplete([...]) (error).
       - Otherwise use .haveCompleteness([...], lambda x: x >= 0.95) (warning).
   - Correlations:
     - Only add .hasCorrelation when the code or assumptions explicitly expect correlation (e.g., approvals vs enrolled). Use a threshold like 0.6 and level "warning" unless marked as a strict precondition.

3) Student dataset domain defaults (use only when consistent with assumptions and code):
   - Semester counts:
     - Columns named “Curricular units [1st|2nd] sem (enrolled|approved|credited|without evaluations)” are non-negative integers.
     - For the same semester:
       - approved <= enrolled
       - credited <= enrolled
       - without evaluations <= enrolled
     - Suggested constraints:
       - .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral) (error)
       - .isNonNegative('<col>') (error)
       - If target is approved/credited/without-evaluations: .isLessThanOrEqualTo('<target>', '<enrolled>') (error)
       - If target is enrolled: .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<enrolled>', '<approved>'), etc. (error)
   - Grades:
     - “Curricular units [1st|2nd] sem (grade)” are numeric on [0, 20].
     - Suggested constraints:
       - .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.Fractional) (error)
       - .hasMin('<col>', lambda x: x >= 0.0) (error)
       - .hasMax('<col>', lambda x: x <= 20.0) (error)
   - Binary indicator columns (Debtor, Tuition fees up to date, Scholarship holder, Daytime/evening attendance with trailing tab in the name):
     - Integers 0/1:
       - .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral) (error)
       - .isContainedIn('<col>', [0, 1]) (error)
   - Target label columns used in risk/alert logic may be categorical (e.g., 'Graduate'); only enforce allowed sets if clearly enumerated in code or assumptions.

4) Levels (severity):
   - Use "error" for strict invariants: completeness=1.0, data type, non-negativity for counts, grade range [0, 20], categorical allowed sets, and strict cross-column inequalities (approved/credited/without-evaluations <= enrolled; enrolled >= others).
   - Use "warning" for soft/statistical expectations: approximate distinct counts, completeness < 1.0 thresholds, correlations, quantile expectations, min/max for typical-but-not-strict ranges (e.g., Age at enrollment 17..45 from code-based heuristics).
   - For satisfies() with SQL-like conditions, use backticks in column names and a coverage threshold (e.g., lambda x: x >= 0.99) when the rule is near-always.

5) Implementation details and formatting:
   - The "suggestion" must be a string starting with "." to be chainable on a Check object: e.g., ".isComplete('Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)')".
   - Use exact column names as provided (including spaces, parentheses, or trailing tab characters). Wrap names in single quotes inside the Python string.
   - For satisfies(), always wrap column names in backticks in the SQL expression.
   - Ensure any .hasMin/.hasMax are compatible with numeric data; include the .hasDataType constraint so numeric analyzers can run reliably.
   - Keep .hasNumberOfDistinctValues as "warning" with a reasonable band; never enforce exact equality.

6) Quantity and coverage:
   - Aim to include at least:
     - completeness or a completeness threshold
     - data type
     - one core domain invariant (non-negativity for counts, bounds for grades, allowed set for binaries)
   - Add critical cross-column inequalities when the target interacts with its companion columns (enrolled vs approved/credited/without evaluations).
   - Add multi-column completeness when the target is used jointly with other columns (e.g., in ratios).
   - Add distinct-count band when the profile provides approximateNumDistinctValues.
   - Do not add unrelated constraints. Avoid duplicates of the same rule.

7) Assumptions linkage:
   - For every constraint, include the indices of the assumptions that justify it in "linked assumptions".
   - If a constraint is supported by both the profile and code usage, include all relevant indices.
   - If a rule is strongly implied by code logic (e.g., approved <= enrolled), include it even if not explicitly stated; link to the assumptions that reference that usage or logical relationship.

Examples of valid suggestion formatting:
- ".isComplete('Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)')"
- ".hasDataType('Curricular units 2nd sem (grade)', ConstrainableDataTypes.Fractional)"
- ".isNonNegative('Curricular units 1st sem (approved)')"
- ".isLessThanOrEqualTo('Curricular units 1st sem (without evaluations)', 'Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)')"
- ".hasCorrelation('Curricular units 1st sem (approved)', 'Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)', lambda x: x >= 0.6)"
- ".hasNumberOfDistinctValues('Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)', lambda x: x >= 20 and x <= 30)"
- ".hasMin('Curricular units 2nd sem (grade)', lambda x: x >= 0.0)"
- ".hasMax('Curricular units 2nd sem (grade)', lambda x: x <= 20.0)"
- ".satisfies('`Curricular units 1st sem (approved)` <= `Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)`', 'ap1_le_en1', lambda x: x >= 0.99)"

2026/01/14 11:54:54 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 18: Proposed new text for dataflow_inspector: You are given:
- code_script: a full Python code snippet (often using pandas/numpy).
- target_column: an exact column name string (e.g., 'Curricular units 2nd sem (without evaluations)').
- sink_variable: optional variable name. If empty, consider the entire snippet; if provided, only include lines that ultimately affect this sink.

Your task:
Return only valid JSON of the form:
{ "sources": [{ "start_line": int, "end_line": int }, ... ] }
representing 1-based line ranges in code_script where the target column is read, written, filtered, transformed, merged, selected, or otherwise passed through. Merge adjacent consecutive lines into a single range; non-adjacent lines must be separate ranges. Do not return any other text.

Critical requirements:
1) Exact line numbering
- Use exact 1-based line numbers from code_script, counting every line as shown, including blank lines and comments.
- Include only lines that contain relevant code (see below). Do not include pure comment-only lines unless they are on the same line as a relevant code expression.

2) What to include (read/write/filter/transform/merge/pass-through)
Include any line that:
- Directly accesses the target column in a DataFrame:
  - df['target_column'], df["target_column"]
  - df.loc[:, 'target_column'], df.loc[rows, 'target_column']
  - df[['target_column']], df.filter(items=[...]) containing the target
  - sort_values(by='target_column'), groupby('target_column'), drop(columns=[...]) with the target
  - rename(columns={'target_column': ...}) or rename(columns={alias_var: ...}) when alias maps to target
  - merge/join using the target: merge(on='target_column'), merge(left_on='target_column', ...), join(keys=...) if equals target
- Accesses the target column via an alias variable that equals the target string (see alias handling below).
- Assigns to, computes, filters, or coerces types using the target column, e.g.:
  - pd.to_numeric(df[col], errors='coerce'), astype on df[col], arithmetic on df[col], boolean masks using df[col], np.where using variables derived from df[col].
- Passes through the target column into outputs/selections:
  - Selecting a set of columns that includes the target (e.g., queue[queue_cols] where queue_cols is a list that contains the target or its alias).
  - Writing to CSV with a selection that includes the target.
- Uses variables that are derived from the target column (transitive dataflow). See dataflow propagation below.

3) Alias handling (very common in these scripts)
- Detect alias variables that are assigned exactly to the target column string, e.g.:
  - col_wo2 = 'Curricular units 2nd sem (without evaluations)'
  - c2_woe = 'Curricular units 2nd sem (without evaluations)'
  - g_infl = 'Inflation rate'
- Include the line that defines such an alias (it “passes” the target column name into the program).
- Treat any use of that alias in DataFrame operations as a use of the target column, e.g. df[col_wo2], df.loc[:, c2_woe], merge(on=g_infl), lists like queue_cols = [..., col_wo2, ...], etc.

4) Dataflow propagation (transitive closure)
- Start from all direct uses of the target column (direct string or via alias).
- Mark any variable assigned from those uses as influenced (e.g., wo2 = pd.to_numeric(df[col_wo2], ...)).
- Propagate forward: any line using influenced variables to compute new variables should be included, and those outputs become influenced too. Continue transitively until no new influenced variables are produced.
  - Examples: noeval_ratio2 computed from wo2; mean_noeval_ratio computed from noeval_ratio2; noeval_component from mean_noeval_ratio; risk_raw from noeval_component; risk_score from risk_raw; output assignments/exports using risk_score.
- Pass-through via selection lists:
  - If a list variable includes the alias/target (e.g., queue_cols = [..., col_wo2, ...]), mark that list as influenced.
  - Any later use of that list to select columns (e.g., df[queue_cols], to_csv with df[queue_cols]) must be included as passing through the target column.
- Do not include generic operations that do not read/use the influenced variables or the target column explicitly (e.g., copying a whole DataFrame without referencing an influenced variable or selecting columns that do not include the target).

5) Sink-variable restriction (when sink_variable is provided)
- If sink_variable is non-empty, include only lines that lie on at least one dataflow path from the target column to sink_variable.
  - Perform backward slicing from sink_variable: find lines that define or use sink_variable, then locate variables it depends on, and continue backward until reaching uses of the target column or its alias.
  - Include all intermediate lines on these dependency paths.
- If no path exists from the target to sink_variable, return { "sources": [] }.

6) Typical patterns to capture (common in provided scripts)
- Type coercions: pd.to_numeric(df[alias], errors='coerce'), astype on df[alias].
- Ratios and masks: np.where(en2 > 0, wo2 / en2.replace(0, np.nan), 0.0) where wo2 was derived from target column; boolean expressions involving the column.
- Aggregations/averages: mean_noeval_ratio = (noeval_ratio1 + noeval_ratio2)/2; grade_risk_avg, fail_rate_avg, etc. If the target column influences any of these terms, include the lines.
- Risk composition pipelines: components (e.g., noeval_component, macro_component), risk_raw, risk_score, tiering (pd.cut), flags, and any outputs that use these influenced variables.
- Output selection including the target: lists like queue_cols = [..., alias, ...]; uses like df[queue_cols].to_csv(...).
- Macro features: if target is 'Inflation rate', it often flows through mac_infl -> zi -> macro_stress -> macro_component -> risk_raw -> risk_score -> outputs.

7) What NOT to include
- Lines that do not reference the target column (directly by string or via alias) and are not on a dependency path via influenced variables or pass-through lists.
- Pure comments or blank lines.
- Unrelated variable definitions and outputs that do not depend on the dataflow from the target.

8) Range merging
- After collecting all relevant line indices, sort them and merge only strictly consecutive lines into a single range:
  - Example: lines [10, 11, 12, 15] become ranges [{start_line: 10, end_line: 12}, {start_line: 15, end_line: 15}].
- Output ranges must be sorted by start_line.

9) Output format
- Return only JSON with key "sources" and an array of objects { "start_line": int, "end_line": int }.
- If there are no relevant lines, return { "sources": [] }.
- Do not include any explanatory text.

Practical checklist to improve recall (based on common misses):
- Include the alias definition line for the target column string.
- Include all direct df[...] uses of the alias/target.
- Propagate through every intermediate variable (ratios, components, aggregations) up to final outputs.
- If lists include the alias/target and are used to select columns, include both the list construction and the selection/export lines.
- For sink_variable, include only the path(s) from the target to the sink.

Follow these rules precisely to avoid missing downstream lines (a common cause of low F1 in evaluations).

2026/01/14 11:57:31 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 19: Proposed new text for ir_generation: You are given a single “target column” and supporting context. Your job is to generate PyDeequ validation constraints that enforce the assumptions and usage requirements for that specific column.

Input format
- target_column: string, the column to validate.
- target_column_desc: profile of the target column with fields like completeness, approximateNumDistinctValues, dataType, histogram, etc.
- code_snippet: code that uses the dataset and (often) the target column. This frequently reveals strong domain rules (e.g., allowed values, ranges/scales, casting).
- downstream_task_description: description of how the data is used (often empty).
- assumptions: list of numbered assumptions (Assumption 0, 1, …) explicitly describing expectations for the target column. These are your primary source of truth.

Output format
- constraint_code: a list of objects:
  - suggestion: a single PyDeequ call string that starts with "." (e.g., ".isComplete('col')") or is a function call per the API (e.g., ".hasNumberOfDistinctValues('col', lambda x: x == 2, binningUdf=None, maxBins=1000)").
  - level: "error" for hard rules that must hold, "warning" for soft/distribution checks.
  - linked assumptions: list of assumption indices (e.g., [0, 2]) that justify this constraint.

General approach
1) Read the assumptions first. They are authoritative and drive which constraints to include and how strict to make them.
2) Use target_column_desc to convert assumptions into concrete constraints:
   - completeness → isComplete/hasCompleteness
   - dataType → hasDataType(...)
   - approximateNumDistinctValues and histogram → isContainedIn, hasNumberOfDistinctValues, range checks
3) Mine the code_snippet for how the target column is used:
   - Casting (e.g., .astype(int) or to_numeric): implies strict data type constraints.
   - Normalizations or scaling (e.g., grade/20.0): implies concrete min/max bounds.
   - Comparisons to constants (== 0/1, >= 0, <= 1): implies membership or range constraints.
   - Mapping via a fixed allowed set (e.g., mapping from a predefined list): implies isContainedIn for that set.
   - Participation in ratios/denominators → consider non-negativity and realistic bounds if applicable.
4) Translate each justified rule into one or more PyDeequ constraints using the APIs listed below.
5) Severity:
   - error: hard domain rules (completeness required by code logic, strict data type, exact allowed value sets, concrete numeric ranges, known cardinality).
   - warning: soft distribution expectations (means/proportions/quantiles from histograms that may drift), and checks that guard against unexpected shifts.
6) Link each constraint to the most relevant assumptions by index. If a constraint arises both from assumptions and code usage, reference both.
7) Favor value-safe comparisons and tolerances for floating assertions. Avoid exact equality on floats unless justified by assumptions.

What to include by default (to improve coverage/recall)
Based on the dataset patterns in the examples:
- Always include completeness and data type constraints when the assumptions or profile specify them explicitly.
  - Include BOTH a row-level isComplete('col') and an aggregate-level hasCompleteness('col', lambda x: x == 1.0) if completeness is 1.0 and isDataTypeInferred is false; otherwise, use a high-threshold completeness (>= 0.99) as a warning.
- If the column is binary or has a small, known set of allowed values:
  - isContainedIn('col', [allowed_values]) as error.
  - hasMin('col', lambda x: x >= min_allowed) and hasMax('col', lambda x: x <= max_allowed) as error.
  - If non-negative values only: isNonNegative('col') as error.
  - If the distinct count is known/small from assumptions/profile: hasNumberOfDistinctValues('col', lambda x: x == expected_count, binningUdf=None, maxBins=1000) as error. If only approximate, use a bounded range (e.g., >= expected and <= expected + small_slack) or set level to warning.
- If the column is used with a known scale from the code (e.g., grade/20.0):
  - hasMin('col', lambda x: x >= 0.0) and hasMax('col', lambda x: x <= scale_max) as error.
- If assumptions provide a stable distribution (e.g., histogram proportions for a 0/1 indicator):
  - For binary col, the mean equals the share of 1s. Add hasMean('col', lambda x: abs(x - p1) <= tol) as warning, where:
    - p1 is the histogram ratio for value 1 (or the minority/majority class as relevant).
    - tol can be max(0.05, 0.25 * p1) to allow some drift unless assumptions say it is very stable, in which case reduce tol.
  - Optionally, add hasApproxQuantile('col', 0.5, lambda x: x == majority_value) as warning if the histogram indicates a clear majority class and isDataTypeInferred is false; otherwise prefer a relaxed bound (e.g., <= 0.5 for binary 0/1 where 0 is majority).
- If the code compares the column to constants or uses it as a boolean mask (e.g., col == 1 or col == 0):
  - Reinforce domain with isContainedIn([0,1]) and range checks (min/max) as error.
- Only use pattern validators (hasPattern, containsEmail/URL/SSN/credit card) if assumptions or code clearly indicate those formats. Use raw strings for regex: r"...".
- Use backticks around column names with spaces only inside satisfies conditions.

Examples of domain-specific expectations from the provided tasks
- Debtor is a binary integral indicator with values {0, 1}, completeness 1.0, used as int, with a roughly stable proportion of ones around 0.117:
  - Include: isComplete, hasCompleteness == 1.0, hasDataType Integral, isContainedIn [0,1], isNonNegative, hasMin >= 0, hasMax <= 1, hasNumberOfDistinctValues == 2 (error).
  - Add distribution guardrails (warning): hasMean close to 0.117 with tolerance (e.g., abs(x - 0.117) <= 0.05 or relative slack).
- “Curricular units 1st sem (grade)” is Fractional, complete, and scaled 0–20 (used as grade/20.0):
  - Include: isComplete, hasDataType Fractional, hasMin >= 0.0, hasMax <= 20.0 (error).

How to derive constraint details
- Allowed values: From assumptions (preferred) or histogram values in target_column_desc. Cast to the correct type per dataType.
- Distinct count: If approximateNumDistinctValues is small and isDataTypeInferred is false, you may assert equality; otherwise, make it a warning or use a bounded check.
- Range: If dividing by a constant in code (e.g., col/20), enforce [0, constant]. If comparisons in code imply bounds, use those.
- Distribution: If histogram shows clear majority/minority and assumptions say distribution is stable, add mean or quantile checks as warnings with sensible tolerances.

API reminders
- Use the provided PyDeequ functions exactly as documented. Examples:
  - .isComplete('col')
  - .hasCompleteness('col', lambda x: x == 1.0)
  - .hasDataType('col', ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral)  # or Fractional/String/etc.
  - .isContainedIn('col', [0, 1])
  - .isNonNegative('col')
  - .hasMin('col', lambda x: x >= 0.0)
  - .hasMax('col', lambda x: x <= 20.0)
  - .hasNumberOfDistinctValues('col', lambda x: x == 2, binningUdf=None, maxBins=1000)
  - .hasMean('col', lambda x: abs(x - 0.117) <= 0.05)
  - .hasApproxQuantile('col', 0.5, lambda x: x == 0.0)
- For satisfies: use SQL-like conditions and backtick-quote columns with spaces: .satisfies('`a` <= `b`', 'a_le_b')

What NOT to do
- Do not output any code besides the list of constraint objects.
- Do not propose irrelevant validators (e.g., containsEmail/URL/SSN) unless clearly indicated by assumptions/code.
- Do not rely on exact float equality in assertions unless the assumption says it must be exact.
- Do not omit core constraints (completeness, data type, domain membership, min/max) when assumptions/profile make them clear.

Linking constraints to assumptions
- For each constraint, include the indices of the assumptions that justify it. If multiple assumptions apply (e.g., binary 0/1 and completeness), include both indices.

Goal
- Maximize coverage: include all core, domain, and usage-derived constraints for the target column, not just a minimal set. Use error for hard domain guarantees and warning for distribution drift checks. This improves recall in validation scoring while staying faithful to the assumptions and code usage.

Your job:
Produce a JSON object of the form { "assumptions": [ ... ] } where each item is an object:

General requirements:
- Only output the JSON object (no extra commentary, no markdown).
- JSON numbers must not have leading zeros.
- Each assumption must be concise, atomic, and verifiable from either:
  1) the provided target_column_desc, or
  2) direct evidence in focused_code, or
  3) universally accepted domain constraints that are directly implied by the column’s semantics (e.g., counts are non-negative integers).
- Attach sources only for statements supported by focused_code. Use exact 1-based line numbers as shown. If a single line supports the claim, set start_line == end_line. If the claim is supported by multiple disjoint code regions, you may include multiple source ranges in the same assumption by adding multiple {start_line, end_line} entries.
- For statements supported solely by target_column_desc (metadata), set "sources": [].
- Do not fabricate line numbers or use placeholders like 0. Only cite lines that exist in focused_code.

What to include for each target column:
A) Schema-derived assumptions (from target_column_desc; sources: []):
- Completeness: If completeness == 1.0, state that the column is complete (no missing values). If < 1.0, state that missing values can occur.
- Data type: State the declared dataType (e.g., Integral) and whether it is inferred or not (isDataTypeInferred). If Integral, explicitly state that values are integers.
- Approximate distinct values: Report approximateNumDistinctValues as an approximate count of unique values.
- If typeCounts indicates mixed types, note potential type heterogeneity.

B) Code-derived assumptions (attach precise sources):
- Whether the column is coerced to numeric or cast to int/float (e.g., via pd.to_numeric(..., errors='coerce') or .astype(int/float)), and the implications (non-numeric values become NaN; downstream arithmetic expects numeric types).
- How it is used in computations:
  - If used as a numerator/denominator in ratios (e.g., approved/enrolled), specify the relationship and any zero-division handling (e.g., replace(0, np.nan) before division).
  - If used to compute rates (pass_rate, fail_rate, no-evaluation ratios), reference the exact lines that show these computations.
  - If grades are normalized by dividing by 20.0, state that scale assumption where relevant (for grade columns).
  - If clipped to [0,1], binned, or transformed (e.g., np.clip, pd.cut), state the transformation and its range.
  - If used in a weighted risk score or tiering, state how it influences those downstream metrics (e.g., contributes through pass_rate1 to academic_risk to risk_score; or directly to an age_risk component).
- If the column appears to be read/typed but not actually used in any subsequent metric or output, state that it is not used downstream in this script beyond typing.

C) Safe domain expectations (only when clearly implied by column semantics; if not enforced by code, phrase as expectations rather than guarantees; sources: [] unless the code enforces them):
- For curricular unit counts (e.g., “Curricular units 1st/2nd sem (approved/credited/enrolled/without evaluations)”):
  - Values are non-negative integers.
  - Approved <= Enrolled; Credited <= Enrolled; Without evaluations <= Enrolled. Mention these as expectations when not enforced but used as denominators elsewhere.
- For age columns:
  - Age is an integer; conversions to float are for computation.
  - Any linear scaling or thresholds evident in code (e.g., (age - 25)/20 clipped to [0,1], or other age-based conditions) should be described with code sources.
- For grades:
  - Grade scale is 0–20 when divisions by 20.0 appear in code.
- For binary indicators used in code (e.g., Debtor, Tuition fees up to date, Daytime/evening attendance), only state binary nature for the target column if it is the target and the code/metadata implies 0/1 usage.

How to cite sources:
- Use the exact line numbers shown in focused_code.
- Cite the line where the column name is bound to a variable if that helps anchor subsequent usage (e.g., col_ap1 = 'Curricular units 1st sem (approved)').
- Cite every computation line that directly uses the target variable or df[target_column].
- For loops or list-based coercions, cite both the list construction including the target and the loop/casting lines.

Prioritize assumptions that validators typically check:
- Data completeness (isComplete).
- Data type (hasDataType: Integral vs. Float vs. String).
- Approximate uniqueness.
- Numeric conversion/coercion details and resulting handling of invalids.
- Division-by-zero protection and clipping to valid ranges.

- “The column is complete (no missing values).” sources: []

- “Contributes to academic_risk via fail_rate1 and thus to overall risk_score.” sources: [{start_line: M, end_line: N}, {start_line: P, end_line: Q}]

Do not:
- Invent ranges or business rules not supported by metadata, code, or standard semantics of the column.
- Cite lines not present in the snippet.
- Use placeholder line numbers (e.g., 0).
- Add markdown or commentary outside the required JSON structure.

2026/01/14 12:01:53 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 21: Proposed new text for ir_generation: Task: Generate PyDeequ validation constraints for a single target column using:
- target_column (exact column name)

Output format:
- Return a Python list of dicts. Each dict must have:
  - "suggestion": a PyDeequ Check-builder call starting with "." (chainable method) or function (if applicable).
  - "level": "error" or "warning"
  - "linked assumptions": [list of integer indices from the assumptions that justify this constraint]
- Return only the list of constraint dicts (no extra text).

Column name resolution:
- Always use the exact column string as it appears in the data/code (including spaces, parentheses, special chars, or trailing whitespace such as tabs).
- If the code_snippet defines a variable for the target that includes subtle differences (e.g., a trailing tab), use that exact spelling from the code_snippet.
- Wrap exact column names in single quotes in the suggestion string. For satisfies(), wrap column identifiers in backticks inside the SQL expression.

Available PyDeequ Check methods to use (common subset):
- .isComplete(col)
- .hasCompleteness(col, lambda_pred)
- .hasDataType(col, ConstrainableDataTypes.<Fractional|Integral|Boolean|String|Numeric>)
- .hasNumberOfDistinctValues(col, lambda_pred)
- .isNonNegative(col)
- .isContainedIn(col, [allowed_values])
- .hasMin(col, lambda_pred)
- .hasMax(col, lambda_pred)
- .isLessThanOrEqualTo(colA, colB)
- .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo(colA, colB)
- .hasCorrelation(colA, colB, lambda_pred)
- .satisfies(sql_condition, constraint_name, lambda_pred)
- .areComplete([cols]) / .haveCompleteness([cols], lambda_pred)

General strategy:
1) Parse target_column_desc first to set base constraints:
   - Completeness:
     - If completeness == 1.0, add .isComplete('<col>') with level "error".
     - If completeness < 1.0 or not guaranteed, add .hasCompleteness('<col>', lambda x: x >= threshold) with level "warning".
       - Choose threshold from the description if given; otherwise use a conservative default like 0.95.
   - Data type:
     - Map profile dataType to ConstrainableDataTypes: Fractional, Integral, Boolean, String, Numeric.
     - Add .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.<type>) with level "error".
     - When code coerces to numeric and uses integer semantics (e.g., .astype(int) or treated as 0/1 flags), prefer Integral.
     - When code performs fractional arithmetic or normalization (division producing floats), prefer Fractional.
   - Approx distinctness:
     - If approximateNumDistinctValues is present, add a distinct-count band check as "warning":
       .hasNumberOfDistinctValues('<col>', lambda x: x >= lower and x <= upper)
       where:
         - Compute base k = approximateNumDistinctValues from profile.
         - Use a band of ±20% or ±5 counts, whichever is larger.
         - Ensure integer bounds: lower = max(1, floor(k - band)), upper = ceil(k + band).
         - For very small k (k <= 3), to avoid equality checks while being safe, use lower = k and upper = max(k, k+1).

2) Analyze code_snippet for how the target column is used. Derive constraints from:
   - Casting/conversions:
     - If the column is cast with .astype(int) or otherwise used as an integer flag/counter, enforce Integral with level "error".
     - If .astype(float) or fractional arithmetic is applied, enforce Fractional with level "error".
   - Arithmetic and bounds:
     - If the code forms ratios or clips to [0, 1], enforce expected ranges:
       - For numeric [0, 1] normalization, prefer:
         .hasMin('<col>', lambda x: x >= 0.0) and .hasMax('<col>', lambda x: x <= 1.0) (usually "warning" unless domain guarantees).
       - For known domain bounds (e.g., grades 0..20), use "error".
   - Division-denominator usage:
     - If the code replaces zeros with NaN to avoid division by zero (e.g., replace(0, np.nan)), zero is allowed for counts. Add:
       .isNonNegative('<col>') (level "error") for counts. Avoid .isPositive unless strictly required by assumptions.
   - Logical relationships and cross-column constraints:
     - If the target is a semester count (approved/credited/without evaluations) and the code uses the corresponding enrolled column, enforce:
       .isLessThanOrEqualTo('<target>', '<enrolled>') (level "error").
     - If the target is the enrolled count and code uses approved/credited/without-evaluations with it, enforce:
       .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<enrolled>', '<approved>'), etc. (level "error").
   - Correlation/relationships used in modeling:
     - If the code comments or logic rely on strong positive correlation (e.g., approvals vs enrolled), add:
       .hasCorrelation('<colA>', '<colB>', lambda x: x >= 0.6) (level "warning" unless assumptions require stricter).
   - Enumerations/allowed sets and isin checks:
     - If the code treats the target as categorical/binary (e.g., 0/1 flags), add:
       .isContainedIn('<col>', [0, 1]) (level "error").
     - If the code constrains integer categories to a range/set (e.g., Application order 1..9 used as an index), add:
       .isContainedIn('<col>', [1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8,9]) (level "error").
   - Multi-column completeness when used together:
     - If the code uses a group of columns jointly (e.g., to compute ratios), you may add:
       .areComplete([list_of_cols]) (level "error") when completeness must be perfect; else:
       .haveCompleteness([list_of_cols], lambda x: x >= threshold) (level "warning").

3) Domain defaults for the student dataset (apply only when consistent with assumptions and code):
   - Semester counts:
     - Columns like “Curricular units [1st|2nd] sem (enrolled|approved|credited|without evaluations)” are non-negative integers.
     - Approval/credited/without-evaluations cannot exceed enrolled (same semester).
     - Suggested constraints:
       - .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral) (error)
       - .isNonNegative('<col>') (error)
       - If target is approved/credited/without-evaluations: .isLessThanOrEqualTo('<target>', '<enrolled>') (error)
       - If target is enrolled: .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<enrolled>', '<approved>'), etc. (error)
   - Grades:
     - “Curricular units [1st|2nd] sem (grade)” are numeric and bounded in [0, 20].
     - Suggested constraints:
       - .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.Fractional) (error)
       - .hasMin('<col>', lambda x: x >= 0.0) (error)
       - .hasMax('<col>', lambda x: x <= 20.0) (error)
   - Binary indicator columns (e.g., Debtor, Tuition fees up to date, Scholarship holder, attendance):
     - Integers 0/1.
     - Suggested constraints:
       - .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral) (error)
       - .isContainedIn('<col>', [0, 1]) (error)
       - If profile provides approximateNumDistinctValues (often 2), also add:
         .hasNumberOfDistinctValues('<col>', lambda x: x >= 2 and x <= 3) (warning)
   - Macro features:
     - GDP/Unemployment/Inflation typically numeric (Fractional). Do not enforce bounds unless assumptions specify them.
   - Application order:
     - Expected integer in 1..9 when used to index weight arrays:
       - .hasDataType('Application order', ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral) (error)
       - .isContainedIn('Application order', [1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8,9]) (error)

4) Levels:
   - Use "error" for strict invariants: completeness=1.0, data type, non-negativity for counts, bounded grade range, categorical allowed sets, and logical cross-column inequalities (approved <= enrolled, etc.).
   - Use "warning" for statistical/distributional expectations or soft thresholds: distinct-count bands, completeness < 1.0 thresholds, correlations, and non-hard min/max ranges (e.g., [0,1] when not guaranteed by domain).
   - For satisfies with SQL-like conditions, use backticks around column names with spaces or special chars, e.g.:
     .satisfies('`colA` <= `colB`', 'colA_le_colB', lambda x: x >= 0.99)

5) Referencing assumptions:
   - For each constraint, include the indices of relevant assumptions in "linked assumptions".
   - If a constraint is supported by multiple assumptions and/or by evident code usage, include all applicable assumption indices.
   - If a constraint is strongly implied by code usage (e.g., approved <= enrolled) but not explicitly stated in assumptions, still include it and link to the assumption(s) that mention the usage or the involved columns.

6) Implementation details:
   - The "suggestion" must be a string that starts with "." so it can be chained on a Check object (e.g., Check(...).isComplete(...).isNonNegative(...)).
   - Use exact column names as they appear (including spaces, parentheses, or tabs).
   - For regexes in hasPattern(), use raw strings r"...".
   - Prefer ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral for integer counts and 0/1 flags, ConstrainableDataTypes.Fractional for grades/floats, and ConstrainableDataTypes.Numeric only when either int or float is acceptable.
   - When creating distinct-count bands, ensure integer bounds and avoid negative or zero lower bounds.

7) Quantity and coverage:
   - Cover all key invariants evidenced by the profile, assumptions, and code usage. This typically includes at least:
     - completeness (or completeness threshold)
     - data type
     - non-negativity for counts (when applicable)
     - domain ranges for bounded metrics (e.g., grades)
     - categorical/binary allowed sets (e.g., [0,1] for indicators)
     - critical cross-column inequalities (approved/credited/without-evaluations vs enrolled) when relevant to the target
     - optional distributional checks (distinct count band) and correlations if used
   - Do not add unrelated constraints.

2026/01/14 12:03:35 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 22: Proposed new text for dataflow_inspector: You are given:
- code_script: a Python code snippet (often using pandas/NumPy) with 1-based line numbers implied by the snippet.
- target_column: the name of a data column to track (string, e.g., 'Debtor', 'Application order', 'Scholarship holder').
- sink_variable: optional variable name to restrict the analysis to a specific sink; if empty, consider the entire snippet.

Your task:
Identify all lines in code_script where the target_column is used or where data derived from it flows through subsequent computations toward any sink. Return only valid JSON:
{ "sources": [{ "start_line": int, "end_line": int }, ... ] }

What to include as a “use” of the target column:
1) Direct column access or reference:
   - Any line that indexes a DataFrame/Series with the target_column (literal or via a variable), e.g.:
     df['Scholarship holder'], df[col_scholar], df[[..., col_scholar, ...]]
   - Any definition of a variable that holds the target_column string, e.g.:
     scholar_col = 'Scholarship holder'
   - Any inclusion of the target_column (literal or variable) in a column list used to subset/select/assign, e.g.:
     out = df[[..., scholar_col, ...]]
     queue_cols = [..., col_app_order, ...]
   - Any method calls applied immediately to that selection (e.g., .astype, .to_numeric, .map, .value_counts, .isin, .clip, .replace, .fillna, .mean, .corr, arithmetic, comparisons, boolean masks).

2) Derived variables and transitive data flow:
   - If a variable is assigned from the target column (directly or after immediate transforms), e.g.:
     scholar = df[scholar_col].astype(int)
     app_order = pd.to_numeric(df[col_app_order], errors='coerce').astype('Int64')
     debtor = df['Debtor'].astype(int)
     then include:
       a) The assignment line(s) that create the derived variable.
       b) Every subsequent line that uses that derived variable in any computation, comparison, boolean filter, aggregation, or assignment (e.g., fee_risk = 0.6 * debtor + ...; arrears = (is_debtor == 1) | ...; scholar_bonus = np.where(scholar == 1, ...)).
       c) Every further line that depends on those downstream variables (transitive closure), e.g., fee_risk_adj = f(fee_risk, ...); raw_score uses fee_risk_adj; risk_score uses raw_score; urgent_fee_flag uses debtor; any outputs that include or depend on these.
   - If sink_variable is provided, only keep the subset of dependent lines that ultimately affect that sink_variable (its assignment/modification/usage). Perform the same dependency tracing but prune paths that do not reach the named sink. Include the sink’s own assignment/usage lines.
   - If sink_variable is empty, do not prune: include all lines in the dependency chain for any downstream computations/outputs influenced by the target column.

3) Output and pass-through lines:
   - Any line that passes the target column (literal or variable) into an output structure or I/O operation:
     - Selecting it into DataFrames that are later written (e.g., out = df[[..., scholar_col, ...]]).
     - Assigning/including it in column lists for later to_csv operations (queue_cols = [..., col_app_order, ...]).
     - Any to_csv/json.dump or similar I/O that writes DataFrames/objects which were influenced by the target column through derived variables (include the lines that build those objects/columns and the final write line if it’s part of a multi-line call).
   - Any line that constructs diagnostics or flags using variables derived from the target column (e.g., urgent_fee_flag uses debtor).

4) Multi-line and complex expressions:
   - If an expression spans multiple lines (inside parentheses/brackets), and any part of it reads/uses a tracked symbol (target column, column-name variable, or any derived/transitively dependent variable), include all lines of that multi-line expression range.
   - Include comparison/mask lines (e.g., (app == 1), app.isin([...]), (debtor == 1), scholar == 1) and their usage in np.where, pd.cut, boolean indexing, etc.
   - Include type/coercion/completeness-like operations: pd.to_numeric(...), .astype(int or 'Int64'), .fillna, .replace, .isna(), .notna(), value_counts, ratios, means. These are considered data type or completeness checks/uses of the target column.

What to exclude:
- Comment-only lines and string literals appearing only in comments.
- Unrelated lines that neither reference the target column (directly or via its column-name variable) nor any variable derived from it, nor contribute (transitively) to a retained sink.

Algorithm to apply:
1) Token discovery:
   - Find any variables assigned exactly to the target_column string (e.g., scholar_col = 'Scholarship holder'). Mark them as column-name aliases.
   - Find any direct DataFrame/Series accesses that use the target_column literal or any alias, and mark the LHS variables assigned from these as “derived-from-target.”
2) Dependency expansion:
   - Build a set S of tracked symbols starting with: {target-column aliases} ∪ {variables directly derived from the target column}.
   - Scan forward; if a line uses any symbol in S on the RHS, add the LHS target(s) to S and include that line. Repeat until no new symbols are added (transitive closure).
   - Also include any line where an alias of the target column appears within a column selection list.
3) Sink pruning:
   - If sink_variable is provided, restrict to the subset of included lines that lie on at least one dependency path to the sink_variable’s assignment/usage. Always include the sink_variable’s own assignment line(s).
   - If sink_variable is empty, keep all included lines from the dependency expansion.
4) Range merging and numbering:
   - Use exact 1-based line numbers from code_script (count every line including blanks).
   - Merge adjacent lines into a single {start_line, end_line} range. Non-adjacent lines must be separate ranges.
   - Sort ranges by start_line, and ensure they are non-overlapping and minimal (no duplicates).

Output format:
- Return only valid JSON with a single key "sources" mapping to a list of objects, each with "start_line" and "end_line" integers.
- Example:
  { "sources": [ { "start_line": 34, "end_line": 52 }, { "start_line": 138, "end_line": 138 } ] }

Domain-specific patterns to remember (commonly present in these snippets):
- Columns are defined as variables holding strings with spaces/parentheses, e.g., col_app_order = 'Application order', scholar_col = 'Scholarship holder'.
- Access patterns: df[col_var], df['literal'], pd.to_numeric(df[col_var], ...).astype(...), .map/.isin/.value_counts/.mean, arithmetic and boolean ops.
- Derived variables propagate into risk components and flags: fee_risk uses debtor; scholar_bonus uses scholar; fee_risk_adj uses fee_risk; raw_score uses fee_risk_adj; risk_score uses raw_score; urgent_fee_flag uses debtor/tuition; commitment computed from app_order; selection into out/output DataFrames includes the target column variables; outputs written via to_csv/json.dump.
- Include both the initial column name variable definition and every downstream line where the column or its derivatives are used, transformed, combined, filtered, or written.

Be precise and exhaustive: missing any of the above uses will be scored as false negatives.

2026/01/14 12:05:42 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 23: Proposed new text for dataflow_inspector: Task: Identify all line ranges in a Python code snippet where a target data column is used or any value derived from it is used, and return them as 1-based line-number ranges in JSON.

Inputs
- code_script: Full Python code snippet as a string.
- target_column: Exact column name (string) to track.
- sink_variable: Optional. A Python variable/column name to act as the “sink.” If empty or not provided, include every usage throughout the snippet. If provided, include only those lines that lie on dependency paths from the target column to the sink.

Output
- Return only valid JSON: { "sources": [{ "start_line": int, "end_line": int }, ... ] }
- Use exact 1-based line numbers from the provided snippet.
- Merge adjacent lines into a single range; non-adjacent lines must appear as separate ranges.
- Do not include any extraneous text or formatting.

What counts as a “use”
Include every line that reads, writes, filters, transforms, merges, selects, computes with, or otherwise passes through the target column. This includes:
- Defining an alias for the column name (e.g., c2_enr = 'Curricular units 2nd sem (enrolled)') when the string matches target_column exactly.
- Any DataFrame access with that alias or literal name (e.g., df[c2_enr], df['Tuition fees up to date'], df.loc[:, alias], df.filter(items=[...]), selection lists that include the alias/literal).
- Any conversion, cleaning, or transformation of the resulting Series/DataFrame (e.g., astype, to_numeric, replace, fillna, clip, map, where, np.where, arithmetic, boolean operations, pd.cut, np.clip).
- Any variable assignment whose right-hand side depends (directly or indirectly) on the target-derived data (e.g., e2 = df[c2_enr].astype(float); e2_safe = e2.replace(...); pass_rate2 = ... e2_safe ...).
- Any subsequent line that uses a variable that is (transitively) derived from the target column, including:
  - Feature composition and aggregation (e.g., academic_gap, raw_score, risk_score).
  - Conditional flags and masks (e.g., urgent_fee_flag = ((risk_score >= 70) | (tuition_up == 0)).astype(int)).
  - Output selection including the column or any derived variables (e.g., out = df[[..., c2_enr]]; queue_cols lists; assigning out['risk_score'] = risk_score).
  - Persistence of results that contain or are computed from the target (e.g., out.to_csv(...), queue[...].to_csv(...)), because the data “passes through” to the sink/output.

Important: Propagate dependencies forward
- Build an alias set: Any variable assigned exactly the literal target_column string becomes a column alias.
- A variable becomes “dependent” if it is assigned from:
  - df[alias] or df['target_column'] (direct read),
  - any expression that uses a dependent variable (transitive read/transform),
  - any selection or transformation that includes the alias/literal column.
- Continue propagating: every line that assigns to, computes with, filters by, or otherwise uses a dependent variable is also a use. This propagation typically spans:
  - Series creation from the column,
  - safety wrappers (e.g., e2_safe = e2.replace(0, np.nan)),
  - ratios, rates, gaps, and aggregates,
  - model inputs (raw_score) and outputs (risk_score, flags),
  - output DataFrame assembly and file writes that include those dependent values.
- Stop propagation if a dependent variable is reassigned to an expression that does not use any dependent variable (i.e., it no longer depends on the target).

Sink-variable handling (if sink_variable is provided)
- Treat sink_variable as the terminal node(s). Only include lines that lie on any dependency path from the target column to the sink_variable.
- The sink can be:
  - A Python variable (e.g., risk_score, urgent_fee_flag).
  - A DataFrame column assignment (e.g., out['risk_score']).
  - A variable used in persistence (e.g., out.to_csv(...), queue[...] where the constructed object includes sink_variable).
- Practical approach:
  1) Do forward dependency propagation from the target to mark all dependent variables.
  2) If sink_variable is provided, do a backward pass from the sink to retain only those dependent variables that connect target -> ... -> sink.
  3) Include only the lines on these connecting paths.

Typical patterns in the provided scripts (be sure to catch these)
- Column alias blocks:
  - c1_* and c2_* strings for first/second semester academic columns.
  - debtor_col, tuition_col, scholar_col, gdp_col, unemp_col, infl_col, etc.
- Direct reads from df[...] into local variables:
  - e.g., tuition_up = df[tuition_col].astype(int)
  - g2 = df[c2_grd].astype(float), e2 = df[c2_enr].astype(float)
- Safety transforms and ratios:
  - e2_safe = e2.replace(0, np.nan)
  - pass_rate2 = (a2 / e2_safe)...
  - grade_norm2 = gr2 / 20.0; grade_gap2 = (20.0 - g2)/20.0
- Composition into higher-level features and final scores:
  - academic_gap from multiple components
  - raw_score from weighted components
  - risk_score from raw_score and shifts
  - flags and tiers (urgent_fee_flag, priority via pd.cut)
- Output assembly and persistence:
  - out = df[[..., alias/literal ...]]
  - out['risk_score'] = risk_score; out.to_csv(...)
  - queue_cols including aliases; queue[...] .to_csv(...)
- Macro and application-order blocks are not relevant unless the target column participates there.

Inclusions and exclusions
- Include only code lines (ignore blank-only lines). Lines with code and trailing comments should be included.
- Do not include import lines or argument parsing unless they directly use the target column or a dependent variable (they generally don’t).
- Be conservative: when unsure if a line passes through the target via a dependent variable, include it. Prefer recall to avoid false negatives.

Merging ranges
- After collecting all matching line numbers:
  - Sort them and merge consecutive numbers into a single {start_line, end_line} range.
  - Non-consecutive lines must be separate ranges.

Numbering
- Use exact 1-based line numbers as they appear in the provided snippet.

Return format
- Output must be only valid JSON with the top-level key "sources".
- Ensure all start_line and end_line are integers.

- Use exact column names (including spaces, punctuation, or tabs) from the code snippet or target_column. When using .satisfies, wrap column names with backticks: `col name`.

- Avoid constraints that require extra runtime parameters unless explicitly justified by assumptions. Specifically, do not use hasNumberOfDistinctValues unless the assumption explicitly fixes a value/range and, if used, include the required parameters (binningUdf=None, maxBins=1000).

   - If target_column_desc.dataType is provided, use it with .hasDataType(target_column, ConstrainableDataTypes.<Type>) at "error".
     - Map to one of: Null, Fractional, Integral, Boolean, String, Numeric.
   - If the code coerces the column with pandas to numeric (pd.to_numeric(...)) but may represent values as floats (e.g., 0.0) or produce NaN, Integral checks can fail. In that case:
     - Prefer .hasDataType(..., ConstrainableDataTypes.Numeric) at "error" if assumptions do not explicitly require integers.
     - If assumptions explicitly state integral counts and the profiling shows dataType=Integral, keep Integral at "error". If you see mixed float representations likely due to coercion, you may optionally add a secondary relaxed .hasDataType(..., ConstrainableDataTypes.Numeric) at "warning".
   - Link to the assumptions that state type or numeric nature.

3) Value bounds for counts and flags:
   - For count-like columns (e.g., “enrolled”, “approved”, “credited”, “without evaluations”) that represent quantities:
     - Add .isNonNegative(column) at "error" (not .isPositive, because zero is often valid).
   - For boolean/flag columns represented as 0/1, use .isContainedIn(column, [0, 1]) at "error" if assumptions/code imply 0/1.

4) Cross-column relationships implied by ratios or usage:
   - If the target column is used as a denominator (e.g., numerator / target_column), add row-level constraints ensuring numerators are bounded by the denominator:
     - For each numerator N divided by target: .isLessThanOrEqualTo(N, target_column) at "error".
     - Examples seen in student academic data:
       - approved <= enrolled
       - credited <= enrolled
       - without evaluations <= enrolled
   - If the code uses target.replace(0, np.nan) before division, zero is allowed in the denominator; do NOT enforce positive-only. Keep .isNonNegative(target) instead of .isPositive(target).
   - If there is an explicit positive correlation assumption or the code relies on a relationship like approvals positively correlated with enrollments, add:
     - .hasCorrelation(target_column, other_column, lambda x: x >= 0.3) as "warning" by default, or "error" if the assumption explicitly states “strong positive correlation”.

5) Ranges for known scales:
   - Only when assumptions or code explicitly define a scale (e.g., grades normalized by dividing by 20 → grades are in [0, 20]):
     - Add .hasMin(column, lambda x: x >= LOWER) and/or .hasMax(column, lambda x: x <= UPPER) at "error".
   - Do not guess ranges for counts unless assumptions provide them.

6) Distinctness and cardinality:
   - Do not add distinctness/number-of-distinct constraints unless the assumptions explicitly require them.
   - If you must use hasNumberOfDistinctValues, include binningUdf=None and maxBins=1000, and mark as "warning" unless the assumption makes it critical.

7) Levels:
   - Use "error" for constraints that, if violated, would break downstream computations or invalidate core assumptions:
     - completeness (when assumed 1.0),
     - data type (when strongly asserted),
     - non-negativity for counts,
     - cross-column inequalities supporting ratios.
   - Use "warning" for softer, model-quality or heuristic checks:
     - correlations,
     - approximate cardinality,
     - relaxed data type when stricter type is already enforced.

8) Linking assumptions:
   - For each constraint, include indices of all assumptions that justify it. Use a list of integer indices.

Formatting examples:
- ".isComplete('Curricular units 2nd sem (enrolled)')"
- ".hasDataType('Curricular units 2nd sem (enrolled)', ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral)"
- ".isNonNegative('Curricular units 2nd sem (enrolled)')"
- ".isLessThanOrEqualTo('Curricular units 2nd sem (approved)', 'Curricular units 2nd sem (enrolled)')"
- ".hasCorrelation('Curricular units 2nd sem (enrolled)', 'Curricular units 2nd sem (approved)', lambda x: x >= 0.3)"

Caveats observed in similar tasks:
- Pandas coercion (pd.to_numeric with errors='coerce') can yield floats like 0.0 and NaN even for integer-like columns; Integral checks may fail in such cases. Respect dataType from target_column_desc and assumptions, but consider Numeric if floats are likely due to processing.
- When division by the target column appears, always enforce numerator <= denominator instead of requiring denominator > 0, since zero is often handled upstream via replace(0, np.nan) or conditional logic.
- Avoid speculative constraints like hasNumberOfDistinctValues based only on approximateNumDistinctValues unless assumptions demand it.

Return only the JSON list of constraints as specified.

2026/01/14 12:12:59 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 27: Proposed new text for dataflow_inspector: You are given:
- code_script: a single code snippet (Python; commonly uses pandas/numpy).
- target_column: the exact column name string to track (case-sensitive; may contain spaces, punctuation, or control characters like tabs).
- sink_variable: optional; if non-empty, include only the lines that ultimately affect this sink.

Your task:
Return only valid JSON with the exact line ranges (1-based) in code_script where the target column is read, written, filtered, transformed, merged, selected, or otherwise passed through. Merge adjacent lines into a single range; non-adjacent lines must be separate ranges.

Output format:
{ "sources": [ { "start_line": int, "end_line": int }, ... ] }

Requirements and detailed guidance:

1) Line numbering and formatting
- Count lines in code_script starting from 1 (1-based).
- Include only code lines that engage with the target column as defined below.
- Do not include comments-only lines or unrelated imports.
- Return only valid JSON. No extra text.

2) What counts as “usage” of the target column
Include every line that does any of the following with the target column (directly or indirectly):
- Reads or writes the column data, e.g.:
  - df['Target'], df[target_alias], df.loc[:, 'Target'], df.loc[mask, 'Target'], df[['Target', ...]]
  - df['Target'] = ..., df.assign(Target=...), df.rename(columns={'Target': 'NewName'}) [passes through]
  - pd.to_numeric(df['Target'], ...), df['Target'].astype(...), df['Target'].fillna(...), df['Target'].replace(...)
  - Expressions/masks based on the column: (df['Target'] == 1), df['Target'] > 0, df['Target'].isna(), np.where(condition involving df['Target'], ...)
  - Aggregations using the column series: df['Target'].mean(), .std(), .value_counts(), etc.
- Filters, transforms, or otherwise uses the column in methods:
  - df.filter(items=['Target']), df.drop(columns=['Target']), df.get(['Target']), df.set_index('Target'), df.reset_index(level='Target'), df.groupby('Target'), df.sort_values('Target'), df.sample(...) with weights=df['Target'].
  - Wide/long operations referencing the column: pivot, pivot_table, melt (id_vars/value_vars), stack/unstack, get_dummies(columns=[...]), merge/join (on='Target', left_on/right_on referencing the column), concat/merge where the column is in the subset/list of columns used.
  - Query/eval strings that reference the exact column name: df.query('`Target` >= 0'), df.eval('`Target` * 2'), even if inside string literals.
- Pass-through via selection or output:
  - Selecting the target column for intermediate or final outputs: e.g., df[[..., target_alias, ...]], building column lists that include it for to_csv/to_parquet, writing subsets that include it, etc.

3) Alias detection and propagation
Many scripts first bind column names to variables. You must:
- Identify alias variables that are assigned exactly the target column string:
  - Example: col_prev = 'Previous qualification (grade)'
  - Include the alias definition line itself, to anchor the reference to the target column.
- Track all later uses of these alias variables wherever a column name is accepted:
  - df[alias], df.loc[:, alias], df[[..., alias, ...]], list/dict of columns that include alias, groupby(alias), merge(on=alias), left_on/right_on=[..., alias, ...], set_index(alias), get_dummies(columns=[..., alias, ...]), etc.
- If the alias is included in a list used later (e.g., num_cols = [alias, ...]) and there is a loop or comprehension that iterates that list to operate on df[col], mark the loop body as usage:
  - Include the line that defines the list containing the alias.
  - Include lines inside the loop/comprehension that operate on df[col] (or similar) because they will execute for the target column in some iteration.
  - Example:
    num_cols = [alias, ...]
    for col in num_cols:
        df[col] = pd.to_numeric(df[col], errors='coerce')
    Include the list definition and the loop body lines that access df[col].
- Derived series/variables propagation:
  - If a variable is assigned from the target column or from another derived variable (e.g., prev_grade = pd.to_numeric(df[alias], ...); preparedness = 0.4 * (prev_grade / 200)), then any subsequent line using that derived variable is considered “passes through.”
  - Include all such lines where the derived variable participates in computations, selections, filters, or is written out, even if a coefficient or weight is zero.

4) Transitive dependency and sink_variable
- By default (empty sink_variable): include all lines that directly or indirectly use the target column as described above.
- If sink_variable is non-empty:
  - Restrict to lines that lie on any dependency path from the target column to the sink_variable.
  - Treat sink_variable as a variable name or dataframe column name defined/assigned in code_script. Include:
    - Lines that compute intermediate variables derived from the target column and ultimately feed into the sink_variable.
    - The line(s) where sink_variable is assigned/updated.
    - Any list-building and loop bodies that operate on the target column if those outputs are used to compute the sink_variable.
  - Exclude usages of the target column that do not affect the sink_variable.
  - If you cannot resolve the dependency precisely (e.g., very dynamic code), prefer recall: include a superset of lines likely on the path to the sink, but do not include clearly unrelated usages.

5) Patterns to detect (non-exhaustive; include these)
- Direct indexing and attribute chains: df['Target'], df[alias], df.loc[..., 'Target'], df.iloc[..., df.columns.get_loc('Target')]
- Column list membership used in operations: df[[..., 'Target', ...]], some_list = ['Target', ...]; df[some_list]; df.drop(columns=some_list); df.to_csv(columns=some_list)
- Loops/comprehensions over column lists that include the target: for col in cols: df[col] = ..., [df[col] for col in cols if ...]
- Transformations: pd.to_numeric(df['Target'], ...), astype, replace, fillna, clip, map, apply where the callable receives df['Target'] or derived series
- Aggregations/statistics: mean, std, quantile, value_counts on the target series
- Conditional logic: masks/np.where involving the target series
- String-based references: df.query('`Target` ...'), df.eval('... `Target` ...')
- Reshaping/grouping/merge: groupby('Target'), pivot(index='Target'...), melt(id_vars=['Target', ...]), get_dummies(columns=[... 'Target' ...]), merge(on='Target') or left_on/right_on including 'Target' or alias
- Rename/select that passes through the target: rename(columns={'Target': ...}), filter(items=['Target']), set_index('Target'), reset_index(level='Target')
- Output selection: assembling column lists that include the target for writes, and calls that consume those lists (to_csv, to_parquet, etc.)

6) Ranges
- Collect all individual line numbers that meet the criteria above.
- Merge consecutive numbers into [start_line, end_line] ranges.
- Keep ranges sorted by start_line and non-overlapping.

7) Edge cases and cautions
- Exact string match only for the target_column, including whitespace/control chars (e.g., 'Daytime/evening attendance\t').
- Include alias assignment lines that bind the exact target_column string.
- Multi-line expressions: include each line that actually contains the target/alias/derived variables; then merge if they are adjacent lines.
- If nothing found, return { "sources": [] }.

Return only:
{ "sources": [ { "start_line": int, "end_line": int }, ... ] }
