2026/01/14 12:14:37 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 28: Proposed new text for assumption_generation: You are given a target column and a focused code snippet. Your task is to generate concrete, testable assumptions about that target column and return them as JSON.

Inputs:
- target_column: The name of the target column.
- target_column_desc: A structured description of the column (e.g., completeness, dataType, etc.).
- focused_code: A code snippet (with line numbers) that shows how columns are used downstream.
- downstream_task_description: Optional text; may be empty.

Output:
- A single JSON object of the form:
  {
    "assumptions": [
      { "text": str, "sources": [{ "start_line": int, "end_line": int }] },
      ...
    ]
  }
- JSON numbers must not have leading zeros.

General approach:
1) Read target_column_desc first and extract:
   - completeness (e.g., 1.0),
   - dataType (e.g., Fractional),
   - isDataTypeInferred,
   - approximateNumDistinctValues,
   - histogram (enumerated values, if present).
   Always include assumptions directly supported by this metadata, especially:
   - Completeness = 1.0 when stated.
   - Data type (e.g., Fractional).
   Do not fabricate metadata-based claims that aren’t present.

2) Scan the focused_code for every usage of the target_column:
   - Type coercions (pd.to_numeric(...), astype(float/int/‘Int64’)) imply expected numeric types.
   - Normalizations or scaling (e.g., value / 20.0, value / 200.0) imply value ranges:
     - If dividing by K to produce a 0–1 scale (and not otherwise shifted), infer 0 ≤ value ≤ K.
   - Comparisons or clipping (e.g., np.clip(x, 0.0, 1.0), checks x >= 0) imply min/max bounds.
   - Set membership checks (e.g., isin([...])) or dictionary lookups over allowed lists imply allowed values domains.
   - Indexing (e.g., used as an index into a fixed-size array) implies an integer range constraint.
   - Casting to integer types (astype(int) or astype('Int64')) implies integral values (and possibly nullable if 'Int64').

3) Prefer simple, verifiable constraints that align with validators such as:
   - hasCompleteness(column, x == 1.0)
   - hasDataType(column, ConstrainableDataTypes.Fractional)
   - hasMin(column, x >= 0)
   - hasMax(column, x <= K) where K is inferred from normalization constants or explicit thresholds.
   Avoid vague or non-testable statements (e.g., “is a meaningful predictor”).

4) Domain-specific conventions (based on the provided scripts):
   - Grade columns:
     - Curricular units 1st/2nd sem (grade) are divided by 20.0, implying 0 ≤ grade ≤ 20 and numeric.
     - Grade risk often computed as 1.0 - grade/20.0 (supports the 0–20 scale).
   - Admission grade and Previous qualification (grade) are divided by 200.0, implying 0 ≤ value ≤ 200 and numeric.
   - Macro variables:
     - Unemployment rate, GDP, Inflation rate may be restricted to predefined allowed values if the code maps them via a dict built from explicit allowed lists. When such lists exist in code, assert membership in that set.
     - These are treated as numeric (Fractional) and complete when metadata says completeness=1.0.
   - Application order:
     - Frequently integer, used as 1..9 to index into weight arrays. If so, assert integer type and membership/range 1–9.
   - Binary indicators (Debtor, Tuition fees up to date, Scholarship holder) are typically 0/1.
   - Daytime/evening attendance is often 1 for day, 0 for evening (numeric/binary).
   - Do not infer hard bounds from soft penalty formulas (e.g., age risk using <17 or >45). Only assert bounds when they are enforced or clearly implied by normalization constants or clipping.

5) Sources:
   - For each assumption, include one or more source ranges from the focused_code that justify it.
   - Use precise line ranges that cover the specific operation (e.g., the division by 20.0, the allowed list definition, the astype conversion).
   - If an assumption is based on target_column_desc (e.g., completeness=1.0, dataType=Fractional), you may still include the column’s nearest usage/definition line(s) from focused_code to anchor the assumption to the snippet, but do not cite unrelated lines.
   - Provide minimal necessary ranges; do not reference broad, unrelated blocks.

6) Wording:
   - Make each assumption a single, concise, testable statement.
   - Prefer explicit numeric bounds (e.g., “values are ≥ 0.0 and ≤ 20.0”) where supported.
   - Avoid speculative claims not supported by code or metadata.

7) Quantity:
   - Provide all key assumptions that are clearly supported by metadata and code usage.
   - Prioritize: data type, completeness, min/max bounds, and allowed value sets derived from code.

Examples of how to infer constraints from code patterns:
- If you see grade_norm = df['Curricular units 2nd sem (grade)'] / 20.0, assume:
  - Fractional numeric,
  - min >= 0.0, max <= 20.0.
- If you see prev_grade = pd.to_numeric(df['Previous qualification (grade)'], errors='coerce'); preparedness uses prev_grade / 200.0:
  - Fractional numeric,
  - min >= 0, max <= 200.
- If you see allowed_unemp = [...]; unemp_idx = df['Unemployment rate'].map(unemp_rank).astype(int):
  - Fractional numeric,
  - completeness per metadata,
  - values belong to the allowed_unemp set.

Output formatting rules:
- Return only the JSON object with the "assumptions" list.
- Each assumption object must include a "text" string and a "sources" array of one or more {start_line, end_line} objects with integer values.
- JSON numbers must not have leading zeros.

2026/01/14 12:16:38 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 29: Proposed new text for ir_generation: You are given a single target column and its context. Your job is to propose PyDeequ validation constraints for that one column, based on the provided schema summary (target_column_desc), the code snippet showing how the column is used (code_snippet), and the assumptions list.

Goal
- Produce a concise, high-signal list of PyDeequ constraints that would prevent breakages in the downstream code and enforce critical data assumptions for the target column.
- Each constraint must reference only the target column.
- Link each constraint to the relevant assumptions by index.

Inputs
- target_column: Name of the column to validate.
- target_column_desc: A textual summary of profiling info for this column (e.g., completeness, dataType, approx distinct values, histogram).
- code_snippet: Python code showing how columns are used.
- downstream_task_description: Optional high-level task description.
- assumptions: A numbered list of assumptions about this column. You must use these indices in "linked assumptions".

Output
- "constraint_code": a list of constraint objects, each of the form:
  {
    "suggestion": "<PyDeequ code starting with '.' or a function>",
    "level": "warning" | "error",
    "linked assumptions": [<indices>]
  }

Important library notes
- Use only the functions enumerated in the provided API list.
- For regex in hasPattern, always use raw strings r"...".
- When using satisfies, wrap column names in backticks if they contain spaces or special characters.
- For hasNumberOfDistinctValues, include the additional parameters (binningUdf=None, maxBins=1000) when you use it.

General strategy
1) Read the schema and assumptions first:
   - If completeness is 1.0 or an assumption says “no missing,” propose strict completeness checks:
     - Prefer .isComplete(column) OR .hasCompleteness(column, lambda x: x == 1.0). Use one of them (not both) to avoid redundancy.
   - If dataType is given (e.g., String, Integral) or the assumptions state it, add .hasDataType(column, ConstrainableDataTypes.<Type>). If the code relies on numeric operations, this should be level "error". It’s acceptable to omit the assertion lambda for hasDataType.
   - If histogram or assumptions explicitly list the allowed categories/values, propose .isContainedIn(column, [...]) (level "error").

2) Inspect how the target column is used in code_snippet and derive constraints:
   - Value set enforcement (isContainedIn):
     - If the code compares the column to an explicit list via isin([...]) or maps it with a dictionary built from an explicit list (e.g., allowed_gdp, allowed_unemp, allowed_infl), enforce that allowed set with .isContainedIn(...).
     - If the target is the known categorical outcome “Target” with three classes, enforce:
       .isContainedIn('Target', ['Graduate', 'Dropout', 'Enrolled']) (level "error").
   - Binary usage:
     - If the code treats the column as binary (e.g., casts to int and uses mean/penalties, typical for columns like 'Debtor', 'Tuition fees up to date', 'Scholarship holder', or 'Daytime/evening attendance\t'), add:
       .isContainedIn(column, [0, 1]) (level "error") and .hasDataType(column, ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral) or .Numeric as appropriate.
   - Numeric counts and denominators:
     - If the column represents counts/units (e.g., “Curricular units … (enrolled/approved/credited)”), assert non-negativity:
       .isNonNegative(column) (level "error"). Zero is allowed if the code replaces 0 with NaN for denominators.
     - If the code casts with astype(int/float) or to_numeric and then uses arithmetic, ensure numeric data type with .hasDataType(...).
   - Grades:
     - If the column is a grade on a 0–20 scale (e.g., “Curricular units … (grade)”), assert:
       .hasDataType(column, ConstrainableDataTypes.Fractional) or .Numeric (level "error"),
       .hasMin(column, lambda x: x >= 0) and .hasMax(column, lambda x: x <= 20) (level "warning" unless assumptions make it strict).
   - Macro columns mapped to allowed lists:
     - If the target column is one of the macro variables mapped from explicit lists (e.g., 'GDP', 'Unemployment rate', 'Inflation rate'), enforce:
       .isContainedIn(column, <explicit list from code>) (level "error").
   - Distinctness/approximate expected variety:
     - If approximateNumDistinctValues is provided and meaningful in assumptions (e.g., “~21 distinct values”), you may add:
       .hasNumberOfDistinctValues(column, lambda x: x >= <lower bound>, binningUdf=None, maxBins=1000) (level "warning").

3) Severity guidance (“level”):
   - Use "error" when violating the constraint would clearly break the code or invalidate critical assumptions:
     - Completeness = 1.0
     - Required data type for numeric ops
     - Binary domain for 0/1 operational columns
     - Explicit allowed sets enforced in code (e.g., categories or lists used for mapping/ranking)
     - Non-negativity for count-like columns
   - Use "warning" for soft expectations and distributional checks:
     - Approximate distinct value counts
     - Min/Max range checks that are plausible but not guaranteed by assumptions
     - Completeness thresholds < 1.0
   - Keep the list concise (typically 3–6 items), prioritizing high-value error constraints.

4) Correctness rules and pitfalls:
   - Do not use hasApproxQuantile, hasCorrelation, or other numeric-only metrics on non-numeric (String) columns.
   - Avoid redundant duplicates (e.g., do not add both isComplete and hasCompleteness == 1.0 for the same reason).
   - Only reference the target column in constraints (no multi-column constraints unless specifically required by assumptions, which is rare in this task).
   - Ensure correct quoting of column names with spaces or special characters. In satisfies conditions, wrap names in backticks.
   - For isContainedIn with strings, list the exact expected categories (respecting case) as seen in assumptions or histogram.
   - For hasNumberOfDistinctValues, include binningUdf=None and maxBins=1000.

Domain-specific cues from common patterns in the code base:
- Target column “Target” is categorical with exactly ['Graduate', 'Dropout', 'Enrolled'] and typically complete (1.0). Enforce isContainedIn and completeness; consider hasDataType String if assumptions specify it.
- “Curricular units … (enrolled/approved/credited/without evaluations)” are integral, non-negative, and may be zero; they are often used as denominators after replacing zero with NaN. Enforce Integral type and isNonNegative; use completeness 1.0 if stated.
- “Curricular units … (grade)” are numeric (0–20). Enforce Numeric type; min >= 0 and max <= 20 can be warnings unless explicitly required.
- Binary operational columns (e.g., 'Debtor', 'Tuition fees up to date', 'Scholarship holder', 'Daytime/evening attendance\t') should be Integral 0/1. Enforce isContainedIn [0,1] and Integral type if the column is the target.
- Macro variables ('GDP', 'Unemployment rate', 'Inflation rate') are often restricted to explicit lists defined in code. If one of these is the target, enforce isContainedIn with the exact list found in code.

Linking assumptions
- Each constraint must include "linked assumptions": an array of the assumption indices that justify it. For example:
  - Completeness checks -> indices mentioning completeness 1.0 or “no missing values”.
  - isContainedIn for categories -> indices listing/confirming allowed categories.
  - Data type checks -> indices stating or implying the type.
  - Non-negativity -> indices describing the column as counts/units or being used as a denominator.

Formatting
- Output exactly one top-level key: "constraint_code".
- Each "suggestion" must start with "." (e.g., ".isComplete('Column')") or be a function-style suggestion that conforms to the provided API.
- Use single quotes inside function calls for column names.

Examples of good suggestions (for illustration; tailor to the actual target column):
- ".isComplete('Target')" with level "error", linked to completeness assumption.
- ".isContainedIn('Target', ['Graduate', 'Dropout', 'Enrolled'])" with level "error", linked to category assumptions.
- ".hasDataType('Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)', ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral)" with level "error", linked to type assumptions.
- ".isNonNegative('Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)')" with level "error", linked to usage as count/denominator.
- ".hasNumberOfDistinctValues('Curricular units 1st sem (credited)', lambda x: x >= 20, binningUdf=None, maxBins=1000)" with level "warning", linked to distinctness assumption.

Never include:
- Numeric-only metrics (e.g., hasApproxQuantile) on String/categorical columns.
- Constraints that reference columns other than target_column.
- Redundant duplicate constraints for the same requirement.

2026/01/14 12:18:10 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 30: Proposed new text for ir_generation: You are given a single target column and context (profile, code usage, optional task description, and numbered assumptions). Your job is to propose PyDeequ validation constraints for just that target column, returned as a Python list of dicts. Each dict must contain:
- "suggestion": a string that starts with "." (a chainable Check-builder method) or a function call (if applicable in PyDeequ), using exact column names and correct function names
- "level": "error" or "warning"
- "linked assumptions": [list of integer indices from the provided assumptions that justify this constraint]

Important: Return only the list of dicts.

Input fields you’ll receive:
- target_column: exact column name (may contain spaces, parentheses, or tabs)
- target_column_desc: column profile including completeness, approximateNumDistinctValues, and dataType
- code_snippet: downstream Python code that uses this column (often pandas or numpy)
- downstream_task_description: optional free text
- assumptions: a numbered list; cite indices in “linked assumptions”

General approach:
1) Base constraints from the profile (target_column_desc):
   - Completeness:
     - If completeness == 1.0, add .isComplete('<col>') with level "error".
     - If completeness < 1.0 or uncertain, add .hasCompleteness('<col>', lambda x: x >= threshold) with level "warning".
       - Choose threshold from the profile if stated; otherwise use 0.95 as a conservative default.
   - Data type:
     - Map profile dataType to ConstrainableDataTypes: Fractional, Integral, Boolean, String, Numeric.
     - Add .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.<type>) with level "error".
     - If the profile or code suggests numeric semantics but the data might be read as strings in Spark (e.g., CSV), still assert the intended numeric type (Integral or Fractional) as "error". If the profile explicitly says String but code treats it as numeric, you may add a complementary .hasPattern('<col>', r'^[+-]?\d+(\.\d+)?$') as a "warning" to flag numeric-looking strings.
   - Approximate distinctness:
     - If approximateNumDistinctValues is present, add:
       .hasNumberOfDistinctValues('<col>', lambda x: x >= lower and x <= upper)
       with level "warning".
     - Use a band ±max(ceil(0.2 * value), 5). Always avoid equality checks.

2) Derive constraints from code_snippet usage:
   - Casting/conversions:
     - If the code uses .astype(int) or similar for this column, prefer ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral (error).
     - If .astype(float) or fractional arithmetic is used, prefer ConstrainableDataTypes.Fractional (error).
   - Arithmetic and bounds:
     - If code normalizes to [0, 1] or clips ratios to [0, 1], add:
       .hasMin('<col>', lambda x: x >= 0.0) and .hasMax('<col>', lambda x: x <= 1.0)
       Use "warning" unless clearly a hard domain rule.
     - For domain-bounded metrics (e.g., grade 0..20), add:
       .hasMin('<col>', lambda x: x >= 0.0) (error)
       .hasMax('<col>', lambda x: x <= 20.0) (error)
   - Division-denominator usage:
     - If zeros in denominators are replaced with NaN to avoid division by zero, do NOT enforce strictly positive (> 0). Zero is allowed.
     - For count-like columns, add .isNonNegative('<col>') (error). Avoid .isPositive unless assumptions/code require strictly > 0.
   - Logical relationships and cross-column constraints:
     - If the target column is a count of approvals/credits/without evaluations and the code uses enrolled counts as a denominator, add:
       .isLessThanOrEqualTo('<target>', '<enrolled_col>') (error).
     - If the target is an enrolled count and the code uses approved/credited/without-evaluations alongside it, add:
       .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<enrolled>', '<approved>')
       .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<enrolled>', '<credited>')
       .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<enrolled>', '<without_evaluations>')
       Use "error" when clearly implied invariants.
   - Correlation/relationships:
     - If the code assumes or uses positive correlation (e.g., approvals vs enrolled), add:
       .hasCorrelation('<colA>', '<colB>', lambda x: x >= 0.6) with "warning" unless a strict precondition is stated.
   - Enumerations/allowed sets:
     - If code checks membership or maps via specific allowed values, add:
       .isContainedIn('<col>', [allowed_values]) with "error" if the set is mandatory.
     - Example enumerations frequently appearing in this domain:
       - Binary indicators (integers 0/1): Debtor, Tuition fees up to date, Scholarship holder, Daytime/evening attendance\t
         -> .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral) (error)
         -> .isContainedIn('<col>', [0, 1]) (error)
       - Application order allowed values often in [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6]
       - Macro series allowed sets sometimes hard-coded, e.g.:
         GDP: [-4.06, -3.12, -1.7, -0.92, 0.32, 0.79, 1.74, 1.79, 2.02, 3.51]
         Unemployment rate: [7.6, 8.9, 9.4, 10.8, 11.1, 12.4, 12.7, 13.9, 15.5, 16.2]
         Inflation rate: [-0.8, -0.3, 0.3, 0.5, 0.6, 1.4, 2.6, 2.8, 3.7]
       - If your target is one of these, enforce .isContainedIn with "error".
   - Multi-column completeness when used together:
     - If the code uses this column jointly with others to compute ratios or aggregates, consider:
       .areComplete([list_of_cols]) (error) when perfect completeness is expected.
       Otherwise: .haveCompleteness([list_of_cols], lambda x: x >= threshold) (warning).

3) Domain defaults for student academic datasets (apply when consistent with assumptions/code):
   - Semester counts:
     - “Curricular units [1st|2nd] sem (enrolled|approved|credited|without evaluations)” are non-negative integers.
     - approved/credited/without evaluations cannot exceed enrolled (same semester).
     - Use:
       .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral) (error)
       .isNonNegative('<col>') (error)
       If target is approved/credited/without-evaluations:
         .isLessThanOrEqualTo('<target>', '<enrolled>') (error)
       If target is enrolled:
         .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<enrolled>', '<approved>') (error)
         .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<enrolled>', '<credited>') (error)
         .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<enrolled>', '<without evaluations>') (error)
   - Grades:
     - “Curricular units [1st|2nd] sem (grade)” are Fractional in [0, 20].
     - Use:
       .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.Fractional) (error)
       .hasMin('<col>', lambda x: x >= 0.0) (error)
       .hasMax('<col>', lambda x: x <= 20.0) (error)
   - Binary indicators:
     - Debtor, Tuition fees up to date, Scholarship holder, Daytime/evening attendance\t are 0/1 integers.
     - Use data type Integral and .isContainedIn('<col>', [0, 1]) (error).

4) Levels:
   - Use "error" for hard invariants: completeness=1.0, data type, non-negativity for counts, bounded grade ranges, categorical allowed sets, and cross-column inequalities (approved <= enrolled, etc.).
   - Use "warning" for softer/statistical expectations: completeness thresholds < 1.0, distinct count bands, correlations, quantiles, and min/max derived from normalization when not guaranteed by domain.
   - If using satisfies() for SQL-like conditions, wrap column names that include spaces or special chars in backticks and provide a constraint name; e.g.:
     .satisfies('`colA` <= `colB`', 'colA_le_colB', lambda x: x >= 0.99)

5) Implementation details:
   - The "suggestion" must be a string starting with "." so it can be chained from a Check, e.g., Check(...).isComplete(...).hasDataType(...).
   - Use exact column names from inputs, including spaces, parentheses, or trailing tabs. Wrap column names in single quotes inside the Python string.
   - For satisfies() expressions, always wrap column names in backticks: `...`.
   - Prefer ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral for integer counts, ConstrainableDataTypes.Fractional for floats/grades, and ConstrainableDataTypes.Numeric only when either int or float is acceptable.
   - For hasNumberOfDistinctValues, always use a band (never equality) and keep it a "warning".

6) Quantity and coverage:
   - Aim to cover the key invariants justified by the profile, code usage, and assumptions, typically including:
     - completeness (or completeness threshold)
     - data type
     - non-negativity for counts (if applicable)
     - domain bounds (e.g., grade range 0..20)
     - critical cross-column inequalities (e.g., approved/credited/without evals <= enrolled)
     - optional distributional checks (distinct count band, correlation if referenced)
   - Do not add unrelated constraints.

7) Linking assumptions:
   - For each constraint, include indices of all relevant assumptions that motivate it.
   - If a constraint is implied by both the profile and code usage, include all applicable assumption indices.
   - If logically required by code usage (e.g., ratios using enrolled as denominator imply <= enrolled), include it and link to the assumptions that mention the usage or relationship.

Examples of valid formatting:
- ".isComplete('Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)')"
- ".hasDataType('Curricular units 2nd sem (grade)', ConstrainableDataTypes.Fractional)"
- ".isNonNegative('Curricular units 1st sem (approved)')"
- ".isLessThanOrEqualTo('Curricular units 1st sem (approved)', 'Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)')"
- ".hasCorrelation('Curricular units 1st sem (approved)', 'Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)', lambda x: x >= 0.6)"
- ".hasNumberOfDistinctValues('Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)', lambda x: x >= 20 and x <= 30)"
- ".hasMin('Curricular units 2nd sem (grade)', lambda x: x >= 0.0)"
- ".hasMax('Curricular units 2nd sem (grade)', lambda x: x <= 20.0)"
- ".satisfies('`Curricular units 1st sem (approved)` <= `Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)`', 'ap1_le_en1', lambda x: x >= 0.99)"
- ".isContainedIn('Application order', [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6])"
- ".isContainedIn('GDP', [-4.06, -3.12, -1.7, -0.92, 0.32, 0.79, 1.74, 1.79, 2.02, 3.51])"

Output:
- Return only a Python list of dicts following the schema above.
- Each suggestion must be chainable (starts with ".") and use exact function names and column strings.

2026/01/14 12:20:22 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 31: Proposed new text for dataflow_inspector: You are given:
- code_script: A Python code snippet (often pandas/numpy heavy).
- target_column: A column name string to track (example: "Curricular units 1st sem (approved)").
- sink_variable: Optional. If provided, only include lines where the target ultimately affects this sink; if empty, consider the entire snippet.

Goal:
Return only valid JSON:
{ "sources": [{ "start_line": int, "end_line": int }, ... ] }

Each range is 1-based and inclusive, using exact line numbers from code_script. Merge contiguous lines into one range; non-adjacent lines must be separate.

What to include as sources:
Include every physical line that reads, writes, filters, transforms, merges, or otherwise propagates data that originated from the target column. This requires tracking the target column through alias variables and derived variables, transitively.

Detailed procedure:

1) Line numbering
- Split code_script by newline and number lines starting at 1. Count every line as it appears (including blanks and comments), because output must use these exact 1-based indices.

2) Identify the target column aliases
- Look for any Python variable assigned to the exact string target_column, e.g.:
  col_ap1 = 'Curricular units 1st sem (approved)'
  col_wo1 = "Curricular units 1st sem (without evaluations)"
  c1_noe = 'Curricular units 1st sem (without evaluations)'
  These variable names become alias variables for the target column.
- Also treat literal usages directly in indexing (df['<target_column>']) as direct uses even if no alias exists.
- If a variable is assigned from another alias (alias2 = alias1), add it to the alias set.
- Include the line(s) where the alias variable for the target column is defined in your result (this “passes through” the target).

3) Detect direct reads of the target column
Mark any line as a read if it uses the target column (by literal or alias) in a pandas indexing context or equivalent, for example:
- df[target_literal_or_alias]
- pd.to_numeric(df[alias], errors=...)
- df.loc[:, alias] or df.loc[mask, alias]
- df[ [alias, ...] ] when used to subset columns
- df[alias].astype(...), .map(...), .replace(...), .fillna(...), etc.
These lines are sources (reads) and also the roots for dependency propagation.

4) Build dataflow dependencies (forward slice from the target)
- Track variable assignments: For each line with an assignment (lhs = rhs), collect which rhs identifiers are used. If rhs uses:
  - the target column literal
  - any alias variables
  - any derived variables already known to depend on the target
  then lhs becomes a derived variable that depends on the target as well.
- Recognize pandas/numpy/python transformations as propagation:
  - Arithmetic and boolean ops (+, -, *, /, //, **, %, &, |, ^, ~, >, <, ==, !=, <=, >=)
  - Vectorized functions and methods: np.where, np.clip, np.tanh, np.sqrt, astype, to_numeric, replace, fillna, clip, mean, std, corr, map, cut, rank, isna, notna, isin, sum, max, min, round, abs, etc.
  - Aggregations/combos: concatenation, addition of Series, combining Semester 1 and 2 features, etc.
  - Indexing/slicing when it consumes a derived Series/DataFrame.
  - Merges/joins/groupby/concat/stack/unstack when a dependent variable is involved.
- Any line that computes a new variable from a dependent variable must be included.

5) Downstream usage and outputs
- Continue propagating dependency transitively: any variable depending on a dependent variable is also dependent.
- Include lines where dependent variables are:
  - used to create or modify DataFrame columns (e.g., output['dropout_risk_score'] = risk_score)
  - used in conditions, sorting, filtering, masking, binning (pd.cut), selection, or priority tiers
  - used in I/O (to_csv, json.dump) when the content written includes or is derived from a dependent variable
  - included in a list of columns for selection if the selected data derives from the dependent variable
- For multi-line expressions (wrapped in parentheses), include every physical line that is part of that expression if any part uses a dependent variable.

6) If sink_variable is provided
- Restrict to the path(s) that ultimately affect sink_variable only.
- Identify all lines where sink_variable is defined, assigned, or used as an output, and do a backward slice: include only ancestors in the dependency graph that originate from the target column and feed into the sink.
- Exclude other branches that stem from the target but do not reach the sink_variable.

7) Exclusions and clarifications
- Do not include imports, argparse boilerplate, or unrelated variable definitions that do not participate in any dependency path from the target.
- Do not include unrelated column name assignments.
- Include the alias assignment line for the target column even if it’s not directly in an indexing expression, provided that alias is later used to read the DataFrame.
- If the target column is never used, return an empty sources list.
- Comments alone do not count; if a line has both code and comment, consider the code only.
- When the target’s influence flows into other high-level variables (e.g., risk_score), include all lines that compute, transform, or output those downstream variables.

8) Merge and output ranges
- After collecting all dependent line numbers, sort them, merge contiguous runs into {start_line, end_line}.
- Output exactly:
{ "sources": [{ "start_line": int, "end_line": int }, ... ] }
- Do not output any additional text.

Typical patterns to catch (common in provided scripts):
- Aliases: c1_noe = 'Curricular units 1st sem (without evaluations)'; col_ap1 = 'Curricular units 1st sem (approved)'; col_en1 = 'Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)'
- Direct reads: ne1 = df[c1_noe].astype(float); ap1 = pd.to_numeric(df[col_ap1], errors='coerce').astype(int); en1 = pd.to_numeric(df[col_en1], errors='coerce').astype(int)
- Derived features that propagate dependency: pass_rate1 = np.where(en1 > 0, ap1 / en1.replace(0, np.nan), 1.0); noeval_ratio1 = np.where(en1 > 0, wo1 / en1.replace(0, np.nan), 0.0); success_gap_ratio1, total_approved = ap1 + ap2; progress = total_approved / total_enrolled
- Risk composition and outputs: risk_raw = ... + components(using dependent vars)...; risk_score = np.clip(risk_raw, 0.0, 1.0); output['dropout_risk_score'] = risk_score; priority_tier from pd.cut(risk_score); saving to CSV that includes these fields.

Remember: the evaluation expects you to include all transitive lines where the target column’s data flows, not just the initial read lines. If sink_variable is empty, consider the whole snippet; if provided, include only the target-to-sink paths.

2026/01/14 12:22:28 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 32: Proposed new text for dataflow_inspector: Task: Identify every line range in a Python code snippet where a specific DataFrame column is used, including full downstream propagation, and optionally restrict to a given sink.

Inputs:
- code_script: The full code snippet to analyze as a single string.
- target_column: The exact column name (string) to track. Match exact text, including whitespace or special characters (e.g., 'Daytime/evening attendance\t').
- sink_variable: Optional. If a non-empty string, return only lines on dependency paths from the target column to this sink. If empty or not provided, consider the entire snippet (no restriction).

Output:
- Return only valid JSON with this shape:
  { "sources": [ { "start_line": int, "end_line": int }, ... ] }
- Use exact 1-based line numbers from the provided snippet (count every line including blanks and comments).
- Merge adjacent lines into a single range. Non-adjacent lines must be separate ranges.
- Sort ranges by start_line.
- If nothing is found, return {"sources": []}.

What counts as a “use” of the target column:
Include a line if it does any of the following with the target column, directly or indirectly:
1) Reads the column from a DataFrame:
   - df[target_column], df["...exact name..."], df.loc[:, target_column], df.loc[mask, target_column], df[[target_column, ...]], df.filter(items=[target_column]), etc.
   - Type coercions and numeric parsing: pd.to_numeric(df[target_column], ...), df[target_column].astype(...), df[target_column].replace(...), fillna/clip/map/isna/notna, comparisons (==, !=, >, <), arithmetic with the series, value_counts, mean/std/corr, etc.
   - Any boolean mask or condition referencing the column (e.g., (df[target_column] == 1)).
2) Writes/renames/drops the column:
   - df.rename(columns={target_column: ...}), df.drop(columns=[target_column]), df.assign(**{target_column: ...}), df[target_column] = ..., df.loc[mask, target_column] = ..., etc.
3) Passes the column through selections or outputs:
   - Selecting or copying DataFrames that include it: out = df[[..., target_column, ...]].copy(), df[cols] where cols contains the target column, df.to_csv(...) when selecting columns that include the target column, assembling queue/output column lists that are then used to select/save from a DataFrame.
4) Uses variables or containers that alias the column name:
   - A Python variable assigned to the exact column string (e.g., col_gdp = 'GDP'). Treat this assignment as the alias definition.
   - Lists/tuples/dicts/sets that contain the alias or the literal column string and are later used in DataFrame operations (selection, rename, drop, to_csv, etc.).
5) Uses any variable derived from the column (“passes through” downstream):
   - Once a variable depends on the target column (via 1–4), every subsequent line that reads or transforms that variable is considered part of the column’s dataflow and must be included. Examples:
     - idx = df[col].map(mapping).astype(int)  → macro_stress = f(idx)  → macro_risk = g(macro_stress)  → risk_score = h(macro_risk)
     - All lines using idx, macro_stress, macro_risk, risk_score are included, because the target column’s value flows through them.
   - This includes arithmetic, numpy/pandas operations, np.where, pd.cut, groupby/agg using the derived variable, merges/joins if the derived variable is used to compute keys or features, concatenations if the derived variable is part of what’s concatenated, and any subsequent inclusion in outputs.

Special handling for sink_variable (dependency-restricted mode):
- If sink_variable is a non-empty string, include only the lines that are on any dependency path from the target column to the sink:
  1) Treat sink_variable as matching either:
     - A Python variable name (e.g., risk_score), or
     - A DataFrame column name (e.g., 'risk_score') when you see df['risk_score'] = ... or inclusion in output selections.
     - A variable that aliases a column name used in selections/outputs.
  2) Build a dependency graph:
     - Start from all lines that read the target column (directly or via its alias variable).
     - Propagate dependencies forward: any variable assigned from a dependent variable becomes dependent; any container (list/dict/etc.) that stores a dependent variable or alias becomes dependent; any DataFrame selection/output using a dependent container or variable is dependent.
     - Identify lines that define or update sink_variable (per the above matching).
     - Backtrack to include only lines that contribute to those sink lines (i.e., on a path from the target to the sink). Exclude branches that don’t lead to the sink.
- If sink_variable is empty or not provided, do not restrict; include the full downstream propagation of the target column across the entire snippet.

Pandas/NumPy patterns to include (non-exhaustive):
- Direct access: df[target], df["..."], df.loc[..., target], df.iloc[..., ...] when used with column positions that involve the target (only if identifiable), df.at[..., target].
- Casting/cleaning: pd.to_numeric(df[target], ...), df[target].astype(...), replace/fillna/clip/map/apply/pipe, comparisons/boolean masks, value_counts/mean/std/corr.
- Derived features: arithmetic on series derived from df[target], np.where(... uses derived), pd.cut of derived, normalizations, z-scores, logit transforms, etc.
- Column lists/containers: cols = [col_a, target_alias, ...]; later df[cols], output[cols], rename/drop with cols.
- Outputs: to_csv with a column selection including the target or its alias; selection into out/queue DataFrames that carry the target; writing df[target] = ...
- Renaming: df.rename(columns={target: ...}) and any subsequent use of the renamed column via its new name if clearly traceable.
- Merges/joins/groupby/pivot that include the target column’s values or use it as a key: df.merge(..., on=target_alias or 'target'); groupby(target_alias) if downstream is used.

What to ignore:
- Lines that do not read, write, transform, filter, select, or pass through the target column or any variable/container derived from it.
- Unused constants unrelated to the target column (e.g., numeric arrays like allowed_gdp that don’t touch df[target]).
- Comments or strings that merely mention the column name without any operational effect.
- Imports, OS/path setup, or other boilerplate unless they directly use a dependent variable from the target column (generally they won’t).

Procedure:
1) Split code_script into lines; index them 1-based.
2) Find alias variables that are assigned exactly to the target_column string. Record these aliases.
3) Scan top to bottom to build dependencies:
   - When a line reads df[alias] or df[target literal], mark it and mark the assigned LHS variable(s) as dependent.
   - If a dependent variable is used to compute another variable, mark that line and mark the new variable as dependent.
   - If a dependent alias or literal appears in containers (list/dict/etc.), mark the container as dependent. Mark downstream operations that use those containers (e.g., df[cols], to_csv with cols).
   - Continue transitive propagation until no new dependent variables/containers are found.
4) If sink_variable is provided, restrict to lines on paths that lead to sink_variable (as a Python variable or DataFrame column). Otherwise, include all lines touched during propagation.
5) Collect all line numbers marked for inclusion. Merge contiguous runs into {start_line, end_line} ranges. Sort ranges by start_line.
6) Output only the JSON per the required schema.

Edge cases and cautions:
- Match the target_column string exactly, including any whitespace or special characters.
- Consider both direct string usage and alias variables that hold the same exact string.
- Include lines where the column is selected for output, even if not transformed on that line.
- When unsure whether a line lies on the dependency path (especially under sink restriction), be conservative and include it.
- Line numbers must correspond exactly to the provided snippet (1-based), including blank lines and comments.

  ]

JSON numbers must not have leading zeros.

Goal:
Generate a concise, testable set of assumptions about the target column that:
- Reflect constraints implied by both the provided column description and the focused code.
- Emphasize explicit, validation-friendly statements (e.g., “values are integers in [0, 8]”) rather than vague language (avoid “mostly”, “generally”).
- Include key domain constraints commonly expected in this student academic/macro-risk context.
- Cite exact focused_code line ranges that support the assumption (list each as {start_line, end_line}). If an assumption comes only from target_column_desc (e.g., completeness=1.0, histogram bounds), leave the sources array empty.

How to derive assumptions (prioritize in this order):
1) From code usage:
   - Data type conversions imply data types (e.g., .astype(int) → Integral; .astype(float) or pd.to_numeric with Fractional values → Fractional).
   - Value bounds implied by computations (e.g., dividing by an enrollment count and clipping 0..1 indicates a ratio in [0, 1]; indexing arrays implies valid integer ranges; enumerated “allowed” lists imply membership constraints).
   - Inter-column relationships used in calculations (e.g., pass rates a/enrolled → implies approved ≤ enrolled; “without evaluations” ≤ enrolled; credited ≤ enrolled).
   - Downstream use (e.g., risk component weighting) is secondary but can be noted to justify why correctness matters.

2) From target_column_desc:
   - Completeness (e.g., completeness: 1.0 → “column has no missing values”).
   - DataType (Integral/Fractional).
   - Histogram min/max, distinct values → state a precise valid range when reasonable (e.g., values in [0, 8] if the histogram only shows 0..8). Use exact inclusive bounds when inferring from histogram.

3) Domain-specific expectations for common column names (use when consistent with code and descriptions):
   - Curricular units ... (enrolled/approved/credited/without evaluations):
     - Non-negative integer counts.
     - Valid range bounded by observed histogram min/max when provided.
     - Relationship constraints:
       - approved ≤ enrolled (expected to hold on at least 95% of rows).
       - credited ≤ enrolled (expected to hold on at least 95% of rows).
       - without evaluations ≤ enrolled (expected to hold on at least 95% of rows).
   - Application order:
     - Integer in [1, 9] when used to index a weight array with index 1..9.
   - Debtor, Tuition fees up to date, Scholarship holder, Daytime/evening attendance:
     - Binary integers {0, 1}.
   - Admission grade, Previous qualification (grade):
     - Numeric grades scaled by division by 200 in code → valid range [0, 200].
   - Inflation rate:
     - Fractional; may be negative or positive.
     - If code enumerates an allowed set, values must belong to that set.
   - GDP, Unemployment rate:
     - Numeric; if code enumerates an allowed set, values must belong to that set.
   - Age at enrollment:
     - Numeric (commonly float).

What to include for each assumption:
- A precise, atomic statement in plain language that can map to a constraint (e.g., isComplete, hasDataType, satisfies range, inter-column relationship).
- Cite code lines where the constraint is implied (e.g., lines with astype(int/float), pd.to_numeric, replace(0, np.nan), np.where, np.clip, array indexing, or explicit allowed lists). Use inclusive ranges; if a single line suffices, start_line=end_line.
- If the information comes only from target_column_desc (e.g., completeness=1.0; histogram indicates bounds), leave sources as [].

Common patterns to look for in code and convert to assumptions:
- astype('Int64') or astype(int) → Integral data type.
- astype(float) or division producing ratios → Fractional data type.
- replace(0, np.nan) before division; np.where(en > 0, ...) → implies denominators must be non-negative and that the numerator cannot exceed the denominator for valid pass rates.
- np.clip(x, 0.0, 1.0) → implies resulting metric is in [0, 1]; if the target column feeds such a ratio, state relevant preconditions.
- Indexing into a fixed-size array using column values (e.g., weights[app_order]) → implies an integer domain bound.
- Mapping with an allowed value list (e.g., map to rank dict built from an explicit list) → implies column values must be in that enumerated set.

Quality and coverage requirements:
- Always include:
  - Data type assumption (Integral or Fractional) supported by code or target_column_desc.
  - Completeness assumption if completeness is 1.0 in the description.
  - A precise valid range or membership constraint when the histogram or code makes it clear (e.g., [0, 8] for count-like columns; enumerated lists for macro columns).
- Include key inter-column relationship constraints when obvious from semantics and code usage (e.g., approved ≤ enrolled) and phrase them as expectations that should hold on at least 95% of rows when they are not strictly enforced in code.
- Avoid hedging terms like “mostly” or “generally.” Use definitive, testable wording.

Examples of precise assumption phrasing:
- “Values are integral counts in [0, 8].”
- “Column is complete (no missing values).”
- “Values must be integers in [1, 9] to validly index commitment weights.”
- “Approved units do not exceed enrolled units (expected ≥95% of rows).”
- “Values are fractional, may be negative, and must belong to the allowed set enumerated in code.”

Output formatting:
- Return exactly one JSON object with top-level key "assumptions".
- Each assumption is an object with keys "text" (string) and "sources" (array of {start_line, end_line}).
- Do not include any additional keys, commentary, or formatting outside the JSON object.

2026/01/14 12:26:30 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 34: Proposed new text for ir_generation: You generate PyDeequ validation constraints for a single target column using:
- target_column
- target_column_desc
- code_snippet
- downstream_task_description
- assumptions (zero-based indexed)

Goal:
Return a comprehensive list of constraint objects that will prevent downstream breakages and codify semantic expectations. Each constraint must be directly relevant to the target_column and how it is used in the code snippet. Include cross-column constraints only if the target_column participates in the relation.

Output format:
- Return only a JSON-like Python list of dicts, where each dict has:
  - "suggestion": PyDeequ code string that starts with "." (e.g., ".isComplete('col')", ".satisfies(...)", ".isLessThanOrEqualTo(...)")
  - "level": "error" or "warning"
  - "linked assumptions": [list of integer indices]

General rules:
- Use only the functions listed in the prompt (Row-Level and Aggregate-Level).
- Use ConstrainableDataTypes from pydeequ.checks.ConstrainableDataTypes.
- When using hasPattern, use raw regex strings r"..." (but avoid patterns unless truly needed).
- Use backticks around column names inside satisfies SQL conditions (e.g., '`A` <= `B`').
- Prefer the most specific built-in comparator:
  - Use .isLessThanOrEqualTo('A', 'B') instead of a .satisfies('`A` <= `B`', ...) when possible.
  - Use .isComplete('col') for strict non-nullness rather than .hasCompleteness(... == 1.0) unless you need a threshold.
- Choose severity:
  - level = "error" for constraints that, if violated, will break downstream code (casts, indexing, arithmetic) or violate core semantics (e.g., negative counts, approvals > enrollments).
  - level = "warning" for distributional or “typical” expectations (e.g., usual max based on histogram) that are not guaranteed.
- Assertion thresholds:
  - Use exact checks (== 1.0) when metadata/assumptions clearly guarantee the property (e.g., completeness 1.0, strict integer typing).
  - Use tolerant thresholds (e.g., >= 0.95 or >= 0.99) only for “likely/typical” assumptions.
- Link every constraint to the relevant assumption indices; include all that apply.

How to derive constraints:
1) From target_column_desc:
   - Data type:
     - If dataType=Integral → .hasDataType(target, ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral, lambda x: x == 1.0) [error].
     - If dataType=Fractional → .hasDataType(target, ConstrainableDataTypes.Fractional, lambda x: x == 1.0) [error].
     - If description suggests both ints and floats are acceptable, use ConstrainableDataTypes.Numeric with a tolerant threshold.
   - Completeness:
     - If completeness=1.0 → .isComplete(target) or .hasCompleteness(target, lambda x: x == 1.0) [error].
     - If not guaranteed, use a threshold and set [warning].
   - Avoid .hasNumberOfDistinctValues unless the assumption explicitly references a minimum/expected number of distinct values; if used, prefer [warning] and be aware it requires binningUdf/maxBins in practice.

2) From the code_snippet (domain- and usage-driven constraints):
   Inspect how the target_column is cast, indexed, or combined:
   - If cast to int or used as counts → add .isNonNegative(target) [error].
   - If used as a binary/indicator in arithmetic or boolean logic (e.g., 0/1) → .isContainedIn(target, [0, 1]) [error].
   - If used as grades normalized by dividing by 20 (common in this dataset):
     - .hasMin(target, lambda x: x >= 0) [warning or error depending on assumptions].
     - .hasMax(target, lambda x: x <= 20) [warning or error depending on assumptions].
   - If used to index arrays (e.g., commit_weights[app_order] relies on 1..9):
     - Ensure integer type and range:
       - .hasDataType(target, ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral, lambda x: x == 1.0) [error].
       - .hasMin(target, lambda x: x >= 1) [error].
       - .hasMax(target, lambda x: x <= 9) [error].
       - Alternatively, .isContainedIn(target, list(range(1, 10))) [error].
   - If the code defines an explicit allowed set and maps to ranks (e.g., GDP/Unemployment/Inflation lists):
     - For the target macro column appearing in those lists, enforce membership:
       - .isContainedIn(target, allowed_list) [error].
   - If the target is an approval/without-evaluations/credited count used with “enrolled” in ratios:
     - Enforce logical relationships with enrolled (use the semester-matching enrolled column):
       - .isLessThanOrEqualTo(target, '... (enrolled)') [error]
         Examples:
         - 'Curricular units 2nd sem (approved)' <= 'Curricular units 2nd sem (enrolled)'
         - 'Curricular units 1st sem (without evaluations)' <= 'Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)'
         - 'Curricular units 2nd sem (credited)' <= 'Curricular units 2nd sem (enrolled)' (use if consistent with assumptions/usage).
   - If the code does arithmetic that would fail on nulls (astype, division, boolean operations), completeness should be enforced:
     - .isComplete(target) [error] (if assumptions support it) or a high .hasCompleteness threshold otherwise.

3) Domain-specific knowledge for this dataset (leverage when applicable):
   - Grades: 0–20 scale; used as Fractional and normalized by /20. Enforce bounds.
   - Enrollment/approval/credited/without-evaluations: non-negative integers; approval and “without evaluations” should not exceed enrolled for the same semester.
   - Binary administration/attendance flags (if target): Debtor, Tuition fees up to date, Scholarship holder, Daytime/evening attendance are typically 0/1. Enforce 0/1 and integral type.
   - Application order: integers in 1..9 (array indexing relies on this).
   - Macro columns may be restricted to predefined lists if the snippet creates allowed_* arrays and maps them; otherwise treat as numeric with no fixed allowed set.

Quality and coverage:
- Favor a complete set of high-value constraints that reflect:
  - Type, completeness, and non-negativity (for counts).
  - Natural bounds (e.g., grades).
  - Cross-column relationships that keep ratios meaningful.
  - Allowed-value sets when used downstream (e.g., mapping/ranking/indexing).
- Avoid speculative or redundant constraints. Do not add constraints for columns unrelated to the target unless the relation includes the target.

Examples of good constraints per common target types:
- Grade column:
  - .isComplete(target) [error]
  - .hasDataType(target, ConstrainableDataTypes.Fractional, lambda x: x == 1.0) [error]
  - .hasMin(target, lambda x: x >= 0) [warning or error per assumptions]
  - .hasMax(target, lambda x: x <= 20) [warning or error per assumptions]
- “Approved/Without evaluations” column:
  - .isComplete(target) [error]
  - .hasDataType(target, ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral, lambda x: x == 1.0) [error]
  - .isNonNegative(target) [error]
  - .isLessThanOrEqualTo(target, '<matching enrolled column>') [error]
- Binary flag column:
  - .isComplete(target) [error]
  - .hasDataType(target, ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral, lambda x: x == 1.0) [error]
  - .isContainedIn(target, [0, 1]) [error]
- Application order:
  - .isComplete(target) [error]
  - .hasDataType(target, ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral, lambda x: x == 1.0) [error]
  - .hasMin(target, lambda x: x >= 1) [error]
  - .hasMax(target, lambda x: x <= 9) [error]
  - Or .isContainedIn(target, [1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8,9]) [error]

Implementation notes:
- Column names may include spaces, parentheses, or special characters; always wrap them in backticks inside .satisfies SQL strings.
- For cross-column checks, prefer .isLessThanOrEqualTo/.isGreaterThanOrEqualTo over .satisfies when available.
- Do not duplicate constraints or add constraints for columns not involving the target.
- Ensure the suggestion string is valid PyDeequ (no comments in the code string).
- Always populate "linked assumptions" with the indices that support the constraint.

2026/01/14 12:28:27 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 35: Proposed new text for dataflow_inspector: You are given:
- code_script: a Python code snippet as a single string, including blank lines and comments.
- target_column: the name of a data column to track.
- sink_variable: optional variable name. If empty, include the entire dependency chain for the target column; if provided, include only lines that ultimately affect that sink.

Your task:
Return the exact 1-based line ranges in code_script where the target column is used, transformed, filtered, merged, or propagated through derived variables. Output must be valid JSON only:
{ "sources": [ { "start_line": int, "end_line": int }, ... ] }

Key requirements:
1) Line numbering and output format
- Use exact 1-based line numbers as they appear in code_script (count every line, including blank lines and comments).
- Merge only adjacent selected lines into a single range. Non-adjacent lines must be separate ranges.
- Return only valid JSON with the top-level key "sources". No extra text.

2) What counts as “use” of the target column
Include a line if it:
- Reads the column from a DataFrame/Series (e.g., df['Col'], df[col_var], df.loc[:, 'Col'], df[[..., 'Col', ...]], df.filter/items selecting the column).
- Writes to the column or assigns it in a DataFrame.
- Filters on, compares, or applies boolean conditions using the column/Series derived from it.
- Transforms the column/Series (astype, map, to_numeric, arithmetic, functions).
- Passes the column/Series or any variable derived from it into another operation (aggregation, joins/merges on=, left_on/right_on, groupby, cut, rank, concat/merge, export).
- Exports or selects it into outputs (e.g., df[[..., col, ...]].copy(), to_csv including it).
- Defines constants or helper structures that are directly used to transform/map the target column (e.g., an allowed_values list or rank mapping used to map the target column’s values).

Do NOT include:
- Pure comments (unless on a line that also contains qualifying code).
- Unrelated code that does not depend on the target column or its derived variables.
- Helper constants not used in a transformation path that touches the target column.

3) Aliases and string matching for column names
- Columns are often stored in variables (aliases), e.g., infl_col = 'Inflation rate' or c2_crd = 'Curricular units 2nd sem (credited)'. Treat such assignments as part of the usage chain.
- Track all aliases: any variable assigned exactly to the target column string becomes an alias for that column.
- Be tolerant to minor whitespace discrepancies in target_column:
  - First, try exact match.
  - If not found, try a normalized match: trim leading/trailing whitespace (including tabs) and collapse runs of whitespace. Also perform case-insensitive match.
  - If multiple candidate literals match under normalization, prefer exact, then case-insensitive exact, then the one with the smallest normalized edit distance to target_column.
- When selecting lines, always match against the literal strings present in the code (including any trailing tabs) or alias variables bound to them.

4) Dependency tracing (transitive usage)
- Build a dependency graph of variable assignments. When a line defines X = <expr>, record that X depends on all identifiers in <expr>.
- Direct uses: any df[...] access using the target column literal or one of its aliases.
- Derived variables: any variable computed from the target column (directly or indirectly) is part of the chain. Include lines where these derived variables are defined and further used.
- Upstream helpers: if a transform of the target column uses helper objects defined elsewhere (e.g., infl_rank built from allowed_infl), include those helper definitions too, as they are inputs to the target column’s transformation.
- Multi-line statements: if a single logical statement spans multiple lines (e.g., with parentheses), include every line in that statement when it uses the target column or any variable derived from it.

5) Sink variable filtering
- If sink_variable is empty: include the entire transitive closure starting from the target column’s first use through all variables that depend on it, up to any outputs that consume them.
- If sink_variable is provided: include only lines on dependency paths that reach sink_variable. This means:
  - Identify all direct uses of the target column.
  - Follow dependencies forward only along paths that eventually reach sink_variable (by name).
  - Exclude branches that do not affect sink_variable.
  - If sink_variable is assigned multiple times, include all relevant assignments and their dependency chains that stem from the target column.

6) Typical patterns to capture (non-exhaustive)
- Aliases: col_infl = 'Inflation rate'
- Reading/typing: df[col_infl], pd.to_numeric(df[col_infl], ...), df['Inflation rate'].astype(...)
- Vectorized ops: series_from_target + other, clip, fillna, where, np.where, arithmetic
- Mapping/ranking used with the column: infl_rank = {...}; infl_idx = df[col_infl].map(infl_rank)
- Derived feature use: macro_stress = f(..., infl_idx, ...); macro_risk = g(macro_stress, ...)
- Inclusion in outputs: out = df[[..., col_infl, ...]].copy(); out.to_csv(...)
- Diagnostics using derived values: diag = {..., 'x': day_share, ...}
- Joins/merges: df.merge(..., on=col_alias) or left_on/right_on with col_alias or literal
- Selection/filtering: df[df[col_alias] > 0], df.query referencing col, isin, isna, value_counts, cut/binning using derived series
- Renames: df.rename(columns={col_alias_or_literal: ...})
- Note: If a helper constant (e.g., allowed_infl) is used to build a mapping that directly transforms the target column, include the helper’s definition and the mapping creation line.

7) Merging adjacent lines
- After identifying all individual lines, sort them and merge consecutive line numbers into contiguous ranges: {start_line, end_line}.
- Do not merge across gaps (even blank-line gaps).

8) Output
- Return only the JSON object: { "sources": [ { "start_line": int, "end_line": int }, ... ] }
- No additional commentary or fields.

Aim for high recall: include every line in the transitive path from the target column to its downstream uses (or to sink_variable if provided), including alias definitions and helper constructs directly used in transformations of the target column.

Your job is to generate clear, evidence-backed assumptions about that column. Your output must help downstream constraint generation/validation, so prefer precise, testable statements that align with both the column metadata and the way the code uses the column.

Inputs:
- target_column: The exact name of the target column.
- target_column_desc: A structured description with fields like completeness, approximateNumDistinctValues, dataType (Integral, Fractional, etc.), typeCounts, isDataTypeInferred, histogram.
- focused_code: A single code file with line numbers. It shows how columns are referenced and used.
- downstream_task_description: Optional free text. Often generic.

Output format:
- Return a single JSON object: { "assumptions": [ ... ] }
- Each assumption is an object: { "text": str, "sources": [{ "start_line": int, "end_line": int }] }
- "sources" should cite specific spans (one or more) from focused_code that support the assumption; use the actual line numbers from the provided code.
- If an assumption is derived solely from target_column_desc (and not the code), you may leave "sources": [].
- JSON numbers must not have leading zeros.

How to construct assumptions:
1) Always incorporate explicit metadata from target_column_desc:
   - If completeness == 1.0, include an assumption that the column is complete (no missing values).
   - Include the dataType (Integral, Fractional, etc.). If isDataTypeInferred is false, treat the provided dataType as authoritative.
   - Include approximateNumDistinctValues to contextualize range/variety (e.g., “~24 distinct values”).
   - These metadata-driven assumptions may have empty sources if not shown in the code.

2) Use focused_code to identify every place the target column is referenced or transformed:
   - Cite the line where the column symbol is defined (e.g., c1_cred = 'Curricular units 1st sem (credited)').
   - Cite lines where the column is coerced or cast (e.g., included in a numeric coercion list, pd.to_numeric(...), astype(int/float)).
   - Cite lines where the column participates in computations (e.g., denominators, ratios, differences, normalizations, clipping).
   - Include each relevant code location as a separate {start_line, end_line} entry in the "sources" array.

3) Derive domain and range expectations from code operations (be precise and cautious):
   - If the column is used as a numerator/denominator in a ratio and zeros are replaced with NaN to avoid division (e.g., e.replace(0, np.nan)), state that zero values are treated as undefined denominators and that downstream ratios are bounded [0, 1] via fillna/clip (cite those lines).
   - If the code divides values by a constant, infer likely scale:
     • Grades divided by 20.0 imply a 0–20 grading scale for “Curricular units ... (grade)”.
     • If a sum of two grades is divided by 400.0 (e.g., (admission + previous)/400.0), this suggests each of those inputs is on an approximately 0–200 scale. State this as “likely/expected scale” unless the desc confirms it.
   - If the column is a count (e.g., “enrolled”, “approved”, “credited”, “without evaluations”), state that values are expected to be non-negative and Integral. Cite integer casting and ratio usage where applicable.
   - Do not assert bounds that the code does not enforce. If domain knowledge suggests non-negativity but the code does not clamp values, phrase it as “expected (not enforced by code)”.

4) Align wording with common data constraints:
   - For completeness: “Column is complete (completeness=1.0; no missing values).”
   - For type: “Column values are Integral/Fractional as per metadata; code coerces to numeric for computation.”
   - For non-negativity of counts: “Values are expected to be >= 0 (counts), though code does not clamp negatives.”
   - For scale-normalized variables: “Values are used on a 0–1 scale via division by [constant] and clip/fillna.”
   - Only include such assumptions if grounded in desc and/or code.

5) Keep the assumptions focused on the target column:
   - Describe how the target column contributes to derived features or risk components (e.g., pass rates, credit ratios, preparedness risk, academic risk). Cite the exact lines where it is used.
   - Do not include assumptions about unrelated columns unless they are part of a formula directly involving the target column.

6) Evidence citation rules:
   - Use only actual line numbers from focused_code for sources.
   - Do not invent or use “line 0” or any line not present.
   - If multiple non-contiguous sections support one assumption, include multiple {start_line, end_line} entries.
   - If an assumption is from target_column_desc only, leave sources empty.

7) Be concise, testable, and avoid overreach:
   - Prefer short, declarative statements that could map to constraints (completeness, data type, non-negativity for counts, likely scale).
   - Qualify uncertain inferences with “likely” or “expected,” especially for ranges/scales not guaranteed by code.
   - Do not contradict the code; e.g., if code uses pd.to_numeric(errors='coerce'), acknowledge possible coercion to NaN for invalid entries.

Examples of good assumption patterns for this domain:
- “The column is complete (completeness=1.0; no missing values).” [desc-based; sources: []]
- “Column is Integral (per metadata) and is coerced to numeric in code before use.” [sources: lines where it appears in the numeric coercion list or astype(...)]
- “Used as denominator in pass/credit/no-evaluation ratios; zeros are replaced with NaN to avoid division by zero; resulting ratios are filled/clipped to [0, 1].” [sources: lines showing replace(0, np.nan), division, fillna/clip]
- “Values are expected to be non-negative counts; code does not clamp negatives.” [sources: lines indicating count usage; no clamp]
- “Scale normalization suggests a 0–20 grading scale for curricular unit grades.” [sources: lines dividing by 20.0]
- “Preparedness risk uses the absolute difference and sum with Previous qualification (grade), scaled by 50.0 and 400.0 respectively, implying each input is likely on a ~0–200 scale.” [sources: the prep_risk lines]

Final checks:
- Return exactly one top-level JSON with the “assumptions” array.
- Ensure all cited line numbers exist and correspond to the focused_code.
- Keep assumptions specific to the target column and grounded in the provided inputs.

Your task:

- the provided schema/metadata (target_column_desc), and/or

Output format:

- Each item in the "assumptions" array must be an object with:
  - "text": a concise assumption statement.
  - "sources": an array of { "start_line": int, "end_line": int } objects pointing to the smallest code span(s) that substantiate the assumption. If an assumption is derived solely from target_column_desc (metadata) and not from the code, set "sources": [].
- Do not include any extra top-level keys.
- JSON numbers (including line numbers) must not have leading zeros.

What to include (prioritize these, when supported by inputs):
1) Schema-level constraints (from target_column_desc; cite no code lines, i.e., sources: []):
   - Completeness: If completeness == 1.0, include an assumption that the column is complete (no missing values).
     Example text: "The 'X' column is complete (no missing values) based on metadata completeness of 1.0."
   - Data type: If dataType is provided (e.g., Integral), include an assumption that the column has that data type.
     Example text: "The 'X' column has data type Integral."
   - Cardinality/binary: If approximateNumDistinctValues == 2 and/or histogram indicates values {0,1}, include an assumption that it is binary and specify the observed values.
     Example text: "The 'X' column is binary with observed values {0, 1}."

2) Code-level usage and semantics (must cite tight line ranges from focused_code):
   - Numeric coercion: If the code coerces the target column with pd.to_numeric(...), .astype(int/float), include that assumption and cite the exact lines where it happens.
     Example text: "The 'X' column is coerced to numeric for computations."
   - Semantic mapping from equality checks: If the code checks (col == 0) or (col == 1), infer the meaning (e.g., 0 = not up to date, 1 = up to date) and cite those lines.
     Example text: "A value of 0 in 'X' indicates a negative/at-risk state, as used in risk calculations."
   - Role in formulas: If used as a numerator/denominator or in ratios, note that role and cite the lines. If it is a count, state that it is non-negative and typically bounded by related counts when that is implied by usage (clearly mark as an assumption if not enforced in code).
     Example text: "'X' is used as the numerator in the pass rate calculation for semester 2."
   - Impact on downstream features: If directly used to form a risk component (e.g., finance_risk, arrears), state that and cite only the necessary lines that demonstrate the dependency.
     Example text: "'X' contributes to the financial risk component when equal to 0."

How to choose and phrase assumptions:
- Mirror the exact target_column name in quotes within the text for clarity.
- Prefer short, factual statements that map to common validation checks:
  - Completeness (isComplete)
  - Data type (hasDataType, e.g., Integral)
  - Binary/allowed value set if evident
  - Numeric coercion
  - Usage in calculations (e.g., pass/fail rates) and expected bounds (e.g., approved ≤ enrolled) as domain assumptions when not explicitly enforced
- Do not invent or overstate facts. If the code does not explicitly enforce a constraint, phrase it as a domain assumption (e.g., "assumed non-negative counts") and still cite where the column is used to motivate the assumption.
- Use the tightest possible code line ranges that directly support the claim. Do not cite broad or unrelated spans.
- If a fact is only in the metadata (e.g., completeness: 1.0, dataType: Integral), include it with sources: [] rather than citing code.

Domain-specific patterns to look for in these scripts:
- Dropout/retention risk scoring pipelines combining:
  - Finance-related signals: e.g., "Tuition fees up to date" (0/1) and "Debtor" flags.
    • Typical semantics from code: 0 = not up to date (higher risk), 1 = up to date.
    • Often coerced to numeric and compared with 0/1 in risk formulas.

    • Approved/enrolled used to compute pass_rate; fail_rate = 1 - pass_rate.
    • Division by enrollment often uses replace(0, np.nan) to avoid division by zero.
    • Grades normalized by dividing by 20.0.
- Therefore:
  - For "Tuition fees up to date": include binary semantics, integral/numeric coercion, and its role in finance/arrears-related risk where shown.
  - For "Curricular units 2nd sem (approved)": include that it is an integer count, complete if metadata says so, used as the numerator for pass_rate2, and domain assumption that it is non-negative and bounded by enrollment when appropriate.

- Always include the schema-driven assumptions (completeness and data type) when provided by target_column_desc.
- Include code-backed assumptions with precise line ranges from focused_code.
- Keep the assumption text concise and directly tied to the evidence.

Do not:
- Fabricate line numbers or cite code lines that do not substantiate the claim.
- Omit the JSON wrapper { "assumptions": [...] }.
- Add any extra commentary or keys outside the required JSON structure.
