2026/01/14 12:34:59 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 38: Proposed new text for ir_generation: Task
Generate PyDeequ validation constraints for a single target column using:
- target_column (exact column name)
- target_column_desc (profile: completeness, approx distinct values, dataType)
- code_snippet (how the column is used downstream)
- downstream_task_description (optional context)
- assumptions (indexed list; cite indices in “linked assumptions”)

Goal
Return only a Python list of dicts. Each dict:
- "suggestion": a PyDeequ Check-builder call starting with "." (chainable method) or a function call if applicable. Use exact method names.
- "level": "error" or "warning"
- "linked assumptions": [list of integer indices from the assumptions that justify this constraint]

General strategy (read carefully)
1) Parse target_column_desc first to set base constraints:
   - Completeness:
     - If completeness == 1.0, add .isComplete('<col>') with level "error".
     - If completeness < 1.0 or not guaranteed, add .hasCompleteness('<col>', lambda x: x >= threshold) with level "warning". Choose threshold from assumptions/code; otherwise 0.95.
     - If the code casts with .astype(int) (not nullable) OR uses the column as an array index (e.g., weights[col]) OR any operation that would fail on NaNs (e.g., pd.cut without fill), then enforce .isComplete('<col>') with level "error" even if profile completeness is not 1.0.
   - Data type:
     - Map profile dataType to ConstrainableDataTypes: Fractional, Integral, Boolean, String, Numeric.
     - If code casts or relies on a specific numeric kind:
       - .astype(int)/.astype('int') => Integral.
       - .astype('Int64') => Integral but may allow nulls; still use Integral, and couple with completeness decision above based on downstream use.
       - .astype(float) or fractional arithmetic => Fractional.
       - If either int or float acceptable, use Numeric.
     - Add .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.<type>) with level "error".
   - Approx distinctness:
     - If approximateNumDistinctValues is present, add a banded check as "warning":
       .hasNumberOfDistinctValues('<col>', lambda x: x >= lower and x <= upper)
       where lower, upper define a reasonable band around the reported count:
       - Compute delta = max(5, round(0.2 * approxDistinct))
       - lower = max(0, approxDistinct - delta)
       - upper = approxDistinct + delta
       Avoid equality checks.

2) Analyze code_snippet for how the target column is used. Derive constraints:
   - Casting/conversions:
     - If the column is cast with .astype(int) or used as an index into an array (e.g., weights[col]), enforce Integral and .isComplete as "error".
     - If .astype(float) or fractional arithmetic is used, enforce Fractional.
   - Arithmetic and bounds:
     - If code computes normalized ratios or clips to [0, 1], add:
       .hasMin('<col>', lambda x: x >= 0.0) and .hasMax('<col>', lambda x: x <= 1.0)
       Use "warning" unless the domain clearly demands strict invariants.
     - For bounded domain metrics, add "error" bounds:
       - Grades (semester grades): [0, 20] exactly.
       - Admission grade / Previous qualification (grade) often scaled by /200.0, so raw domain [0, 200] if used directly.
     - For Application order:
       - If code uses indexing with a fixed mapping array (e.g., commit_weights[app_order]), enforce "error" allowed set 1..9:
         .isContainedIn('Application order', [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9])
       - If code only uses comparisons (e.g., app == 1) or clip(lower=1) with no indexing, prefer "warning" numeric bounds (min >= 1), unless assumptions specify hard domain.
   - Division-denominator usage:
     - If the column participates in ratios where denominator zeros are replaced with NaN (e.g., e.replace(0, np.nan)), do NOT enforce strictly positive; zero is allowed.
     - For count-like targets used in ratios, keep:
       .isNonNegative('<col>') (level "error").
   - Logical relationships and cross-column constraints:
     - Semester counts (domain defaults; see section 3):
       - If the target is approved/credited/without evaluations for a given semester, enforce that it cannot exceed the corresponding enrolled:
         .isLessThanOrEqualTo('<target>', '<enrolled_col>') (error).
       - If the target is enrolled, ensure it is >= each of approved/credited/without-evaluations for that semester:
         .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<enrolled_col>', '<approved_col>')
         .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<enrolled_col>', '<credited_col>')
         .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<enrolled_col>', '<without_eval_col>')
       - Use "error" for these logical invariants.
   - Proportion/threshold preconditions implied by code:
     - If code computes math.sqrt(p - t) or np.sqrt(p - t) for a share p without clamping, enforce p >= t:
       Use .satisfies with a fraction threshold on the event defining p. Examples:
       - share_first_choice = (app == 1).mean(); sqrt(share_first_choice - 0.60)
         => .satisfies('`Application order` = 1', 'share_first_choice_ge_0_60', lambda x: x >= 0.60) (error)
       - share_in_allowed = app.isin([1..6]).mean(); sqrt(share_in_allowed - 0.95)
         => .satisfies('`Application order` IN (1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6)', 'app_in_1_6_ge_0_95', lambda x: x >= 0.95) (error)
       - share_non_zero_noeval2 = 1 - (noeval2 == 0).mean(); sqrt(0.10 - share_non_zero_noeval2)
         => proportion non-zero <= 0.10
         => .satisfies('`Curricular units 2nd sem (without evaluations)` = 0', 'noeval2_zero_rate_ge_0_90', lambda x: x >= 0.90) (error)
     - Prefer these satisfies() constraints over isContainedIn for “mostly-in-set” logic.
   - Enumerations/allowed sets:
     - If the code maps by membership or relies on a fixed set (e.g., GDP, Unemployment rate, Inflation rate enumerations), enforce:
       .isContainedIn('<col>', [allowed_values]) (error).
     - Do not enforce a restricted set when the code merely measures the share within a subset; use satisfies as above.
   - Correlations used for modeling:
     - If code computes regression-like expectations or assumes strong positive correlation (e.g., approvals vs enrolled), add:
       .hasCorrelation('<colA>', '<colB>', lambda x: x >= 0.6) (warning unless assumptions make it strict).
   - Multi-column completeness:
     - If code uses multiple columns jointly (e.g., to compute ratios) and requires them to be present together, add:
       .areComplete([list_of_cols]) (error) if strictly required; otherwise:
       .haveCompleteness([list_of_cols], lambda x: x >= threshold) (warning).

3) Domain defaults for the student dataset (apply when consistent with assumptions and code)
   - Semester counts:
     - Columns like “Curricular units [1st|2nd] sem (enrolled|approved|credited|without evaluations)” are non-negative integers.
     - Approval/credited/without-evaluations cannot exceed enrolled (same semester).
     - Add:
       - .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral) (error)
       - .isNonNegative('<col>') (error)
       - If target is approved/credited/without-evaluations: .isLessThanOrEqualTo('<target>', '<enrolled>') (error)
       - If target is enrolled: .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<enrolled>', '<approved>'), etc. (error)
   - Grades:
     - “Curricular units [1st|2nd] sem (grade)” are numeric in [0, 20].
     - Add:
       - .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.Fractional) (error)
       - .hasMin('<col>', lambda x: x >= 0.0) (error)
       - .hasMax('<col>', lambda x: x <= 20.0) (error)
   - Binary indicators:
     - Debtor, Tuition fees up to date, Scholarship holder, Daytime/evening attendance are integers 0/1.
     - Add:
       - .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral) (error)
       - .isContainedIn('<col>', [0, 1]) (error)
   - Application order:
     - Values are integral.
     - If used as array index (e.g., commit_weights[app]), enforce exact set [1..9] (error).
     - Otherwise, prefer min bound >= 1 as "warning" unless assumptions specify hard limits.
   - Macro features:
     - Some scripts use fixed allowed sets for GDP/Unemployment/Inflation; if present in code/assumptions, enforce .isContainedIn with those enumerations (error). Otherwise, treat as Fractional/Integral numeric with no sign bound (e.g., Inflation can be negative).

4) Levels
   - Use "error" for strict invariants:
     - completeness=1.0 or required by code (.astype(int), indexing)
     - data type
     - non-negativity for counts
     - bounded grade ranges [0,20] and [0,200] for Admissions if used
     - categorical allowed sets (binary indicators, Application order 1..9 when indexing required, macro enumerations when enforced)
     - logical cross-column inequalities (approved <= enrolled, etc.)
     - satisfies-based preconditions from sqrt(...) constructs (e.g., share thresholds)
   - Use "warning" for statistical/soft expectations:
     - approximate distinct count bands
     - completeness thresholds < 1.0 when not strictly required
     - correlations
     - min/max when used only for normalization/clipping without strict domain rule

5) Referencing assumptions
   - For each constraint, include indices of relevant assumptions supporting it in "linked assumptions".
   - If multiple assumptions and code usage both support a constraint, include all applicable indices.
   - If a constraint is strongly implied by code (e.g., sqrt-share thresholds, array indexing requirements) but not explicitly stated, link to the assumption(s) that mention that logic or usage of the target, or the closest applicable assumption describing the downstream use.

6) Implementation details
   - The "suggestion" string must start with "." so it can be chained on a Check.
   - Use exact column names as they appear (including spaces, parentheses, or tabs). Wrap them in single quotes in the Python string.
   - For satisfies(), always wrap column names in backticks in the SQL expression. IN lists are allowed, e.g., '`Application order` IN (1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6)'.
   - Use ConstrainableDataTypes enum names exactly: ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral, .Fractional, .Boolean, .String, .Numeric.
   - Keep hasNumberOfDistinctValues as "warning" with a reasonable band as defined above.
   - Do not add unrelated constraints.

7) Quantity and coverage
   - Aim to cover all key invariants evidenced by the profile, assumptions, and code usage. Typically include at least:
     - completeness (or completeness threshold)
     - data type
     - non-negativity for counts
     - domain ranges for bounded metrics (grades)
     - critical cross-column inequalities (approved/credited/no-evals vs enrolled)
     - precondition shares derived from sqrt(...) constructs (e.g., app in [1..6] ≥ 95%, first-choice share ≥ 60%, no-eval2 zero share ≥ 90%)
     - optional distributional checks (distinct count band, correlation if used)
   - Keep levels aligned with strictness per the rules above.

Examples of valid suggestions formatting
- ".isComplete('Application order')"
- ".hasDataType('Curricular units 2nd sem (grade)', ConstrainableDataTypes.Fractional)"
- ".isNonNegative('Curricular units 1st sem (approved)')"
- ".isLessThanOrEqualTo('Curricular units 1st sem (approved)', 'Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)')"
- ".hasCorrelation('Curricular units 1st sem (approved)', 'Curricular units 1st sem (enrolled)', lambda x: x >= 0.6)"
- ".hasNumberOfDistinctValues('Application order', lambda x: x >= 2 and x <= 12)"
- ".hasMin('Curricular units 2nd sem (grade)', lambda x: x >= 0.0)"
- ".hasMax('Curricular units 2nd sem (grade)', lambda x: x <= 20.0)"
- ".isContainedIn('Application order', [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9])"
- ".satisfies('`Application order` IN (1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6)', 'app_in_1_6_ge_0_95', lambda x: x >= 0.95)"
- ".satisfies('`Application order` = 1', 'share_first_choice_ge_0_60', lambda x: x >= 0.60)"
- ".satisfies('`Curricular units 2nd sem (without evaluations)` = 0', 'noeval2_zero_rate_ge_0_90', lambda x: x >= 0.90)"

Output
Return only the list of constraint dicts.

2026/01/14 12:37:13 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 39: Proposed new text for ir_generation: You are given a single target column and supporting context. Your job is to generate high‑quality PyDeequ constraints for that column by combining:
- the semantic assumptions provided,
- the actual way the column is used in the code snippet,
- and metadata in target_column_desc.

Follow these rules.

Objective
- Return a list of constraint objects for the single target_column.
- Each object: {"suggestion": PyDeequ code string (must start with "." or a function), "level": "warning"|"error", "linked assumptions": [indices]}.
- Prefer constraints that will catch real data issues without being overly brittle.

Inputs (all provided)
- target_column: exact column name to validate (may include spaces/special chars).
- target_column_desc: profile for the target column (completeness, dataType, histogram, etc.).
- code_snippet: the user’s Python code where columns are read/used (Pandas/Numpy).
- downstream_task_description: may be empty; do not rely on it.
- assumptions: list of textual assumptions about the target column. Always map constraints back to these indices.

Available functions
- Only use the functions listed in the prompt (row-level and aggregate-level). Do not invent new ones.

General generation strategy
1) Start from hard invariants explicitly implied by the assumptions or code usage:
   - Completeness: If metadata shows completeness = 1.0 or assumptions say “complete”, add .isComplete(column) with level="error".
   - Data type: If assumptions/desc say counts/integers -> .hasDataType(column, ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral); for real numbers -> Fractional/Numeric; for strings -> String. Use level="error".
   - Non-negativity: If the values are counts, or a mask, or any quantity that cannot be negative, add .isNonNegative(column) with level="error".

2) Add relational constraints derived from code usage:
   - If target_column appears as a numerator when computing a ratio with a denominator column (e.g., pass_rate = approved / enrolled.replace(0, np.nan)):
     - Enforce upper bound by relationship to the denominator:
       .isLessThanOrEqualTo(numerator, denominator)
     - If appropriate, also assert implication when denominator is zero that numerator is zero using .satisfies (see “SQL conditions” below).
   - If target_column appears as a “without evaluations”, “approved”, or “credited” count alongside “enrolled”, enforce:
     - .isLessThanOrEqualTo(target_column, '... (enrolled)')
     - Optionally, if strongly implied by domain: approved + without_evaluations <= enrolled via .satisfies.
   - If target_column is used as a binary flag (cast to int or compared to 0/1), enforce:
     - .hasDataType(column, ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral)
     - .isContainedIn(column, [0, 1])
   - If code defines an explicit allowed set/list for a column (e.g., GDP/unemployment/inflation allowed lists, application order 1..6, target classes):
     - Use .isContainedIn with that exact set.
     - Examples pulled from code patterns:
       • target: .isContainedIn('Target', ['Graduate','Dropout','Enrolled'])
       • Application order: .isContainedIn('Application order', [1,2,3,4,5,6])
       • GDP/Unemployment rate/Inflation rate: exact lists defined in code.
   - If target_column is a grade scaled by 20 (e.g., df['... grade']/20.0), assert value range using:
     - .hasMin(column, lambda x: x >= 0)
     - .hasMax(column, lambda x: x <= 20)

3) Use target_column_desc to refine constraints:
   - If dataType is provided and not inferred (isDataTypeInferred: false), prefer .hasDataType with level="error".
   - If completeness is 1.0, add .isComplete with level="error"; if close to 1.0, prefer .hasCompleteness(column, lambda x: x >= 0.99) with level="warning".
   - Avoid guessing hard min/max unless the code or assumptions make it explicit (e.g., grades 0–20). Do not assert an arbitrary upper bound for counts unless clearly stated.

4) SQL conditions via .satisfies:
   - Use backticks around column names with spaces/special characters: '`Curricular units 2nd sem (approved)`'.
   - Name constraints descriptively in snake_case.
   - For near‑certain invariants that may tolerate tiny noise, add an assertion lambda with threshold 0.99 (or 0.95 for softer expectations).
   - Examples:
     - .satisfies('`approved` <= `enrolled`', 'approved_le_enrolled', lambda x: x >= 0.99)
     - Implication when denominator is zero:
       .satisfies('(`enrolled` <> 0) OR (`approved` = 0)', 'approved_zero_when_enrolled_zero', lambda x: x >= 0.99)

5) Levels:
   - error: hard rules grounded in domain or code (completeness 1.0, data type, non‑negativity, approved<=enrolled, binary in {0,1}, explicit allowed sets).
   - warning: softer thresholds (e.g., hasCompleteness >= 0.99 when not guaranteed), distributional checks, or min/max not strictly guaranteed.

6) Mapping to assumptions:
   - For each suggested constraint, include indices of all assumptions that justify it.
   - If a constraint is derived primarily from code usage, still include the assumption that states the semantic meaning (e.g., “counts, integral, non‑negative”) plus any assumption mentioning its role (numerator/denominator).

7) Practical details:
   - Ensure suggestions are valid PyDeequ Check builder calls, starting with a dot (e.g., ".isComplete('col')") or a function in the catalog.
   - Use ConstrainableDataTypes from pydeequ.checks for hasDataType.
   - Use raw strings (r"...") only for regex in hasPattern.
   - Avoid duplicates and contradictory constraints.
   - Prefer a concise, high‑signal set (typically 3–8 constraints) over an exhaustive but noisy list.

Domain-specific guidance for common columns in the provided scripts
- Curricular units X sem (enrolled): integral, complete, non‑negative; often a denominator for “approved/credited/without evaluations”; other counts should be <= enrolled. When enrolled == 0, other related counts should be 0 (use satisfies implication).
- Curricular units X sem (approved/credited/without evaluations): integral, complete, non‑negative; each <= corresponding “enrolled”; optionally, approved + without_evaluations <= enrolled via satisfies.
- Curricular units X sem (grade): numeric in [0, 20]. Use hasMin >= 0 and hasMax <= 20.
- Application order: allowed integers 1..6. Use isContainedIn(..., [1,2,3,4,5,6]) and Integral data type.
- Daytime/evening attendance\t: binary 0/1 integer. Use isContainedIn([0,1]) and Integral.
- Debtor, Tuition fees up to date, Scholarship holder: binary 0/1 integer. Use isContainedIn([0,1]) and Integral.
- Target: categorical in {'Graduate','Dropout','Enrolled'}. Use isContainedIn with these exact strings.
- GDP, Unemployment rate, Inflation rate: values must be in the explicit numeric lists defined in code. Use isContainedIn with those lists.

Examples of robust constraint patterns to reuse
- Completeness: .isComplete('col') or .hasCompleteness('col', lambda x: x >= 0.99)
- Type: .hasDataType('col', ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral)
- Non-negativity: .isNonNegative('col')
- Pairwise bound: .isLessThanOrEqualTo('colA', 'colB')
- Allowed set: .isContainedIn('col', [allowed_values])
- Conditional rule: .satisfies('(<SQL condition>)', 'descriptive_name', lambda x: x >= 0.99)

Output
- Return a JSON-like Python list of dicts, each with "suggestion", "level", and "linked assumptions".
- Ensure the “suggestion” strings are directly appendable to a PyDeequ Check chain.

Your task:
Identify every line range in code_script (1-based line numbers) where the target column is read, written, filtered on, transformed, merged, or where its derived values subsequently flow through computations and outputs. Return only valid JSON with the exact line ranges.

Output format (strict JSON only):
{ "sources": [ { "start_line": int, "end_line": int }, ... ] }

Key requirements:
1) 1-based line numbers:
   - Count every line in the snippet from the first line as 1, including blank lines and comments.
   - Ranges are inclusive [start_line, end_line].
   - Merge adjacent lines into a single range. Non-adjacent lines must be separate ranges.
   - Sort ranges by start_line.

2) Exact column matching:
   - Match target_column exactly (including punctuation and whitespace like the trailing tab in "Daytime/evening attendance\t").
   - Do not normalize or trim column strings.

3) Alias detection (column name variables):
   - Track variables assigned to the target column string, e.g.:
     c2_woe = 'Curricular units 2nd sem (without evaluations)'
     gdp_col = 'GDP'
   - Treat such alias definition lines as sources if the alias is later used to access/manipulate the DataFrame for this column.
   - Propagate aliases through simple reassignments (e.g., x = c2_woe; y = x).
   - Track when the target column is included in lists/tuples/dicts of columns (e.g., cols = [gdp_col, ...]) used later in DataFrame operations.

4) Identify all direct uses of the target column in pandas/numpy patterns, including but not limited to:
   - Indexing/access:
     df['Target'], df[target_alias], df.loc[:, 'Target'], df.loc[mask, 'Target'], df[['Target', ...]], df.filter(items=[..., 'Target', ...])
   - Writes:
     df['Target'] = ..., df.loc[mask, 'Target'] = ...
   - Aggregations/transformations on the column or Series extracted from it:
     .astype(), pd.to_numeric(...), .fillna(), .clip(), .mean(), .std(), .corr(), .value_counts(), arithmetic on the Series, np.where with the Series, etc.
   - Use in masks/filters/sorts:
     mask = df['Target'] > 0, df[df['Target'] > 0], sort_values(by=['Target']), query expressions that reference the column
   - Groupby/merge/index interactions:
     groupby(by=['Target']), merge(..., on='Target' or left_on/right_on uses), set_index('Target'), reset_index when it moves ‘Target’ between index/columns, rename(columns={...}) that either references or renames the target column.
   - Selection for output:
     df[[..., 'Target', ...]], building lists of columns to write to CSV that include the target or variables derived from it.

5) Dataflow/taint propagation (pass-through):
   - Treat any variable/Series/DataFrame produced by reading the target column as "tainted".
   - Any line whose right-hand side (RHS) uses a tainted variable is part of the chain. Mark that line and propagate taint to its outputs (new variables, Series, DataFrames, columns).
   - Continue propagation through:
     - Arithmetic and numpy operations: +, -, *, /, np.where, np.clip, np.tanh, etc.
     - Pandas ops: .fillna, .clip, .replace, .astype, .mean, .std, .corr, .map, .rank, .cut, pd.concat, merges/joins, sort_values, groupby, aggregations, etc.
     - Assignments to new DataFrame columns (e.g., df['risk_score'] = tainted_expr).
     - Passing tainted variables into function calls: include the call line; treat the function’s return as tainted if captured.
     - Using tainted variables in conditions for filtering/slicing/masking.
   - Include downstream lines where tainted variables are used to build outputs written to disk (e.g., to_csv) or artifacts. If a DataFrame created for output includes any tainted columns, include the line that writes it.
   - Do NOT include function definitions (def ...) that do not themselves use/transform the tainted data; include the call sites instead.

6) Renames:
   - If the target column is renamed (df.rename(columns={'GDP': 'GDP_new'})), treat the new name as tainted and continue tracking lines that reference the new name.
   - Include the rename line itself.

7) Constants vs taint:
   - Pure constant lines (e.g., allowed value lists, dicts of ranks) are NOT sources unless they also use a tainted variable on the same line.
   - Exception: include the alias definition line where a variable is assigned the target_column string literal (because it participates in subsequent access to the column).

8) Sink handling:
   - If sink_variable is empty: include all lines in the forward taint chain starting from the earliest read/write of the target column (plus alias definitions as noted).
   - If sink_variable is provided:
     - Identify the sink: it may be a variable name (e.g., risk_score) or a DataFrame column name (e.g., df['risk_score'] or 'risk_score' in selection/output).
     - Perform a backward slice from the final computation/write of the sink: include only lines in the taint chain that ultimately influence the sink.
     - Exclude branches derived from the target column that never affect the sink.
     - Include output lines that write/emit the sink (e.g., to_csv including the sink column).

9) Typical domain columns and patterns you may see (do not hardcode; recognize them if present):
   - 'GDP', 'Unemployment rate', 'Inflation rate'
   - 'Application order', 'Debtor', 'Tuition fees up to date', 'Scholarship holder'

   - These derived variables must be marked if they depend (directly or indirectly) on the target column.

10) Ambiguity rule:
   - When in doubt, prefer recall: if a line plausibly depends on the target column via the taint chain, include it.

11) Final JSON only:
   - Return exactly: { "sources": [ { "start_line": int, "end_line": int }, ... ] }
   - No extra keys, no explanations, no trailing commas.
   - If no lines qualify, return { "sources": [] }.

- target_column (exact column name)

- code_snippet (how the column is used downstream)
- downstream_task_description (optional context)

Output format:
- Return a Python list of dicts. Each dict must have:
  - "suggestion": a PyDeequ Check-builder call starting with "." (chainable method) or function (if applicable). Use the function names exactly as provided in the Available Functions lists.
  - "level": "error" or "warning"
  - "linked assumptions": [list of integer indices from the assumptions that justify this constraint]
- Return only the list of constraint dicts (no extra commentary).

General strategy:
1) Parse target_column_desc first to set base constraints:
   - Completeness:
     - If completeness == 1.0, add .isComplete('<col>') with level "error".
     - If completeness < 1.0 or not guaranteed, add .hasCompleteness('<col>', lambda x: x >= threshold) with level "warning". Choose threshold from the description or a conservative default (e.g., 0.95) if not stated.
     - Note: If the code uses pd.to_numeric(..., errors='coerce'), do not weaken completeness based on that; rely on the provided profile statistics (we validate the source data).
   - Data type:
     - Map profile dataType to ConstrainableDataTypes: Fractional, Integral, Boolean, String, Numeric.
     - Add .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.<type>) with level "error".
   - Approx distinctness:
     - If approximateNumDistinctValues is present, add a banded distinct-count check as "warning":
       .hasNumberOfDistinctValues('<col>', lambda x: x >= lower and x <= upper)
       where lower and upper are ±max(20% of value, 5 counts) around the provided value. Avoid equality checks.

2) Analyze code_snippet to derive additional constraints:
   - Type coercions:
     - If the column is cast to int (e.g., .astype(int) or 'Int64'), enforce Integral (error).
     - If used in float arithmetic or normalization, enforce Fractional (error).
   - Bounds and normalization:
     - If code normalizes a grade by dividing by 20.0 (e.g., x/20.0), enforce grade domain:
       .hasMin('<col>', lambda x: x >= 0.0) (error)
       .hasMax('<col>', lambda x: x <= 20.0) (error)
     - If code normalizes by dividing by 200.0 (common for Admission grade or Previous qualification (grade)), enforce the 0..200 domain similarly (typically error if clearly implied by code).
     - If the code only uses the column in a way that does not reveal its scale (e.g., used in sums or differences without explicit normalization), do not assume a [0, 20] domain; skip hard min/max unless an assumption explicitly states the bounds. When in doubt, omit min/max or use softer "warning" thresholds only when justified.
     - For numeric [0, 1] normalization/clip of the target itself, prefer:
       .hasMin('<col>', lambda x: x >= 0.0) and .hasMax('<col>', lambda x: x <= 1.0) (usually "warning" unless specified as invariant).
   - Division denominators and non-negativity:
     - If the column is a count used as a numerator or denominator and zeros are replaced with NaN to avoid division-by-zero, do not enforce positivity (> 0). Enforce non-negativity instead:
       .isNonNegative('<col>') (error) for counts.
   - Logical cross-column relationships:
     - For semester counts:
       - If target is approved/credited/without-evaluations, enforce ≤ enrolled for the same semester:
         .isLessThanOrEqualTo('<target>', '<enrolled>') (error)
       - If target is enrolled, enforce enrolled ≥ each of approved/credited/without-evaluations:
         .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<enrolled>', '<approved>') (error), etc.
     - If the code references or relies on correlations (e.g., approvals vs enrollment), add:
       .hasCorrelation('<colA>', '<colB>', lambda x: x >= 0.6) (warning unless assumptions mark it as a strict precondition).
   - Enumerations/allowed sets:
     - If the code uses .isin([...]) or array indexing that assumes a fixed domain (e.g., Application order 1..9 for commitment weights), enforce:
       .isContainedIn('<col>', [allowed_values]) (error if mandatory).
   - Multi-column completeness for joint usage:
     - If the code uses a group of columns together to compute ratios/features and implicitly expects high joint presence, add:
       .areComplete([list_of_cols]) (error) when all are expected present; otherwise:
       .haveCompleteness([list_of_cols], lambda x: x >= threshold) (warning).

3) Domain defaults for the student dataset (apply only when consistent with assumptions and code):
   - Semester counts (“Curricular units [1st|2nd] sem (enrolled|approved|credited|without evaluations)”):
     - Non-negative integers:
       .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral) (error)
       .isNonNegative('<col>') (error)
     - Cross-inequalities (same semester):
       - For approved/credited/without evaluations:
         .isLessThanOrEqualTo('<target>', '<enrolled>') (error)
       - For enrolled:
         .isGreaterThanOrEqualTo('<enrolled>', '<approved>'), etc. (error)
   - Course grades (“Curricular units [1st|2nd] sem (grade)”):
     - Fractional with hard bounds in [0, 20]:
       .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.Fractional) (error)
       .hasMin('<col>', lambda x: x >= 0.0) (error)
       .hasMax('<col>', lambda x: x <= 20.0) (error)
   - Admission/previous qualification grades:
     - Often normalized by 200.0 or via sums scaled by 400.0 in code. Only enforce [0, 200] if code or assumptions clearly indicate this scale. Do not assume [0, 20] for these columns unless explicitly evidenced.
   - Binary indicators (e.g., Debtor, Tuition fees up to date, Scholarship holder, Daytime/evening attendance\t):
     - Integers 0/1:
       .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral) (error)
       .isContainedIn('<col>', [0, 1]) (error)
   - Application order:

       .hasDataType('<col>', ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral) (error)
       .isContainedIn('<col>', [1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8,9]) (error)

4) Levels:
   - Use "error" for strict invariants: completeness=1.0, data type, non-negativity for counts, bounded course-grade ranges (0..20), categorical allowed sets, and logical cross-column inequalities (approved ≤ enrolled, etc.).
   - Use "warning" for statistical/distributional expectations or soft thresholds: approximate distinct counts, completeness < 1.0 thresholds, correlations, and min/max when not strictly guaranteed.
   - For satisfies() with SQL-like conditions, use backticks around column names with spaces/special chars:
     .satisfies('`colA` <= `colB`', 'colA_le_colB', lambda x: x >= 0.99)

5) Referencing assumptions:
   - For each constraint, include indices of relevant assumptions in "linked assumptions".
   - If a constraint is implied by both the profile and code usage, include all applicable assumption indices.
   - If a constraint is strongly implied by code usage (e.g., approved ≤ enrolled, or normalization by 20/200), include it even if not explicit in assumptions; link to the assumption(s) that mention the usage or relationship.

6) Implementation details:
   - The "suggestion" string must start with "." so it can be chained on a Check object (e.g., Check(...).isComplete(...).isNonNegative(...)).
   - Use exact column names as they appear (including spaces, tabs, or parentheses). Wrap them in single quotes inside the Python string.
   - For satisfies(), always wrap column names in backticks in the SQL expression.
   - Prefer ConstrainableDataTypes.Integral for integer counts, ConstrainableDataTypes.Fractional for grades/floats, and ConstrainableDataTypes.Numeric only when either int or float is acceptable.
   - If you use hasNumberOfDistinctValues, keep it as a "warning" with a reasonable band.

7) Quantity and coverage:
   - Aim to cover all key invariants evidenced by the assumptions and code usage. That typically includes:
     - completeness (or completeness threshold)
     - data type
     - non-negativity for counts
     - domain ranges for bounded metrics (e.g., course grades 0..20; admission/previous grades 0..200 only when shown)
     - critical cross-column inequalities (approved/credited/without-evaluations vs enrolled)
     - optional distributional checks (distinct count band, correlation if used)
   - Do not add constraints that are not supported by the profile, code, or assumptions. In particular, do not assume a 0..20 bound for Admission grade or Previous qualification (grade) unless the code or assumptions clearly indicate that scale.

2026/01/14 12:47:49 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 43: Proposed new text for dataflow_inspector: Task: Identify all line ranges in a Python code snippet where a specified DataFrame column is used or where values derived from that column propagate through subsequent computations, optionally limited to a given sink variable.

Inputs
- code_script: The complete Python code snippet as a single string. Use exact 1-based line numbering starting from the first line of this string. Count every physical line including blank lines and comments (but see “What counts as usage”).
- target_column: The exact column name to track. Matching is case-sensitive and must be exact, including whitespace (e.g., trailing spaces/tabs are part of the name).
- sink_variable: Optional name of a Python variable to constrain the result to the usage path that ultimately affects this sink. If empty or missing, consider all propagation paths.

Output
- Return only valid JSON of the form:
  { "sources": [{ "start_line": int, "end_line": int }, ... ] }
- The list must be sorted by start_line ascending.
- Merge adjacent lines into a single range (i.e., if you mark lines 10,11,12, output one range {"start_line":10,"end_line":12}). Non-adjacent lines must be separate ranges.
- If nothing matches, return {"sources": []}.
- Do not include any extra keys, text, or explanations.

What counts as usage (include these lines)
A. Direct column access
- Any executable line that reads, writes, filters, transforms, merges, or otherwise accesses the target column from a DataFrame, e.g.:
  - df['Target'], df[[..., 'Target', ...]], df.loc[:, 'Target'], df.loc[mask_on_Target]
  - df[target_alias] where target_alias is a variable whose value is exactly target_column
  - df.rename(columns={'Target': ...}) or df.rename(columns={...: 'Target'}) if it affects target_column
  - pd.to_numeric(df['Target'], ...), df['Target'].astype(...), df['Target'].map(...), df['Target'].isna(), any Series operation off df['Target']
  - Any filtering/boolean masks or conditions that reference the target column: df[df['Target'] > x], (df['Target'] == y), etc.
  - Selecting or projecting columns where the target is included: out = df[['Target', ...]] (include that line).

B. Column aliasing
- Any line that defines an alias variable to the exact target column name, e.g.:
  - col_gdp = 'GDP'
  - columns = [..., 'GDP', ...]  (mark the assignment line and taint the container variable)
- Any later use of that alias/container that results in accessing the DataFrame with the target column (as per A) is also usage.

C. Propagation through derived variables (taint propagation)
- After a line reads the target column (A) or defines an alias/container for it (B), mark any variables assigned on that line as “tainted.”
- For every subsequent line, if it uses any tainted variable on the right-hand side (RHS), include that line and mark any left-hand side (LHS) assigned variables as tainted.
  - Examples: gdp_idx = df[col_gdp].map(...); macro_stress = f(gdp_idx, ...); macro_risk = g(macro_stress); raw_score = ... + 0.10 * macro_risk; risk_score = h(raw_score); priority = pd.cut(risk_score, ...); urgent_flag = (risk_score >= 70) | ...
- Continue propagating taint transitively through computations, function calls, arithmetic, boolean logic, indexing, list/dict construction, etc.
- If a tainted variable is reassigned on a line that does not use any tainted inputs (i.e., it’s overwritten from clean sources), treat it as no longer tainted after that assignment.

D. Output assignment lines
- Include lines that assign tainted values into output structures or columns (e.g., out['risk_score'] = risk_score if risk_score is tainted).
- Include lines that select DataFrame columns using lists/aliases that include the target column (e.g., out = df[[..., target_alias, ...]]).
- Do NOT include generic I/O calls like to_csv/to_json unless the call itself directly references the target column or a tainted value in the same statement (e.g., df[['Target']].to_csv(...)). If it just writes an already-built object without referencing tainted symbols in that line, exclude it.

E. What to ignore
- Comments and docstrings even if they contain the target column name.
- Strings that mention the target column but are not used to access/alias columns or are not assigned to variables used later (e.g., logging strings).
- Configuration/constants that do not depend on the target column (e.g., allowed_gdp = [...]) unless they are on the same line as a tainted computation (they usually are not).
- Unrelated code paths that do not consume tainted variables.

Sink-variable constraint (when sink_variable is provided)
- Treat sink_variable as the “end” of interest. Include only lines that are on at least one dependency path from the target column to the sink_variable.
  - Practically: build dependencies via the taint propagation above, then backward-filter to retain only tainted lines/variables that can reach sink_variable uses/assignments.
- Match sink_variable exactly (case-sensitive) against Python variable names in the code. It is NOT a column name.
- If the target column never reaches the sink_variable, return {"sources": []}.

Matching details and edge cases
- Matching of target_column is exact and case-sensitive, including whitespace and tabs.
- Track aliases and containers:
  - If a variable is assigned a list/dict/tuple that includes the exact target column string, mark that variable as tainted as a “column container.” When such a container is later used to index/select DataFrame columns, that line is usage and the result becomes tainted.
- Multi-line statements: mark only the physical lines that actually reference tainted symbols or directly access the target column. Then merge consecutive marked lines into ranges as required.

Validation checklist before returning
- Use 1-based line numbers from the provided code_script.
- Include all direct column accesses, alias definitions, and all downstream lines that are computed from tainted variables (transitive closure), subject to the sink_variable restriction if provided.
- Merge adjacent lines into ranges; sort by start_line.
- Output strictly valid JSON with the single top-level key "sources".

2026/01/14 12:50:07 INFO dspy.teleprompt.gepa.gepa: Iteration 44: Proposed new text for assumption_generation: You are given:
- target_column: the exact name of the column to focus on.
- target_column_desc: metadata for that column (completeness, dataType, etc.).
- focused_code: a code snippet with 1-based line numbers.
- downstream_task_description: optional context (may be empty).

Your task:
Generate a list of concrete, testable assumptions about target_column that can be validated as data quality/semantic constraints and usage expectations in the given codebase.

Output format:
- Return a single JSON object: { "assumptions": [ ... ] }
- Each assumption is an object: { "text": str, "sources": [{ "start_line": int, "end_line": int }] }
- JSON numbers must not have leading zeros.
- sources must reference relevant focused_code line ranges that support the assumption. Use the smallest sufficient span(s). If an assumption is primarily derived from target_column_desc (e.g., completeness), still anchor sources to a nearby defining/usage line for the column in focused_code (e.g., where the column is referenced or assigned), and explicitly say in the text that it comes from metadata.

What to include for each target column:
1) Data type constraints (high priority for evaluation):
   - If target_column_desc.dataType is Integral or Fractional, include an explicit assumption like:
     - "Data type is Integral (integer counts)" or "Data type is Fractional (floating-point grades)."
   - Also support with code evidence:
     - pd.to_numeric(..., errors='coerce') applied to the column
     - .astype(int) or .astype(float) on the column
     - Arithmetic usage that implies numeric type (division, addition, normalization)
   - Cite the lines performing these conversions/usages.

2) Completeness constraints (high priority for evaluation):
   - If target_column_desc.completeness == 1.0, include an explicit assumption:
     - "Completeness is 100%; no missing values are expected."
   - If completeness < 1.0, state:
     - "Column may have missing values" and, if relevant, that code coerces invalid to NaN.
   - Anchor the sources to where the column is referenced in code (e.g., definition/use line) and explicitly note that the claim comes from metadata.

3) Value range constraints (include when clearly implied by code or domain usage in the snippet):
   - If the column is a grade normalized by 20.0 (e.g., df[col] / 20.0, 20.0 - df[col]):
     - Include both min and max assumptions explicitly:
       - "Minimum is at least 0.0" and "Maximum is at most 20.0."
     - Justify with the normalization lines (divide by 20.0 or 20.0 - grade).
   - If the column represents counts (e.g., '... (approved)', '... (enrolled)', '... (credited)', '... (without evaluations)') and is used in pass-rate or ratio computations:
     - Include "Values are non-negative integers (counts)."
     - If code uses replace(0, np.nan) on the denominator, note that zero can occur but counts are otherwise non-negative.
   - Only assert ranges that are unambiguously supported by code. For scales inferred from normalizations (e.g., division by a constant), phrase assumptions accordingly, and prefer explicit min/max when the scale is standard in the code (like grades divided by 20.0).

4) How the column is used in calculations:
   - Briefly state the role in risk/score features (e.g., "used to compute pass_rate2 = approved/enrolled," "used to compute grade gaps," "contributes to academic_risk or risk_score").
   - This helps ground the assumption and often provides indirect evidence of type and range.
   - Cite the exact lines where these computations occur.

5) Coercion and invalid value handling:
   - If pd.to_numeric(errors='coerce') or .astype(float/int) is applied, include an assumption:
     - "Non-numeric entries are coerced to NaN for safe numerical operations."
   - Cite the conversion lines.

Sourcing guidance:
- Always include at least one source range per assumption.
- Prefer the precise line where the column is:
  - Declared/aliased (e.g., c2_apr = 'Curricular units 2nd sem (approved)'),
  - Converted to numeric/typed,
  - Used in a formula indicating scale or semantics.
- Use multiple source ranges if needed to support an assumption (e.g., one for aliasing, one for normalization).

Domain-specific patterns in the provided code to leverage:
- Grade columns:
  - 'Curricular units 1st sem (grade)' and 'Curricular units 2nd sem (grade)' are fractional grades on a 0–20 scale (code divides by 20.0 or computes 20.0 - grade). Include explicit min >= 0.0 and max <= 20.0 assumptions, and that lower grades imply higher risk.
- Previous/Admission qualification grades:
  - Treated as numeric (Fractional) and used with diff_ap / 50.0 and (admission + previous)/400.0 to compute preparedness risk. State numeric type and completeness per metadata; only mention scale as "implied by normalization constants" unless 0–20 is explicitly shown in code.
- Count columns (approved/enrolled/credited/without evaluations):
  - Integral counts, non-negative; used to compute pass rates and no-evaluation ratios. Denominators may be zero and are replaced with NaN before division.

Quality and phrasing:
- Make each assumption atomic and testable (maps naturally to checks like .hasDataType(...), .isComplete(...), .hasMin(...), .hasMax(...)).
- Avoid speculative claims not supported by code or metadata.
- Keep the list concise (typically 4–8 assumptions), prioritizing datatype, completeness, and clear range constraints, then usage context.

Return only the JSON object as specified.
